This is a P-authored spec edit and needs a P gate before it is normative. Every `nucleus-store` card (Phase A) and the storage side of every `nucleus-txn` card that touches trees (B-1c apply, B-1d provisional entries, B-3 GC, B-4d bulk load, B-6 drop cleanup) implements against this file. A change to an invariant (`I-*`, §15) is made here first, then in the tests and mutants that pin it, and only then in code.

**Scope.** This file covers:
- the file, the pages, the page map, the free set and the buffer manager;
- the B+tree, including merge;
- the **versioned entry layout of N3 = (b)** and the read, scan and GC algorithms over it;
- the memory and eviction model;
- the WAL format, the checkpoint order and recovery.

It covers **what must hold**; the reference implementations show one way to meet it.
- **Reference implementations, both at `nucleus/v3-scaffold` 7ccd1cff:**
  - `spikes/e0-btree` (card X-0, the E0 tree: page, pool and tree);
  - `spikes/e1-arm-b` (card X-2, the arm (b) entry layout and read, scan and GC algorithms).
- **Where the spec departs from a reference**, it says so. A reference's spike limit is never a requirement.

**Precedence.** This file refines PLAN-v3 §3.3 (N1, N4, N8, N14) and records the storage consequences of the E1′ decision (PLAN-v3 §4, "E1′ decision: card X-4"). If the plan disagrees with this file, this file wins. §18 lists every deliberate refinement of §3.3, so the plan can be fixed through an Opus card. The transaction semantics (snapshots, the watermark W, locks, the write set, commit visibility, SSI) belong to **C-T0 v8 (card S-2)**. This file states only the preconditions it needs from them (§17).

**Error classes.** Errors use PostgreSQL SQLSTATEs:

| SQLSTATE | Meaning here |
|---|---|
| XX001 | data corrupted: an integrity check, of what the device returned, failed (§9.2) |
| XX000 | internal error: an engine invariant violation, a check that only an engine bug can fail (§9.2) |
| 58030 | I/O error |
| 53100 | disk full |
| 53200 | out of memory (N16) |
| 54000 | program limit exceeded |
| 55000 | object not in prerequisite state: a file that a later format version has written to, which every open refuses (§2.2); or an explicit rollback (§14 step 4a) asked for where its precondition does not hold |
| 22023 | invalid parameter value: file creation asked for a `wal_seg_size` below its floor (§2.3, §12.1) |

---

## 1. Vocabulary

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| slot | A page-sized region of the page file at offset `slot × page_size`. Slots 0–2 and the reserved slots of the replica region are fixed (§2, §2.4). Every other slot is either the **home** of exactly one page image or free. |
| LPID | Logical page id, `u64`, ≥ 1 (0 = null). B+tree pages refer to each other only by LPID, never by slot (N1). |
| page map | LPID → `(slot, epoch, crc)` (§4.1). |
| epoch `e` | The checkpoint epoch: a counter that starts at 1 when the file is created and is incremented by each checkpoint's flip (§13 step 0). Checkpoint `k` flips epoch `k` to `k+1`. **Image `k`** is the state of the page map and every page at the end of epoch `k`. |
| content epoch | The epoch in which a page's content was last mutated. It is stored in the page header and the map entry, and also kept per frame. |
| meta `k` | The meta record of image `k`, written twice: its primary copy in slot `1 + (k mod 2)` and its replica in the replica region (§2.2, §2.4). |
| durable image | The image of the meta that §2.2's open rule selects: the newest meta with a valid copy in its own place. |
| previous image | While meta `k` is the durable image, image `k−1`, when meta `k−1` is valid and its free set reads (§4.4, I-TWO-GEN). It and its WAL span are kept intact, as I-TWO-GEN states, so that an open can fall back to it (§14 step 4a). |
| `U` | Usable bytes of a B+tree page: `page_size` minus the page header (§3.1) and the B+tree header. `used(p)` = the bytes of p's live cells plus its slot array. |
| user key | The C-K1 memcomparable encoding (N7) of a row's primary key or of an index key, within one tree. Opaque bytes here. |
| entry | One B+tree cell `(entry key, entry value)`. Under N3 = (b), an entry is **one version** of one user key (§7), or a **provisional entry** (below). |
| provisional entry | A large transaction's own data for one key, at the head of the key's group: ts suffix `u64::MAX`, tags 6–8 (§7.3). It is not a version until C-T0 v8 §2.4 says so. A **dead** provisional entry is one from a previous boot. |
| group | The contiguous run of entries of one user key, ordered newest first (§7.2). |
| `ts` | A commit timestamp (C-T0 `Ts`, `u64`). `0` and `u64::MAX` are never commit timestamps (§7.1). |
| `S` | A snapshot ts. A read at `S` sees, per user key, the newest version with `ts ≤ S`. |
| `W` | The GC watermark (C-T0 v8 §9.1): `W ≤` every registered snapshot, `W ≤` the apply frontier `≤ visible_ts`, and monotonic (§17). |
| apply frontier | The largest ts such that every commit with ts ≤ it has its versions in the trees as **ordinary entries** (tags 0–5). It is C-T0 v8's materialized frontier `M` (C-T0 v8 §3.5). It equals `visible_ts` except while a large transaction finalizes: its provisional entries are already readable as versions, so it is visible, but it is not yet applied in this sense. |
| gated operation | A mutation that holds the **epoch gate** shared from its first page mutation to its last (§5.6). |
| SMO | Structure modification: a split, a pack step (§6.5), a root split or a root collapse. |

---

## 2. File

One page file plus a `wal/` directory of segments (N1). All I/O goes through `nucleus-env` (`Env`/`File`, PLAN-v3 §3.2). No storage code calls `std::fs` or `std::time`.

### 2.1 Page 0: file header
Written once at creation and synced before the first meta is written. Never rewritten.
```
magic          8   "NUCLEUS\0"
format_version u32
page_size      u32   4096 | 8192 | 16384 | 32768 | 65536 (default 8192)
checksum_alg   u8    1 = CRC32C
wal_seg_size   u32   bytes per WAL segment, at least 212 (§12.1); default 64 MiB
creation_uuid  16
crc            u32   CRC32C of the bytes above
```
The rest of the page is zero. Page 0 is read as the file's first 4096 bytes, whatever the page size. Every meta copy carries the same fields (§2.2), so an open does not need page 0: when page 0 is damaged or its read fails, the fields come from the meta copies (§2.2, "The file's identity"). An intact page 0 that is not well-formed is not damaged, and fails the open (§2.2).

### 2.2 Meta records: slots 1–2 and their replicas
Meta `k` is written twice (I-META-COPIES): its **primary copy** to slot `1 + (k mod 2)`, and its **replica** to its parity's block in the replica region (§2.4). A torn or lost meta write can therefore destroy only the older meta of its parity, or one copy of the new one. Both copies are this record, and differ only in their `copy` byte and their `crc`:
```
magic          8   "NUCMETA\0"
format_version u32
copy           u8    0 in the primary copy, 1 in the replica; under the crc, so the two copies of one meta never have equal bytes (§2.4)
page_size      u32   page 0's (§2.1)
checksum_alg   u8    page 0's
wal_seg_size   u32   page 0's
creation_uuid  16    page 0's
ckpt_id        u64   k
T              u64   apply frontier at the flip of checkpoint k (§13 step 0)
gc_w           u64   the largest W handed to any GC mutation in image k (§10.5)
epoch          u32   k (low 32 bits; compared for equality only)
map_root       ref   (slot u64, epoch u32, crc u32) of the page-map root (§4.1)
map_level      u8    the radix level of the page-map root, counted from the leaf level (0); at least 1 (§4.1)
free_root      ref   the free-set root (§4.3)
dir_root       u64   LPID of the tree directory's root (§6.8)
next_lpid      u64   one past the highest LPID ever allocated
slot_count     u64   the file length in slots covered by image k's free set
wal_start_lsn  u64   replay start (§12.6)
wal_start_prev u64   the start LSN of the record immediately before wal_start_lsn in the stream (§12.2 prev_lsn); all-ones if none
wal_start_prev_crc u32   the crc field of that record (§12.2 prev_crc); 0 if none
wal_ckpt_end   u64   the WAL append position read at the flip of checkpoint k (§13 step 0); step 1 made every byte below it durable
ts_ckpt_hi     u64   the commit_ts of the last Commit or CommitMarker appended before that flip, or the frontier the boot started from if none was; ≥ T
boot_id        u32   the boot id of the session that wrote meta k (C-T0 v8 §2.5, §14 step 8); a boot that opens meta k gets a newer id (§14 step 8); 0 in meta 0, written by the creating session (§2.3)
crc            u32   CRC32C of the bytes above
```
The rest of the slot, or of the replica block, is zero. Card A-2 pins the bytes and fuzzes the decoder, which never panics.

**Intact and well-formed** (card S-ZETA). A record, page 0's or a meta copy's, is **intact** when its magic and its CRC are valid, and **well-formed** when it is intact and its fields lie in their domains: `format_version` 1, the version these layouts describe; `page_size` one of §2.1's; `checksum_alg` 1; `wal_seg_size` at least 212 (§12.1); and, in a meta copy, `copy` 0 or 1, `map_level` at least 1, `epoch` the low 32 bits of `ckpt_id`, `T ≤ ts_ckpt_hi` and `wal_start_lsn ≤ wal_ckpt_end` (§14 step 1). Magic and CRC decide whether a record exists; the domains decide whether the open may use it, and an intact record of this file that it may not use fails the open (below). Up to a CRC collision, no fault within the file (a lost, torn or misdirected write, or a read error) leaves an intact record with the file's `creation_uuid` that is not well-formed or that differs from the file's identity in another static field: such a record was written whole, by another format version, a faulty writer or on purpose, so the open never takes it for a torn one. A write misdirected from another file can leave an intact record that is not well-formed, but that record carries the other file's `creation_uuid`: in a meta place it is foreign (below), and over page 0 it fails the open, as another file's well-formed page 0 does. Creation (§2.3) and §13 step 4 check that each record they write is well-formed before they write it; a record that is not is an engine invariant violation (XX000, §9.2), which fails creation and is fail-stop at step 4, so no correct build leaves one. The open's refusal of such a record checks what the device returned, and is not one: it fails with XX001, or with 55000 for a record of a later format version (below). Every later format version keeps each record's first bytes, the magic, `format_version` and, in a meta copy, `copy` and the static fields, at these offsets, and the record's CRC32C at the same offset over the bytes before it, and puts anything it adds after the record under a check of its own; any change to a layout that `format_version` pins (§2.1, §2.2, §3.1), or to a domain, is a later format version, with a higher `format_version`. So every build can tell whether a record of any version is intact, and refuses one it cannot use.

**A later format version** (card S-IOTA; the owner's decision of 2026-11-20, PLAN-v3 §10 item 22). A record is **of a later format version** when it is intact and its `format_version` is above 1. When page 0, or a copy that carries the file's `creation_uuid` (below), is of a later format version, the file is one that a later format version has written to, and every open of it fails with **55000** (detail: page 0 or the place, and its `format_version`): read-write and read-only alike, and an explicit rollback too, since it meets this refusal first (§14 step 1). No open falls back past such a record, serves salvage from the file or overrides the refusal, and the refusal writes nothing. This build cannot judge such a file: a later version may give any field another domain and the bytes after a record's first bytes another meaning, and its checkpoints may be newer than every record this build can read, so opening the newest record that this build can use could roll the database back past them without a word; the remedy is the later version's build. An intact page 0 is judged first, whatever the meta places hold (below); after it, a copy of the file of a later format version fails the open with 55000 whatever XX001 another copy would give. Only a `format_version` above 1 is a later version's. An intact record of the file whose `format_version` is 1 but that is otherwise not well-formed, or whose `format_version` is 0, is no later version's: it was written by a faulty writer or on purpose, and fails the open with XX001, in both modes, as above. A write misdirected over page 0 from another file of a later format version fails the open with 55000 too, since an intact page 0 is the file's identity; that version's build then finds page 0's `creation_uuid` in no copy.

The creating session's boot id is 0, meta 0's `ckpt_id`. Meta 0 is built through the checkpoint path and synced before epoch 1 (§2.3), and every boot checkpoint has `ckpt_id ≥ 1` and a boot id no older than it (§14 step 8), so no later boot has id 0.

`wal_start_prev` and `wal_start_prev_crc` are the comparands for the first record a walk from meta `k` reads (§12.5, I-WAL-CHAIN). Step 0 records them with `wal_start_lsn`, and step 4 writes them (§13). The commit path keeps the pair for every position §12.6 can choose: for a record there, it is the `prev_lsn` and `prev_crc` written into that record; for the append position, it is the chain head (§12.2). That record ends at or below `wal_start_lsn ≤ wal_ckpt_end`, so step 1 has written it, and its `crc` is final, before step 4 writes the meta. They are compared, never read through: the record they name may lie in a recycled segment. Meta 0's are all-ones and 0.

**At open (the open rule).** The open reads page 0 and the four meta places: slots 1 and 2, and the two replica blocks (§2.4). A place whose read fails holds an invalid copy; if every place's read fails and page 0 is intact and well-formed, the open fails with 58030, and so it does when page 0's read and both replica blocks' reads fail, which leaves slots 1 and 2 unlocated (below): the open has seen no damage, only read errors, which a later open may not meet. A read-write open zeroes every place whose read failed before its first write (§14 step 1, the fence), and an open that this rule refuses writes nothing. Card A-2 builds the rule as a function of those five blocks' bytes, so that every combination is tested with no I/O (`t_meta_copies`).
- **The file's identity** is page 0's fields (§2.1) when page 0 is intact and well-formed; when page 0 is intact and of a later format version, the open fails with 55000, and when it is otherwise intact but not well-formed, with XX001, whatever the meta places hold (detail: page 0, the field and its value). Otherwise the open reads the two replica blocks first: if their reads and page 0's have all failed, the open fails with 58030 (above); if neither holds an intact copy, the open fails with XX001; if the intact replica copies carry one `creation_uuid` and one of them is of a later format version, the open fails with 55000, and reads neither slot 1 nor slot 2; if the page sizes of the intact replica copies differ or are not one of §2.1's, the open fails with XX001; else the intact replica copies give the page size, and so the offsets of slots 1 and 2. The identity is then the fields that every intact copy, in any of the four places, shares; if two of them differ in `creation_uuid`, the open fails with XX001; else that `creation_uuid` is the file's, and if one of them is of a later format version, the open fails with 55000; else, if two of them differ in `format_version`, `page_size`, `checksum_alg` or `wal_seg_size`, or the fields they share are not well-formed, the open fails with XX001. So with page 0 invalid a copy of another file's identity cannot be told apart and fails the open; the rest of this rule, and the table below, apply once the identity is known, and a copy is foreign only against it.
- **A valid copy** is well-formed, has the file's identity, and sits in a place of **its own parity**: slot `1 + (ckpt_id mod 2)` or that parity's replica block. Its `copy` byte names the place it was written for, but serves only to make the two copies' bytes differ (§2.4): a copy found in its parity's other place, a misdirect within the parity, is still valid. A well-formed copy with the file's identity in a place of the other parity is **misplaced**, a misdirected write, and is never opened. An intact copy whose `creation_uuid` is not the file's is **foreign**, and is ignored, whatever its other fields hold. An intact copy with the file's `creation_uuid` that is of a later format version fails the open with 55000, and one that is otherwise not well-formed, or that differs from the file's identity in another static field, fails it with XX001, in any place (detail: the place, the field and its value): no fault within the file makes one (above), its `ckpt_id` cannot be relied on, and passing over it could select an older meta past a later checkpoint, of this format version or another (I-META-SLOT).
- **Each parity's candidate** is its valid copy with the higher `ckpt_id`. When both of its places hold valid copies with one `ckpt_id` that differ in a byte other than `copy` and `crc`, a rolled-back `ckpt_id` was written beside a stale copy (§14 step 8): the copy with the newer `boot_id`, in serial-number order (§12.5), is the candidate, and equal `boot_id`s fail the open with XX001. This is **the tie rule**.
- **The newest meta** `k` is the candidate with the higher `ckpt_id`. If there is none, the open fails with XX001. If a misplaced copy's `ckpt_id` exceeds `k + 1`, the open fails with XX001 (I-META-SLOT): checkpoint `k + 2` or a later one then reached its step 4, after checkpoint `k + 1` had completed, so step 6 of checkpoint `k + 2` may have freed image `k`'s superseded slots, and the open does not select meta `k` past it. A misplaced copy whose `ckpt_id` is `k + 1` shows only that checkpoint `k + 1` reached its step 4: image `k` and the WAL from meta `k`'s `wal_start_lsn` stay intact until step 6 of checkpoint `k + 2` (I-TWO-GEN), so the open selects meta `k`, as it does when both copies of meta `k + 1` are lost (§14, "Post-sync damage"). A misplaced copy is never opened.
- **The previous meta** is the other parity's candidate when its `ckpt_id` is `k−1`. Its image is the previous image when its free set reads (§4.4, I-TWO-GEN), and it is the only meta an open falls back to (§14 step 4a).

For one parity's two copies the rule is this table; the newest and previous metas follow from the two parities' candidates as above.

| Primary place | Replica place | The parity's candidate |
|---|---|---|
| valid, `ckpt_id` `m` | valid `m`, equal but for `copy` and `crc` | `m` |
| valid `m` | valid `m`, another byte differs | the copy with the newer `boot_id`; equal `boot_id`s: XX001 |
| valid `m` | valid `m′`, `m′ < m` (a lost write left it stale) | `m` |
| valid `m′`, `m′ < m` | valid `m` | `m` |
| valid `m` | invalid (torn, zeros, unreadable), misplaced or foreign | `m` |
| invalid, misplaced or foreign | valid `m` | `m` |
| invalid, misplaced or foreign | invalid, misplaced or foreign | none |

Nothing depends on which copy is read first. A single fault on one copy of a meta whose two copies were both written (lost, torn, misdirected or unreadable) therefore never changes which meta opens or what it recovers (I-META-COPIES). A crash between step 4's two writes can leave one copy of the new meta; a fault that leaves no valid copy of it makes the open select the meta before it, whose image and WAL recover the same state (I-TWO-GEN), and so does a misdirect of that copy into a place of the other parity, which leaves a misplaced copy one `ckpt_id` above the meta before it (I-META-SLOT). No open repairs a damaged copy, and only a read-write open's fence zeroes one, a copy whose read failed (§14 step 1): the next checkpoint of that parity rewrites both.

**One fall-back, and only one.** An open never selects an older meta than the newest, except as §14 step 4a allows, one generation, to the previous meta: when the newest image fails at open, with proof that the log is complete; and, when it fails at open and the proof fails, or when a read error (58030) stops it, by explicit rollback or in a read-only open's salvage. After meta `k` is synced, image `k−2` and the WAL below meta `k−1`'s `wal_start_lsn` may be reused (§13 step 6, I-TWO-GEN). So if both copies of the two newest metas are lost, the open selects a meta older than the two generations: a recycled start segment fails I-WAL-START, and a reused slot of its image fails I-INTEGRITY whenever it is read (§3.3), because it holds a later write whose `crc`, `id` or `epoch` differs from the reference. If that failure comes at open, §14 step 4a may fall back once from the selected meta, so three generations from the lost newest one; its proof then holds against the selected meta only, and a reused slot of the image it opens fails I-INTEGRITY when read too, at open (XX001) or at a later read (§9.2). Such an open is never silent, but it can succeed and then fail reads.

**Test `t_meta_copies`** (A-2, A-7c, A-7d; §15.1).
- *Table form (A-2).* The rule runs over every combination, for the newest meta and the previous one, of each place holding a valid copy of its parity's newest meta, the same copy written for its parity's other place, a valid stale copy, a torn copy, zeros, an unreadable place, a misplaced copy (one `ckpt_id` above the newest valid meta, and two above it), a foreign copy with a higher `ckpt_id`, another with `format_version` 2, or an intact copy with the file's `creation_uuid` that has, at a higher `ckpt_id`, `format_version` 2 or `T` above its `ts_ckpt_hi`, or, at a stale `ckpt_id` two below its parity's newest, `map_level` 0 or another `page_size` (four symbols), with page 0 valid, torn, unreadable, intact with `format_version` 2 and intact with `format_version` 0, and over both tie cases: the newer `boot_id` in either place, and equal `boot_id`s. Each result is the rule's: the meta selected, the previous meta, 55000, XX001 or 58030. Among them, page 0's read and both replica blocks' failing gives 58030, and page 0 torn with both replica blocks' reads failing gives XX001; a copy that the rule refuses gives XX001 wherever the open reads it and whatever its `ckpt_id`, and 55000 when it, or another copy of the file, is of a later format version; page 0 intact with `format_version` 2 gives 55000, and with `format_version` 0 XX001, whatever the places hold; and a file whose page 0 and every intact copy carry `format_version` 2 gives 55000.
- *Write form (A-7c).* Both places are read back after every checkpoint, and after a SimEnv crash between step 4's two writes and after both: each holds meta `k` or its parity's previous meta; after every checkpoint that completes, both hold meta `k`, copy 0 in slot `1 + (k mod 2)` and copy 1 in the replica block, and the two differ in their `copy` byte and `crc` and in no other byte. A hook that hands step 4 a meta whose `map_level` is 0, a value no caller produces, makes that checkpoint fail-stop with XX000 (§9.2) with neither place written, so that both still hold the parity's previous meta; one that hands creation an identity whose `page_size` is 12288 makes creation fail with XX000 before it writes page 0.
- *Fault form (A-7d).* One post-sync fault (LOST, TORN, MISDIRECTED to another meta place of either parity, after or before that place's own write, or to a page slot), or a read error made by a hook, on one copy of the newest synced meta, at every step of a checkpoint and of the next one; separately, a fault on page 0, on both replica blocks at once, and a foreign copy with a higher `ckpt_id` written into a replica block, in a second run one with `format_version` 2. Every open selects the meta the faultless run selects and recovers the same state, and every image passes amcheck. In its bulk-load variant the fault is on one copy of the forced checkpoint's meta (§12.8, S3 for the load), and the open still succeeds. In its tie variant, a read error on both copies of meta `k` at one open makes that open select meta `k−1`, the fence's two zeroing writes are lost after their sync (post-sync LOST, §14 step 1), and its boot checkpoint rewrites `ckpt_id` `k` with the primary copy's write lost (a second run loses the replica's), meta `k` having been written after acked commits of its own boot, so that its `boot_id` is the older; the stale meta `k` then sits beside the new one: the next open selects the new one. *Fence variants* (§14 step 1): (i) a boot that appended no records writes meta `k` as its boot checkpoint; at the next open a hook fails the reads of both copies of meta `k`, so that open selects meta `k−1`, its walk accepts the same records and its boot id equals meta `k`'s `boot_id`, and its boot checkpoint rewrites `ckpt_id` `k` with the primary copy's write lost (a second run loses the replica's); the fixture makes the two metas differ in a byte other than `copy` and `crc` (the second open runs with a smaller pool, so that its pass 2 evicts and its image's map root lands in another slot). The next open finds zeros beside the new meta `k`, selects it and recovers every acked commit. (ii) A crash between step 4's two writes of checkpoint `k+1` leaves one copy of meta `k+1`; at the next open a hook fails that place's read, so the open selects meta `k`, and with a pool at its minimum and acked commits after meta `k` that fill it, pass 2 evicts, and a crash follows its first eviction. The read succeeds at the open after: that place holds zeros, the open selects meta `k` and recovers every acked commit, and every image passes amcheck. *In-flight variant* (I-META-SLOT): step 4 of checkpoint `k+1` misdirects its first copy into one of meta `k`'s two places, and a crash follows before the second copy's write; the open selects meta `k` from its other copy and recovers every acked commit. *Later-format variant* (card S-ZETA): a hook takes meta `k`'s copy, sets its `ckpt_id` and `epoch` to `k+1` and its `format_version` to 2, re-seals it, and writes it through `Env` into both places of meta `k+1`'s parity, as a later format version's checkpoint would; a second run re-seals only page 0 with `format_version` 2, and a third re-seals page 0 and the copy in every meta place so. Every open, read-write and read-only, and an explicit rollback naming meta `k`, fails with 55000, its detail naming page 0 or a place, and `format_version`, and leaves every file as the hook left it. *Unlocated variant* (card S-ZETA): a hook fails every read of page 0 and of both replica blocks at one read-write open, and again at one read-only open; each fails with 58030 and leaves every file byte-identical, and the next open, whose reads succeed, selects the meta the faultless run selects.

Mutants:
- **REPLICA-SKIP**: step 4 writes the primary copy only. The write form finds the replica place still holding the parity's previous meta. The fault form opens meta `k−1` after a fault on meta `k`'s primary copy, and in its bulk-load variant step 3b then refuses the open (§14).
- **COPY-INDEX-OFF**: both copies carry `copy` 0 and are byte-identical. The write form finds the two places' bytes equal.
- **TIE-NO-BOOT-ID**: a tie selects the primary copy, whatever the `boot_id`s. The table form selects the stale copy when the newer `boot_id` is in the replica, and the tie variant reopens the old meta `k`.
- **TIE-EQUAL-OPENS**: equal `boot_id`s with another byte differing select a copy. The table form opens where the rule gives XX001.
- **MISPLACED-SHADOWS**: a misplaced copy fails the open although a valid copy has an equal or higher `ckpt_id`. The table form, and the fault form's misdirect onto another meta place, give XX001 where meta `k` opens.
- **IDENTITY-SKIP**: `creation_uuid` and the static fields are not compared. The table form and the fault form's foreign copy select the foreign copy.
- **PAGE0-REQUIRED**: an invalid page 0 fails the open. The table form and the fault form's page-0 fault give XX001 where a copy gives the fields.
- META-WRONG-SLOT (I-META-SLOT) skips the place test: the table form opens a misplaced copy newer than every valid one.
- **MISPLACED-NEXT-REFUSES** (I-META-SLOT): a misplaced copy one `ckpt_id` above the newest valid meta fails the open, as at draft 7.12. The table form gives XX001 where meta `k` opens, and the fault form's in-flight variant refuses an intact store.
- **MISPLACED-IGNORED** (I-META-SLOT): a misplaced copy two or more `ckpt_id`s above the newest valid meta fails no open. The table form opens meta `k` where the rule gives XX001.
- **FENCE-SKIP**: a read-write open writes before it zeroes the meta places whose read failed, or never zeroes them (§14 step 1). The fence variants fail: (i) its form that never zeroes, since the stale meta `k` sits beside the new one with an equal `boot_id` and the next open gives XX001, while a fence made after the first eviction but before the boot checkpoint passes (i); (ii) both forms, since the open after the crash selects meta `k+1`.
- **MALFORMED-AS-TORN** (card S-ZETA; verdict A-2-0): an intact record that is not well-formed is read as invalid, as card A-2-0 first built the rule. The table form opens meta `k` past an intact copy with the file's `creation_uuid` and `map_level` 0, `T` above its `ts_ckpt_hi` or `format_version` 2, and past a page 0 with `format_version` 2, where the rule gives XX001, or 55000 for `format_version` 2; the fault form's later-format variant opens meta `k` in its first and second runs, and a read-write open then writes over the later version's copies.
- **IDENTITY-NO-FV-ALG** (card S-ZETA; verdict A-2-0, its gate 2): a copy's `format_version` and `checksum_alg` are neither compared with the file's identity nor checked against their domains, so an intact copy with the file's `creation_uuid` and another format version passes for the file's own. The table form opens it where the rule gives 55000.
- **STATICS-AS-FOREIGN** (card S-ZETA): an intact copy with the file's `creation_uuid` and another static field is ignored as foreign, as at draft 7.15. The table form opens meta `k` past a copy with another `page_size`, where the rule gives XX001.
- **IDENTITY-DOMAIN-SKIP** (card S-ZETA): no static field, of page 0 or of a copy, is checked against its domain. The table form, and the fault form's later-format variant in its third run, open a file whose page 0 and every copy carry `format_version` 2.
- **FOREIGN-MALFORMED-REFUSES** (card S-ZETA): a foreign copy that is not well-formed fails the open. The table form, and the fault form's foreign copy in its second run, refuse the open, with XX001 or 55000, where the faultless meta opens.
- **UNLOCATED-AS-CORRUPT** (card S-ZETA): page 0's read and both replica blocks' reads failing give XX001, as at draft 7.15. The table form and the fault form's unlocated variant give XX001 where the rule gives 58030.
- **FENCE-BEFORE-REFUSAL** (card S-ZETA): a read-write open zeroes the places whose read failed before the open rule decides, so an open that the rule refuses has written. The fault form's unlocated variant finds both replica blocks zeroed.
- **WRITE-MALFORMED** (card S-ZETA): creation or step 4 writes a record without checking that it is well-formed. The write form finds the forged meta in both places, or the forged page 0 written.
- **WRITE-CHECK-AS-CORRUPT** (card S-IOTA): creation's or step 4's check of a record it is about to write fails with XX001, the code of the open's refusal of the same domains. The write form's forged meta and forged page 0 fail with XX001 where the rule gives XX000.
- **REFUSAL-AS-INVARIANT** (card S-IOTA): the open's refusal of an intact record of the file that is not well-formed fails with XX000, the code of step 4's check of the same domains. The table form gives XX000 where the rule gives XX001.
- **LATER-FORMAT-AS-CORRUPT** (card S-IOTA): a record of a later format version fails the open with XX001, as at draft 7.19. The table form, and the fault form's later-format variant in each of its runs, give XX001 where the rule gives 55000.
- **REFUSAL-AS-LATER** (card S-IOTA): every record that the open refuses fails it with 55000, one of `format_version` 1 that a faulty writer or a deliberate write made included. The table form gives 55000 for a copy with the file's `creation_uuid` and `map_level` 0, `T` above its `ts_ckpt_hi` or another `page_size`, where the rule gives XX001.
- **LATER-BY-INEQUALITY** (card S-IOTA): the open takes every `format_version` other than 1 for a later one. The table form gives 55000 for page 0 intact with `format_version` 0, where the rule gives XX001.
- **LATER-FORMAT-SALVAGED** (card S-IOTA): a read-only open of a file that a later format version has written to serves what this build can read, the newest meta it can use, instead of failing with 55000. The fault form's later-format variant opens read-only in its first and second runs, where the rule gives 55000.

### 2.3 File creation
Creation first checks the `wal_seg_size` it is to write against its floor: 8 MiB, so that every row-layer op fits one record (a record is at most `wal_seg_size / 4`, §12.2; C-T6-rehost §4.3, I-OP-BOUND). A test of the store may lower the floor to §12.1's 212, since no row-layer op runs there. A value below the floor fails creation with 22023 before anything is written (mutant SEG-SIZE-UNCHECKED, §12.1). Creation then runs as one sequence:
1. write page 0, set the file's length to at least the end of the replica region (§2.4), and `sync_all`;
2. create WAL segment 0 (`wal/0000000000000000.log`), preallocated, with its creation and length durable as §12.5's writer rule requires (I-WAL-START);
3. build image 0 through the normal checkpoint path (§13): an empty tree directory and a page map whose root is one empty MapInner at level 1 (§4.1);
4. write meta 0's two copies, to slot 1 and to parity 0's replica block (§2.2, §2.4), and `sync_all`.

Epoch 1 then begins. Segment 0 exists before any meta does, preallocated and durable, so the start segment of every meta exists, with its preallocated length durable, when the meta is written (§13 step 1).

### 2.4 The replica region
The meta replicas lie at fixed byte offsets, so that an open finds them without page 0 (§2.2):
- parity 0's replica block, the replica of every even `ckpt_id`: the 4096 bytes at offset 1,048,576 (1 MiB);
- parity 1's replica block: the 4096 bytes at offset 1,052,672.

The distance from slots 0–2 is logical. It guards against a scribble or a partial copy that damages the start of the file. It promises no physical separation: a flash translation layer may place both copies of one meta write close together, or keep one physical copy (§19 T4). The `copy` byte keeps the two copies' bytes different, so a deduplicating device cannot collapse them into one.

**Reserved slots.** The slots that overlap the region, from `⌊1 MiB / page_size⌋` up to but not including `⌈(1 MiB + 8 KiB) / page_size⌉`, are reserved: never a page home (§4.4), and referenced by every image, so never free in a slot bitmap (§4.3); amcheck checks both. At 4 KiB pages they are slots 256 and 257; at every larger page size, the one slot `1 MiB / page_size`. The file is created at least as long as the region (§2.3), so the reserved slots always lie in `[3, slot_count)`.

**Test `t_replica_region`** (A-3c, A-7b; §15.1): at 4 KiB pages, a store grows past the region, with allocations and evictions through it, and at A-7b a checkpoint after each growth. A hook on every page write asserts that none lands in a reserved slot, and the persisted slot bitmaps mark the reserved slots used. Mutant **REPLICA-SLOT-FREE**: the reserved slots are free in the free set, in memory or persisted; an allocation takes slot 256, and the hook fires.

---

## 3. Pages

### 3.1 Page header (every page in slots ≥ 3)
```
crc     u32   CRC32C of bytes 4..page_size
kind    u8    3 Leaf | 4 Inner | 5 Overflow | 6 MapLeaf | 7 MapInner | 8 FreeSetLeaf | 9 FreeSetInner
flags   u8    0 (reserved)
_       u16   0
id      u64   LPID for kinds 3–5; for kinds 6–9 the radix position: the page's level, counted from the leaf level (0), and its index at that level, in an encoding A-2 pins that gives no two positions one id
epoch   u32   content epoch (low 32 bits)
_       u32   0
```
That is 24 bytes; the kind-specific body follows. A page's writer, a dirty eviction (§5.2) or step 2 (§13), sets `epoch` to the content epoch of the bytes it writes before it seals the page; a map page that step 2's map phase builds for image `k` has content epoch `k` (card S-MU). The format version (§2.1) pins the byte layout of every body. `spikes/e0-btree/src/page.rs` is the reference for the slotted body:
- the slot array grows up and the cells grow down;
- leaf cells are `klen, vlen, key, value`; inner cells are `klen, child LPID, key`;
- every in-page offset field must address the whole page at every allowed page size.

Card A-2 pins the bytes and fuzzes the decoders. A decoder never panics: every malformed byte sequence is XX001. At open, a page read through the newest image's references that passes §3.3 but that its decoder refuses makes that image fail at open (§14 step 4a; card S-LAMBDA).

### 3.2 Size limits
- `K_MAX(page_size)` bounds an encoded **entry key** (§7.2).
- `V_INLINE(page_size)` bounds an inline entry value.

Both are derived so that every leaf and inner page holds at least **4** cells of maximum size. E0 relied on 2 (`spikes/e0-btree/README.md`, "Known limits"). The doubling gives the pack step (§6.5) room to move a cell without splitting a parent. A-2 pins the formula and the values for each allowed page size.
- **Over-size keys (I-KEY-SIZE).** A write whose encoded entry key exceeds `K_MAX` is refused with 54000 **when it enters the write set** (S-2's write path calls the store's size check). Apply never sees it (§9). Mutant: SIZE-CHECK-OFF. Without the check, the over-size key reaches apply, fails there and poisons the store (§9.2), instead of returning 54000 to the writer with the store still usable.
- **Large values.** A value above `V_INLINE` is stored in an overflow chain (§7.4), with C-T6 §5 TOAST semantics (card A-5; the row-level rules belong to S-3).

### 3.3 Integrity on every read (I-INTEGRITY)
Every page is read through a **reference**: a page-map entry for an LPID, or a parent reference for a map or free-set page. The reference supplies `(slot, epoch, crc)`, and the expected id is known. The read checks all four of:
- the header `crc` equals the CRC32C of the bytes;
- the header `crc` equals the reference's `crc`;
- the header `id` equals the expected id;
- the header `epoch` equals the reference's `epoch`.

Any mismatch returns XX001, and the bytes are never served. The check catches torn, lost (stale) and misdirected writes, not just torn ones (PLAN-v3 §3.3).

---

## 4. Page map, LPIDs and slots

### 4.1 Page map (N1)
- **Shape.** The map is a radix tree of **physically addressed** pages. MapLeaf pages hold `⌊(page_size − 24) / 16⌋` entries (510 at 8 KiB) of `slot u64, epoch u32, crc u32`. `slot = 0` means **unmapped**: the LPID is free. In the current map a page that has been allocated and not yet written also has slot 0, and a frame holds it until a write gives it a home (§5.4 "What a pin finds"). MapInner pages hold refs of the same shape to their children, as many as a MapLeaf holds entries: that count is the map's **fanout** `f`. In the current map a MapLeaf's ref names its home, so it is null from the MapLeaf's making (§5.6 "Restart") until a write of its frame gives it one (below), and meanwhile a frame holds the MapLeaf (§5.4); a MapInner lives outside the pool, and its parent's ref names it from its making ("Growth", below). Every map page also has an **image ref**, the ref with which the newest image names it, kept in memory beside the current map (card S-MU): a MapLeaf's in its MapInner beside its current ref, 16 bytes a MapLeaf (R-1-memory §5.2), and a MapInner's with the MapInners. Only step 2's map phase sets an image ref, for a page once it has written it for image `k` (§13 step 2), and the open sets every one from the image it opens (§14 step 2); a page made since the newest image has none, and no pin or load reads one (§5.4). A MapLeaf's current ref and its image ref differ once the map phase has written the MapLeaf, since that write never becomes its current home (§13 step 2): its current home may then hold older entries, so an image names a MapLeaf that step 2 does not write by its image ref, never by its current ref. A map page's position is its level, counted from the leaf level (MapLeafs are level 0), and its index at that level: the MapLeaf at index `i` maps LPIDs `[i·f, (i+1)·f)`, and the MapInner at level `L` and index `j` covers MapLeaf indexes `[j·f^L, (j+1)·f^L)`. The root is always a MapInner, at level 1 or above, so it is always resident (below): the meta's `map_root` is its ref and `map_level` its level (§2.2). A test build may set a smaller fanout (`f ≥ 2`) for the whole store, so that its tests reach MapInner and level boundaries with few LPIDs; nothing else changes, and only a build with the same fanout opens such a file.
- **Residency.** MapInner pages are **always resident**. They number about 1/510 of the MapLeaf pages, about 4 MiB per TiB of data at 8 KiB pages, and the image refs that the level-1 MapInners keep beside their current refs (above) take as much again. MapLeaf pages are buffer-managed and evictable, under one rule: **a MapLeaf stays resident while any page it maps is dirty in the pool** (it holds one pin per such page; §5.4). A page's first mutation since it was last written takes that pin from a resident MapLeaf and reads nothing, since the operation pinned the MapLeaf before its first mutation (§5.6 "Restart"). An epoch copy (§5.6) is not such a page and holds no pin: a claim records the copy's write in the image-record list, not in the MapLeaf, and the map phase reads its sources into buffers of its own (§13 step 2), so no step needs the copy's MapLeaf resident (card S-MU; R-1-memory §14's Q-MAPLEAF-DIRTY and Q-COPY-PIN).
- **Writes are copy-on-write.** Map pages are mutated through `write_guard` like every other page (§5.6). A dirty map page is written to a fresh slot (I-NO-INPLACE), and its parent's ref is then set in memory in two steps, so no thread latches a map page's parent while it holds that page's latch (§5.3). Under the page's latch the writer writes the bytes, keeping the frame pinned and in the page table; it then releases the page's latch, latches the parent exclusively and sets the ref. An eviction drops the frame only after that, and only if no one has dirtied or pinned it meanwhile: the pin keeps a second eviction off the frame, and the page-table entry keeps a lookup from reloading the old slot. A dirty eviction holds the gate across both steps (§5.2), so no flip lands between them. The writer holds no other map latch when it latches the parent, because no frame is obtained by eviction under a map latch: `write_guard`'s copies come from the reserve, and a MapLeaf is pinned or created before any map latch is taken (§5.6). Step 2 builds the image map's parents in buffers of its own (§13 step 2), so only current refs are set this way.
- **Growth (I-MAP-GROW).** The map grows only when an allocation needs a MapLeaf that does not exist yet (§5.6 "Restart"), in that restart's own gated operation, before it makes the MapLeaf:
  - **A new level.** When the MapLeaf's index lies beyond the root's cover, the thread latches the current root `R` exclusively. If the map's root is still `R` and still does not cover the index, it makes a new root `R′` at `R`'s level plus one, whose first ref names `R`, makes `R′` the map's root, and releases `R`'s latch. Otherwise another thread grew the map first: it releases the latch and continues from the new root. It repeats until the root covers the index.
  - **A MapInner.** Then, from the root down, wherever the path to the MapLeaf lacks a MapInner, it latches the parent exclusively and makes the MapInner only if the parent's ref is still null; otherwise it takes the MapInner the ref names. It releases each parent's latch before it latches anything below it, so it holds one map latch at a time, top-down (§5.3).

  MapInner pages live outside the pool (§5.7), so making one obtains no frame, evicts nothing and reads nothing; the MapLeaf's frame is obtained first, holding no latch (§5.6). A new page has content epoch `e` and no epoch-`k` content, so it needs no epoch copy. The parent a new MapInner changes is changed through `write_guard`, which copies no MapInner (§5.6 "When it copies"), so a thread making a MapInner under its parent's latch waits on nothing. A new MapInner has no image ref (above); checkpoint `k+1` builds a level added in epoch `k+1` from its children's image refs, the old root's among them, which is the old root's ref in image `k` once checkpoint `k` has written the old root, whether before or after the level was made (§13 step 2; card S-MU). A new level changes no existing page. Every position is counted from the leaf level, so a new level changes no page's id (§3.1) and no image-record key (§13 step 2). The growth lies wholly before or after a flip, as every gated operation does: image `k` is built up to the root and level that step 0 recorded (§13 steps 0 and 2), and a page made in epoch `k+1` is first written by checkpoint `k+1`. A crash loses only pages made since the durable meta, and they map no LPID of any durable image.
  - Mutants: **MAPINNER-CREATE-UNCONDITIONAL**: a thread makes the MapInner although the parent's ref is set, and replaces the ref. **LEVEL-GROW-UNCONDITIONAL**: a thread adds a level over `R` although the map's root is no longer `R`, and makes its new root the map's root. **MAP-ROOT-CURRENT**: step 2's map phase builds image `k` up to the current root, not the root step 0 recorded. **MAP-POSITION-FROM-ROOT**: positions are counted from the root, so a new level renumbers every page.
  - Tests. `t_alloc_restart_progress` gains two variants (A-4a, with a test fanout and S2 for the image check; A-7d in full; §15.1). *MapInner race:* two allocations restart on the same missing MapInner, with a pool at its minimum; the first makes the MapInner and its MapLeaf, and its retry maps an LPID there, before the second latches the parent; the second finds the ref set and uses that MapInner. Every image passes amcheck, both LPIDs stay mapped, and a hook asserts that no page is evicted or read under a map latch. MAPINNER-CREATE-UNCONDITIONAL fails it through amcheck: the first LPID's mapping is lost (I-LPID-EXACT). *Level race:* the same, with the MapLeaf beyond the root's cover, so that both restarts need a new level; the second latches `R` after the first has released it, finds the root changed, and continues from it. LEVEL-GROW-UNCONDITIONAL fails it through amcheck: the first thread's MapInner, with its mapped LPID, is no longer reachable from the root. `t_step2_waits_for_claimed_copy`'s namespace variant adds a level in epoch `k+1`, and maps an LPID under the new root's second child, before the map phase runs (A-3c on S1–S3, the growth called as A-4a's restart will call it; A-7d in full; §15.1): meta `k` names the root and level step 0 recorded, and image `k` maps no LPID of epoch `k+1`. MAP-ROOT-CURRENT fails it: image `k`'s root is the new one, holding the epoch-`k+1` MapInner, which S2's read-back and amcheck find. MAP-POSITION-FROM-ROOT fails it: after the new level the list's records name other pages, and image `k`'s map is wrong (S2, amcheck), or a reload fails its `id` check with XX001.

### 4.2 LPID allocation
- **Allocation.** Take the lowest free LPID; if none is free, take `next_lpid++`.
- **The free-set lock.** It guards the LPID bitmap's in-memory state and `next_lpid` only, despite its name: slot allocation (§4.4, §4.5) does not take it, and neither does eviction. It ranks after the gate and any frame latches: a thread may take it while holding those, and may take the page table inside it, never the reverse, so a thread holding the page table never takes the free-set lock. Under it a thread only tests and sets LPID bits, advances `next_lpid`, and tests and pins resident MapLeaf frames (§5.6): no I/O, eviction or frame latch runs under it, it creates no page, and it waits on nothing but the page table. So a dirty eviction, which allocates a slot and updates the page table while holding frame latches (§5.2), never meets it, and an allocation that finds a MapLeaf cold or missing releases it before doing anything about that (§5.6).
- **Freeing.** Freeing an LPID is a mutation of the page and of its map entry, both through `write_guard`, so image `k` keeps the page's epoch-`k` content if the free happens in epoch `k+1`. The free sets the entry to slot 0 and leaves the page's frame **freed** and not dirty (card S-THETA): no eviction (§5.2) and no step 2 (§13 step 2) ever writes it, since that write would map the freed LPID again, and the pin its MapLeaf holds for it while it is dirty ends (§4.1). Nothing mutates a freed page. Its frame stays in the page table until an eviction drops it as a clean victim, with no write, or until an allocation takes the LPID again and makes its new page in that frame (§5.4): reusing the frame, rather than putting a second one in the page table, keeps every pin on it valid, a stale name's included (§6.8 "Root names": "in that frame or another"). Mutant **FREED-FRAME-WRITTEN**: a freed page's frame is written, which sets the LPID's entry again and maps an LPID that no reference names. Its eviction half leaves the frame dirty, so that an eviction writes it (I-LPID-EXACT's row test at A-4b, §15.1); its step-2 half has step 2 write it though the free left it clean, as a step 2 that selects every frame whose epoch-`k` content no image slot holds, dirty or not, would (the same row test at A-7f, through a checkpoint whose flip follows the free while the freed page's frame is still resident; card S-KAPPA).
- **Reuse is immediate, in memory.** A freed LPID may be reused at once because LPIDs never appear in the WAL (§12) and readers reach a page that can be freed only through latched parents (§6.2); for a tree's root, through the root table with the name validated under the root's latch (§6.8 "Root names"); or, for an overflow chain, from its entry under the leaf's latch or, after that latch is released, under a registered snapshot `S ≥ W`, whose registration keeps the version, and so its chain, from being freed (§6.7 "Reading without the leaf latch", I-GC-READEQ). The image keeps its own epoch-`k` map version.

### 4.3 Free set
The free set is **two bitmaps**, persisted with each image as a radix of physically addressed FreeSet pages (the same shape as the map), with the root ref in the meta (`free_root`):
- **slot bitmap:** one bit per slot in `[3, slot_count)`; set = free in image `k`;
- **LPID bitmap:** one bit per LPID in `[1, next_lpid)`; set = unmapped in image `k`.

**Why bitmaps.** Their size depends only on the file length and `next_lpid`, never on fragmentation. That keeps the store's metadata memory a fixed fraction of the file: 1 bit per page, or 1/65,536 of the file at 8 KiB pages (§5.7). It also removes any fixed-point problem when the checkpointer writes them (§13 step 2).

**I-FREE-EXACT.** The persisted slot bitmap of image `k` marks free exactly the slots in `[3, slot_count)` that image `k` does not reference. "References" covers:
- the homes of the image's mapped LPIDs;
- the image's MapLeaf and MapInner pages;
- the image's FreeSet pages;
- the reserved slots of the replica region (§2.4).

The LPID bitmap marks exactly the LPIDs that image `k`'s map leaves unmapped. amcheck verifies both bitmaps exactly: no leak, no double use.

As a result, the slots that eviction allocated after meta `k` are free in image `k`, so a crash reclaims them automatically (PLAN-v3 §3.3).

### 4.4 Slot reuse (I-NO-INPLACE, I-FREE-SAFE)
**In memory**, a slot may be written only if it is referenced by none of:
1. the durable image;
2. the image being written (the epoch-`k` map and its image-record list, §13 step 2, during checkpoint `k`);
3. the current map (the homes of the current epoch's pages);
4. the previous image (§1): image `k−1` while meta `k` is the durable image, until step 6 of checkpoint `k+1` (I-TWO-GEN); or, after an open from meta `k` that could not read meta `k−1`'s free set, every slot below meta `k−1`'s `slot_count` that image `k` does not reference, until the first step 6 after that open (§14 step 2).

Slots 0–2 and the reserved slots (§2.4) are never page homes. The invariants follow:
- **I-NO-INPLACE:** a live page's home is never overwritten. Every page write (eviction or checkpoint) goes to a fresh slot. Mutant **INPLACE-EVICT**: a dirty eviction writes its victim to the slot its current entry names, when that slot is not 0, and keeps it as the home (§15 I-NO-INPLACE; card S-MU).
- **I-FREE-SAFE:** a slot referenced by image `k−1` but not by image `k` becomes reusable only after meta `k+1` is synced (§13 step 6 of checkpoint `k+1`): one checkpoint after the meta that stopped referencing it. The one exception is an open from meta `k` that has no previous meta (§2.2): no valid meta names image `k−1`, so the slots it referenced are reusable once meta `k` is the durable image, as at draft 7.10, and that open has nothing to fall back to (§14 step 4a); a meta whose place this open could not read does not come back, because the open zeroes that place before its first write (§14 step 1). An open whose read of meta `k−1`'s free set fails still protects image `k−1` (I-TWO-GEN, §14 step 2).
- **I-TWO-GEN (two generations):** while meta `k` is the durable image and the newest meta that was synced, the WAL from meta `k−1`'s `wal_start_lsn` stays intact, and so does image `k−1` as long as every open since meta `k` was synced has protected its slots, by reading meta `k−1`'s free set or, when that read fails, by protecting every slot below meta `k−1`'s `slot_count` that image `k` does not reference (below). Step 6 of checkpoint `k` frees only the slots that image `k−2` referenced and neither image `k−1` nor image `k` references, and recycles only segments wholly below meta `k−1`'s `wal_start_lsn` (§12.6, §13 step 6). An open from meta `k` keeps this across a reopen: it reads the previous meta's slot bitmap through its `free_root` (§2.2) and treats every slot that bitmap marks used as referenced, until the first step 6 after the open (§14 step 2). If there is no previous meta, there is no previous image: the open protects nothing more, reports it, and has nothing to fall back to (§14 step 4a). If the previous meta's free set fails a read, by an integrity failure (§3.3, or its decoder's refusal of a page that passes it, §14 step 4a) or a read error (58030), there is no previous image either and nothing to fall back to, but the open still protects image `k−1`: until the first step 6 after the open it treats every slot below meta `k−1`'s `slot_count` that image `k` does not reference as referenced, and it reports the failed read, so a later open that reads that free set finds image `k−1` as it was (§14 step 2; mutant UNREAD-PROTECTS-NOTHING). Neither is an error, unless image `k` fails at open or a read error (58030) stops it (§14 step 4a item 1). Two states lie outside the condition above and rely on nothing more. A meta `k+1` that a crash between step 4's two writes left in one copy, before step 5's sync, is the durable image although it was never synced: image `k` and the WAL from meta `k`'s `wal_start_lsn` are then as this invariant states for a synced meta `k+1`, because step 6 of checkpoint `k+1` never ran. After a retire write (§14 step 4a), meta `k−1` is the durable image with no previous meta, and the WAL from its own `wal_start_lsn` stays intact, because step 6 of checkpoint `k` recycled only below it and nothing recycles before the boot checkpoint's step 6.

  So a fall-back of one generation from the newest synced meta finds image `k−1` and its whole WAL span as they were, when every open since that meta was synced protected image `k−1`'s slots (§14 steps 2 and 4a). If meta `k+1` was synced and then lost too, the open selects meta `k`, and a fall-back from it meets image `k−1` outside the two generations: its recycled start segment fails I-WAL-START, and its reused slots fail I-INTEGRITY when read (§3.3), at open or at a later read (§9.2); neither is silent (§2.2).
  - Mutants: **FREE-ONE-GEN**: step 6 of checkpoint `k` frees the slots that image `k−1` referenced and image `k` does not, as at draft 7.10. **RECYCLE-ONE-GEN**: step 6 of checkpoint `k` recycles the segments wholly below meta `k`'s own `wal_start_lsn`. **OPEN-PROTECTS-ONE-GEN**: an open protects only the opened image's slots. **UNREAD-PROTECTS-NOTHING**: an open whose read of meta `k−1`'s free set fails protects nothing beyond image `k`'s slots, as at draft 7.12.
  - Test `t_two_gen` (A-3c on S3, A-7c, A-7d; §15.1). At A-3c the test calls step 6's release as A-7c's checkpoint will: a slot that image `k−1` referenced and image `k` does not is not allocated before the second release after it stopped being referenced; FREE-ONE-GEN fails it through an allocation hook. At A-7c, checkpoints `k−1`, `k` and `k+1` run with every page rewritten in each interval and a pool at its minimum, so that allocations reach every free slot: write and rename hooks assert that no slot image `k−1` references is written, and no segment at or above meta `k−1`'s `wal_start_lsn` is renamed, before checkpoint `k+1`'s step 6; FREE-ONE-GEN and RECYCLE-ONE-GEN fail it through the hooks. At A-7d, in full: (a) after checkpoint `k`'s step 6, with allocations that would reach image `k−1`'s superseded slots and a segment boundary crossed, a post-sync LOST on both copies of meta `k` and a crash; the open selects meta `k−1`, replays every acked `on` commit, those after meta `k` included, and every image passes amcheck. FREE-ONE-GEN fails (a): a reused slot fails I-INTEGRITY, and the open gives XX001. RECYCLE-ONE-GEN fails (a): meta `k−1`'s start segment is gone, and the open gives XX001 (I-WAL-START). (b) an open from meta `k` with a pool at its minimum, so that pass 2 and the boot checkpoint evict, a crash before the boot checkpoint's step 4, so that no copy of its meta is written and the next open selects meta `k` again, then a post-sync fault on a page of image `k` that the next open reads at step 2: the next open falls back to meta `k−1` with proof (§14 step 4a) and recovers every acked commit. OPEN-PROTECTS-ONE-GEN fails (b): an eviction overwrote a slot of image `k−1`, which then fails I-INTEGRITY, and the open gives XX001. (c) An open from meta `k` whose read of meta `k−1`'s free set fails, in one run through bytes a hook corrupts (an integrity failure) and in another through a read error a hook makes (58030), with a pool at its minimum and acked commits after meta `k` enough that pass 2 and the boot checkpoint evict until allocations reach every free slot, and a crash before the boot checkpoint's step 4, so that no copy of its meta is written and the next open selects meta `k` again: the open runs, and a write hook asserts that none of its writes lands in a slot that image `k−1` references. The next open reads the free set, meets a hook that corrupts one read of a page of image `k` that step 2 reads, falls back with proof (§14 step 4a), recovers every acked commit, and every image passes amcheck. UNREAD-PROTECTS-NOTHING fails (c) through the write hook.

A slot that only the current map referenced is reusable as soon as the page's new home is written (card S-MU). It is an earlier eviction home of a page in this epoch, never adopted by an image, which a writer tells by its ref's epoch, the current one, since every image is built from content of an earlier epoch; or an eviction home of a MapLeaf that no image-record list names, whatever epoch its ref carries, since no image names one: step 2 writes each MapLeaf it changes in image `k` to a slot of its own and names every other by its image ref (§4.1, §13 step 2). The home a MapLeaf had from the open is an image's slot, which step 6 frees once no image references it (§13 step 6).

### 4.5 Allocation policy and file growth
- **Allocation.** Slots are allocated lowest-free-first, which keeps the file dense and reuses freed space before the file grows.
- **Growth.** When no slot is free, the file grows by `max(1 MiB, file_len / 16)`, rounded down to a whole number of slots (`set_len`; card S-MU). The new slots are free.
- **Errors are fail-stop.** A failed extension or a failed page write is fail-stop (§9.2), with 53100 or 58030.
- **No shrinking in 2.0.** The file never shrinks in 2.0: GC returns space to the free set, not to the filesystem (§19 Q1).

---

## 5. Buffer manager and memory model (N8, N16)

### 5.1 Structure
- **Pool.** A fixed array of `F` frames, set at open from the N16 budget as R-1-memory §4.1 says. It **never grows**.
- **Frame.** Holds the page bytes, a task-fair latch (§5.3), a dirty flag, a pin count and the content epoch.
- **Page table.** A hash map from a page to its frame (`spikes/e0-btree/src/pool.rs`). A page is an LPID or a MapLeaf's map position, the two keyed apart, as §13 step 2 keys image records: a position's header `id` can equal an LPID, since §3.1 keeps positions apart only from one another. The table holds at most one frame for a page, and holds a frame only while the frame holds the page or is loading it (§5.4 "What a pin finds"; I-PIN-CURRENT). Mutant **PAGE-KEY-MERGED**: the table keys a MapLeaf by its header `id`, so the MapLeaf at index `i` and LPID `i` share one entry (`t_pin_load_race`).
- **No `unsafe`** (N9).

Optimistic latches and pointer swizzling come only when an E0 or E7 trigger fires (PLAN-v3 §3.12).

### 5.2 Eviction
**Victim choice.** Clock. The evictor takes victims with `try_latch` and **never blocks on a victim**: if the latch is not free, it moves on. Pinned frames are skipped.
- **Clean victims** are dropped.
- **Dirty victims.** A dirty victim is written **to a fresh slot** (§4.4), and its map entry is updated in memory to `(new slot, content epoch, crc)` before its frame leaves the page table, so that a pin finds the page in its frame or through that entry (§5.4 "What a pin finds"). Mutant **EVICT-SKIP-DIRTY**: a frame acquisition passes over dirty frames as if they could not be evicted, so a dirty set larger than the pool deadlocks or is refused (§15 I-POOL-BOUND; card S-MU). This is a gated operation (§5.6), but it never blocks on the gate:
  - an eviction made inside a gated operation runs under that operation's hold;
  - any other eviction (a reader, a background evictor) only *tries* to take the gate shared. If the try fails because a flip is pending, the evictor takes a clean victim. If no victim is clean, the thread releases its latches and restarts (§5.3); a thread that has published a page as being loaded ends that load first, serving nothing, and loads the page again only once the flip has run (§5.4 "The load").

  A blocking acquire there could deadlock: the waiting flip blocks new shared holders, while the current holders may wait on that thread's latches.
- **Dirty victims from the previous epoch.** If the victim's content epoch is `k < e` during checkpoint `k` (after the flip, before step 2 has written that page), the write's record is also appended to the image-record list (§13 step 2) before the victim's latch is released, and the page counts as written for image `k`.

A dirty page may therefore be evicted **at any time**, independently of checkpoints. This is what lets write-heavy loads and long-reader version retention exceed RAM (PLAN-v3 §3.3).

### 5.3 Latch rules
- **Order.** Latches are acquired in one order: directory pages, which only an operation that changes the directory latches (§6.8 "Root names"); then the pages of one tree, top-down, and among siblings left to right (I-LATCH, §6.2); then map pages, in radix order (a MapInner before its children, siblings by ascending LPID range). Nothing ever latches sideways or upward: a thread blocks only on a latch ranked after every latch it holds, map pages ranking after every tree page. Among map pages two rules make that hold: a claim of an epoch copy takes no map latch, since only step 2 writes the image map (§5.6 "Reserve", §13 step 2); and a map page's write releases that page's latch before it latches the parent (§4.1).
- **Latch scope.** A thread holds frame latches only inside one storage call, and only on one tree's pages. Before the call returns, and before it starts an access to any other tree, it releases every frame latch and every pin it holds. Two exceptions add no wait edge: a `try_latch` acquirer (an eviction victim and its page-local prune, §5.2 and §10.3; an epoch-copy claim or a reserve draw, §5.6), which never blocks on a tree latch and under a victim's latch takes only map latches; and a stale root name's first latch (§6.8 "Root names", step (2)), which may land on another tree's page while the thread holds no other latch. Directory pages count as no tree's here: a directory change latches its directory path and then at most one tree's pages, as the order says. No gated operation latches pages of two trees: apply, settle, convert, the GC sweep and index maintenance each work on one tree per gated operation (N2), and a cross-tree check such as an index scan's per-entry verification (C-T6 §8) is a separate call.
- **Fair latches.** A frame latch is task-fair: a waiting exclusive request blocks new shared grants; a waiter is overtaken by at most `K` later requests, a count fixed when the latch is built, never a time; and `try_latch` never succeeds over a queued exclusive waiter. Strict FIFO handoff is not required, since it convoys on a write-hot root. The latch makes every lock, wait, wake-up and atomic of its own through the store's sync layer (§15 "The interleaving harness"), so the harness runs the latch's own code and `t_root_latch_fair` counts its bypass (card S-THETA). `std::sync::RwLock` promises no policy and does not qualify. Nor does a latch that parks threads on primitives of its own, `parking_lot`'s RwLock among them: the harness cannot schedule its waits, and its unlock is not fair by count (it unlocks unfairly by default). A-3b pins `K`, and measures the latch's cost against PLAN-v3 §3.12's buffer-pool trigger.
- **No recursive latch.** No thread latches a page it already holds, in either mode. A task-fair latch deadlocks on a recursive shared acquisition behind a queued writer; the latch scope makes one impossible, since no access starts under a latch.
- **No I/O while holding an exclusive latch on an inner node.** An operation that needs a non-resident page releases its latches, pins the page (loading it), and restarts from the root, re-validating what it saw (PLAN-v3 §3.3). Pins guarantee residency, not identity, so validation re-reads the parent under its latch.
- **A reader's hold includes no page read.** A reader that needs a non-resident page releases its latches, pins the page and re-descends, as an inner exclusive holder does (above), so a reader's latch hold is CPU-bounded and a gated writer queued on it waits for no I/O. A writer's hold of its leaf may still include a read: on a leaf root, a chain build or free at `ROW_LIMIT` reads MapLeafs in place under the root's exclusive latch (§6.7), which A-5 measures.
- A frame's latch is never held across a wait on another transaction.
- **No latch escapes a call.** No store API returns a borrow into a frame, no latch guard outlives the call that took it, and a storage call never yields (an `await`, or an N10 cooperative step boundary) while it holds a latch, nor while it holds a page being loaded (§5.4 "The load"; card S-KAPPA). So a load starts and ends within one step of its task, and under N10's cooperative mode no task finds a page that another task of its thread is loading: no task waits for a load there, neither a creation, which waits holding its tree's latches, nor a chain build or free, which waits holding its leaf's (§5.4, §6.7), so none of them blocks the one thread or yields holding a latch. `!Send` guards do not enforce this on a single-threaded executor, so `t_latch_scope`'s tracker checks it at every yield point, where a load would hold its frame's pin (mutants LATCH-ACROSS-YIELD and LOAD-ACROSS-YIELD, §15 I-LATCH).
- **Why latching cannot deadlock.** Every holder waits only down the order. A latch-free thread holds nothing, or only the gate while it waits on a latch, and a latch holder never waits on the gate (§5.6), so it is on no cycle. A cursor is parked between calls and holds nothing (§6.6), so no thread reads under a cursor's latches. No thread holds latches of two trees except under the two exceptions above, and neither adds a wait edge. A gated writer latches within one tree, top-down, after the gate. A pin-only holder has no latch edge. A wait for a load (§5.4 "The load") adds none either: a thread waits for a load holding no latch, except a creation, which may hold its tree's latches, and a chain build or free, which holds its leaf's (§6.7), and none of them holds a map latch while it waits (mutants MAPLEAF-WAIT-UNDER-INNER and CREATE-WAIT-UNDER-MAPLEAF); a load waits on no tree latch, no gate and no other thread's call for a frame, since it takes only its page's MapLeaf's latch shared, or for a MapLeaf its MapInner's, whose holders wait for no frame or load (§4.1), and obtains each frame it needs without waiting for another thread's call to end, as §5.4's count lets every frame acquisition do ("The fairness bound", below); and a load whose thread must restart or wait for the flip ends first and wakes its waiters (§5.2), so no thread waits on a load whose loader waits on that thread (card S-KAPPA). A directory change holds the directory path and then at most one tree's pages; the directory ranks first and no ordinary operation latches it, so it waits only on that tree's brief holders. A `try_latch` acquirer never waits on a tree latch, and under a victim's latch it takes only map latches, which rank last. The checkpointer's step 2 waits on a latched frame holding no latch, map latches included (§13); it takes a current MapLeaf's latch only after a written frame's latch, and in the map phase it holds one latch at a time. Among map pages a thread blocks only toward a later radix rank: a claim takes no map latch, since only step 2 writes the image map (§5.6 "Reserve"), so two `write_guard`s that exhaust the reserve while holding MapLeafs never wait on each other's MapLeaf; and a map page's write releases the page's latch before it latches the parent (§4.1). At the pool floor, a thread that holds a latch and may still restart never waits for an unpin: with no evictable frame it releases its latches and restarts (§5.2), and a reader pins with no latch held (above). A gated operation past its first mutation cannot restart (§5.6 "Restart"), so it may wait for an unpin while it holds its one tree's latches. Every wait for an unpin, that one included, is finite only because §5.4 counts every pin any thread can hold, the MapLeaf pins a restarted allocation keeps while it holds no latch and waits on the gate included (§5.4's third per-thread term; card S-DELTA, S-1-F7-F2-F2-F2), so the pins of threads that wait on the gate never take the last frame that a gated operation holding the gate needs. With one order, no recursive acquisition and no latch held across a call, fair blocking latches add no cycle.
- **The fairness bound.** A waiter of either mode is granted once the holders present at its arrival and the waiters queued ahead of it have released, plus the bounded bypass, and each hold is bounded by one call's work on one tree, which includes no transaction wait: a batch fill, bounded by A-4e's per-call bound (§6.6); a descent step, CPU-bounded for a reader (above); a writer's critical section, which on a leaf root includes a chain build or free bounded by the maximum value size (§6.7, measured by A-5); a frame acquisition's victim write; a creation's wait for another thread's load of the page it creates, a load that reads no slot and needs no frame beyond the one it published (§5.4 "The load"), so the wait lasts one brief hold of a map latch; a chain build's or free's wait for another thread's load of a chain page or its MapLeaf (§6.7), which lasts that load's frame acquisition and one page read, since a load waits on no tree latch, no gate and no other thread's call for a frame (above); and, for a gated operation past its first mutation, a pool-floor wait for an unpin (above) per frame it obtains. That wait is bounded as a frame acquisition is, because no unpin has to happen for it to end: §5.4's count, which counts every pin any thread can hold, leaves for every pin a thread may still take a frame outside `R_EPOCH` that it can obtain without any other thread's call ending: a frame no pin holds, or a MapLeaf whose only pins are §4.1's, held for dirty pages no thread pins, which a frame acquisition releases by evicting those pages, each a frame no pin holds (R-1-memory §4.4); so the operation waits only for such a frame's brief latch holder, at most one per other thread at any instant, namely an evictor's victim write, under which it takes only map latches, whose holders never wait for a frame (§4.1), or step 2's latch on a frame (§13). It therefore depends on no pin holder's call, and on no waiter queued behind the operation's latches, so the bound is not circular, and each such wait adds at most one write per other thread at any instant to the hold A-3b's budget is sized from; a schedule can keep moving the frame the clock reaches, so A-3b sizes that budget with slack for this contention. The bound rests on §5.4's count being true of every pin, so it depends on §5.4's three per-thread terms (above) and on `R_MAP` as §5.4 sets it from R-1-memory §4.4: one MapLeaf for each page a thread's path term can hold dirty, and one for each epoch-copy frame; `⌈dirty frames / 510⌉` would understate them, since with scattered LPIDs each dirty page can hold a MapLeaf of its own. This covers writers and readers alike, every holder shape and writers among themselves, and nothing is exempt. A gated writer blocked on a root delays a pending flip by at most the holds ahead of it on that latch, each bounded as above (at most one per thread), plus the bounded bypass, as §5.6 already permits for a page read.

### 5.4 Pins and the minimum pool size
**Minimum size.** `F ≥ threads × 3 × (max tree height + 2) + R_EPOCH + R_MAP`, where `threads` counts every thread that pins frames, the checkpointer and the store's background jobs included, the store's own threads as well as its callers', and under N10's cooperative mode every task that can hold a pin while it waits, and `max tree height` counts a tree's levels, root and leaf included, the directory's among them: it is the open's height bound `H`, which an open sets from its pool (R-1-memory §4.5; a read-only open from its evictable part, before each op its pass 2 replays, R-1-memory §5.3), so that no descent pins a page deeper than `H` and no root split makes a tree taller than `H`. A descent that would pin a page at depth `H + 1` stops with 53200 before that pin, which a client read returns to its caller and which poisons a gated operation (§9.2), so a read of a tree written under a larger pool and taller than `H` gets 53200 (§19 T8). An operation whose reservation includes a root split of a tree of `H` levels raises 53200 before its first mutation (§6.4), which poisons it, so no root split makes a tree of `H + 1` levels (mutants HEIGHT-SPLIT-UNCHECKED and HEIGHT-DESCENT-UNCHECKED, test `t_height_bound`, below; §19 T8). Each thread counts every pin it can hold at once, whether or not it holds a latch, in three terms of `max tree height + 2` frames each (card S-DELTA, S-1-F7-F2-F2-F2):
- **the path:** the latch-coupled path, at most `h` pages on a tree of `h` levels, or a pack step's path down to `P` with `L` and `R` below it, at most `h + 1`, plus the one page a restart pinned cold (§5.3, §5.6 "Restart"), which its retry's descent reaches or releases: at most `h + 2`. A chain build or free (§6.7), which holds only its leaf, reads its chain's pages and their MapLeafs in place, holding at most two chain pages and their two MapLeafs at once and releasing each before it pins a third, so its pins stay within the thread's three terms;
- **the reservation's pages:** the pages one gated operation creates, all reserved before its first mutation (§6.4): a split at every level of its tree, the root's split making two (§6.3), or CreateTree's root page with a split at every level of the directory (§6.8);
- **the reservation's MapLeafs:** the MapLeafs one gated operation pins for the LPIDs it allocates or frees, at most one per LPID: a split's or CreateTree's allocations (§5.6, §6.4, §6.8), and the frees of a pack step or a DropTree step (§5.6, §6.5, §6.8). A restarted allocation keeps these pins while it holds no latch and waits on the gate (§5.6 "Restart"), which is why they are counted per thread and not in `R_MAP`;
- `R_EPOCH` is the epoch-copy reserve (§5.6);
- `R_MAP = threads × (max tree height + 2) + R_EPOCH` covers the MapLeafs that §4.1's pins hold and that no frame acquisition can release by evicting: one for each page a thread's path term can hold dirty, whether §4.1's pin or the thread's own holds it, and one for each epoch-copy frame (R-1-memory §4.4 derives it). A MapLeaf whose dirty pages no thread pins is not counted: evicting those pages, which a frame acquisition does (§5.2), ends its pins; a read-only open, which evicts no dirty page, counts such MapLeafs with its dirty frames instead (§14.1). No constant bounds §4.1's pins themselves: with scattered LPIDs each dirty page can hold a MapLeaf of its own, so `⌈dirty frames / 510⌉`, their number when dirty pages are packed onto MapLeafs, is the least they reach, not the most. A test may set `R_MAP` higher; a larger value only adds frames.

**Below the minimum,** the open is refused with 53200. A thread that cannot pin a frame because every frame is pinned waits for an unpin. Mutant **POOL-GROW**: a frame acquisition that finds no frame it can obtain takes memory for a frame beyond `F` instead of evicting or waiting for an unpin, so the pool holds more than `F` pages (§15 I-POOL-BOUND; card S-MU). The minimum makes that wait finite. A parked cursor holds no latch and no pin (§6.6), so each thread counts one operation's pins and open cursors add nothing to `F`. The wait is finite only because this formula counts every pin a thread can hold, those it keeps while it waits on the gate included (§5.3 "Why latching cannot deadlock").
- Mutant **POOL-MIN-PATH-ONLY**: the minimum counts only the path per thread, `threads × (max tree height + 2)`, as at draft 7.12. Test `t_pool_minimum` (A-3b; §15 I-POOL-BOUND, §15.1): for one, two and eight callers' threads, to each of which it adds the build's own threads that pin frames (its checkpointer, evictors and background jobs, as the build reports them), at every allowed page size and the height floor `H_min` (R-1-memory §4.5), the test computes the minimum from the formula above, with `R_EPOCH = threads + s + 1`, `s` as the build reports it, and `R_MAP` as above (R-1-memory §4.3, §4.4), not by asking the build for them; a pool one frame below that minimum is refused with 53200 and writes nothing, and a pool at it is accepted.
