POOL-MIN-PATH-ONLY accepts the first, and so do **MIN-RMAP-DENSE** (the build sizes `R_MAP` as `⌈dirty frames / 510⌉`, 0 at open) and **MIN-RESERVE-NO-STEP2** (the build sizes `R_EPOCH` as `threads + 1`, leaving out step 2's share).
- Mutant **RESTART-KEEPS-EMPTY-MAPLEAF**: a restart keeps the pin of a MapLeaf left with no free LPID (§5.6 "Restart"), so a restarted allocation holds more MapLeaf pins than the LPIDs it takes. Test `t_alloc_restart_progress`'s *pool-floor variant* (A-4a, with a test fanout, S1 for the flip and S5 for the second and third trees; §15 I-POOL-BOUND, §15.1): a pool at exactly the minimum, at the fixture's height `h`, for its four threads, the two inserting threads, the hook's thread and S1's flip thread, together with the build's own threads that pin frames, if it has any (`threads` counts every thread that pins frames), with `R_MAP` set to cover the MapLeafs that the fixture's dirty pages pin, the hook's splits' included, and two inserts, one into each of two trees of height `h`, each into a full leaf that holds no dead entry, so that no prune makes room (§10.3), and whose split cascades to the root, so that it allocates `h + 1` LPIDs, each the lowest free LPID under an evicted MapLeaf of its own. At each insert's first restart a hook holds it once it has released its latches and the gate and pinned its MapLeafs, until both inserts are there; S1's flip then takes the gate exclusively, and the hook lets both go on, so that both wait on the gate behind the flip, holding those pins and no latch. S1 releases the gate once a hook in the gate's shared acquire, which fires when an acquirer finds the gate held exclusively, has fired for both; A-3c's gate offers that hook as a test seam: its shared acquire tries the gate first and, when it finds the gate held exclusively, calls the hook before it blocks. After each of an insert's first `h + 2` restarts, once its retry holds the gate shared and before it takes the free-set lock for its LPIDs, a hook holds the retry while the hook's thread takes every free LPID under the MapLeafs that restart pinned, by inserts into full leaves of a third tree whose parents have room, one split at a time; the inserting thread then holds the gate shared, its pins and at most its own tree's latches, and no flip is pending, so those splits take the gate shared, the third tree's latches and the free-set lock without waiting on anything it holds. A pin tracker, a hook on the pool's pin and unpin, counts the pins each thread takes for its own operations (their paths, a restart's cold page, their reservations' pages and MapLeafs), not the pin a MapLeaf holds for each dirty page it maps (§4.1, `R_MAP`), whichever thread dirtied the page, nor a thread's own pin of the MapLeaf of a page it will dirty (§5.6 "Restart", `R_MAP`; card S-MU), nor an epoch-copy frame (`R_EPOCH`); it asserts at every pin that no thread holds more than `h + 2` MapLeaf pins or more than `2 × (h + 2)` tree-page pins, and both splits finish within the restart bound (§5.6). RESTART-KEEPS-EMPTY-MAPLEAF fails it through the tracker: after each hooked round the retry holds only emptied pins and restarts (§5.6), and each such restart keeps them and pins at least one more MapLeaf, so that by its third restart a thread holds more than `h + 2` MapLeaf pins (`2h + 2` at its second where the round's `h + 1` LPIDs lie under `h + 1` MapLeafs).
- Mutant **MAPPIN-KEPT**: a dirty eviction does not end its victim's §4.1 pin on the MapLeaf, so a MapLeaf whose dirty pages have all left the pool stays pinned. Test `t_pool_floor_scattered` (A-3c on S3, A-4a in full; §15 I-POOL-BOUND, §15.1), for the count `R_MAP` rests on: a test map fanout; the build's own pinning threads at 0 (N10's cooperative mode); a pool of exactly the minimum for three test threads A, B and G at `H_min`, with `R_EPOCH` and `R_MAP` as R-1-memory §4.3 and §4.4 set them; no flip, no checkpoint and no other caller. First the pool is filled with dirty, unpinned pages whose LPIDs lie under MapLeafs of their own, each MapLeaf held only by its page's §4.1 pin. Then A and B each pin `H_min + 2` of those pages, the most a thread's path term holds, and mutate them, so that each pinned dirty page holds a MapLeaf no other resident dirty page maps, and take the rest of their three per-thread terms; a hook holds each once it has, holding pins only. Then G, holding the gate shared, so that a frame acquisition it cannot complete waits for an unpin rather than restarts, as a gated operation's past its first mutation does, obtains `3 × (H_min + 2)` frames one at a time, by evictions. A hook in the pool fires when a frame acquisition is about to wait for an unpin; it must not fire, for A, B or G. MAPPIN-KEPT makes it fire: the evicted pages' MapLeafs stay pinned, and the frames a thread can obtain run out before G's last acquisition, or before B's. The threads take their pins, mutations and frame acquisitions through the pool's own calls, the most §5.4 counts per thread in the layout `R_MAP` is counted for, which no operation's pins reach (an operation takes its pins before its first mutation, all but a chain build's or free's, at most four at once; §5.4, §5.6), so both forms make them in the test itself. The forms differ in the fill's MapLeafs: at A-3c S3's growth call makes them, as A-4a's restart will, and in full, at A-4a, A-4a's own allocations and restarts make them.
- Mutants **HEIGHT-SPLIT-UNCHECKED** (a root split of a tree of `H` levels proceeds) and **HEIGHT-DESCENT-UNCHECKED** (a descent pins a page deeper than `H`). Test `t_height_bound` (A-4a, with S3 for (b)'s lowered bound and S5 for its second tree; A-7f in full; §15 I-POOL-BOUND, §15.1): the smallest allowed page size; keys of `K_MAX` bytes that share all but their last 8, so that separators are long and an inner page holds few; one caller and the build's own pinning threads at 0; a pool that R-1-memory §4.5 sizes for `H = H_min` (a test build may lower `H_min`); no flip, no checkpoint. (a) Ascending inserts go on until one returns 53200: a hook on root splits sees no tree reach `H + 1` levels, the insert that fails is the one whose reservation includes a root split of the `H`-level tree, and the store is poisoned (every later call returns the error; a write hook sees no write after the mark). (b) A second store of the same shape builds a tree of `H + 1` levels under a pool sized for `H + 1`, and a test-only call then lowers the bound to `H`, as an open with the smaller pool would set it (S3): a client read of a key in that tree returns 53200 and poisons nothing (a read of a second, two-level tree, S5, succeeds after it), and an insert into the tall tree returns 53200 and poisons the store. In full (A-7f), (b)'s store checkpoints after its last insert and closes, and is reopened with the smaller pool, from which the open sets `H`, with no amcheck at open; its second tree is made by CreateTree. HEIGHT-SPLIT-UNCHECKED fails (a): the root-split hook sees the tree reach `H + 1` levels, and the insert that fails is a later one, at its descent, not the one whose reservation includes the root split. HEIGHT-DESCENT-UNCHECKED fails (b), in both forms: the read returns the row.

**What a pin finds** (card S-DELTA, S-1-F7-F2-F3-F2; card S-THETA). A pin looks for the page in the page table first. Only when it finds no frame there for the page does it read the page's current reference, an LPID's current map entry or a MapLeaf's ref (§4.1), and only after it has put a frame of its own there for the page, to load it (below). It finds the LPID **unmapped** when no frame holds it and that entry has slot 0 (§4.1); a MapLeaf that no frame holds and whose ref is null has not been made (§5.6 "Restart"). A page that has been allocated and not yet written has no home: its current map entry holds slot 0 until a write gives it one, a dirty eviction (§5.2) or step 2's write of a frame not mutated since the flip (§13 step 2), and that write sets the entry before the frame leaves the page table, so until then a frame holds the page. So a pin never finds a mapped LPID unmapped, a new root or a new split page included: a pin that finds a referenced LPID unmapped has found damage (XX001, §9.2), and on the root-name path it re-reads the entry first (§6.8 "Root names"). Mutant **ROOT-PIN-MAP-FIRST**: a pin reads the map entry before the page table, so a resident, never-written page reads as unmapped (`t_root_name_reuse` case F, §15 I-LINK-ATOMIC; `t_pin_load_race` case (j), I-PIN-CURRENT).

**The load** (card S-THETA). A pin that finds no frame for the page obtains a frame, and puts it in the page table for the page, as a page **being loaded**, only if in the same step it finds the table still without a frame for the page; otherwise it gives the frame back and looks again. Only then does it read the page's current reference. The load holds a pin on its frame throughout, so no eviction takes it (§5.2). It reads the slot the reference names, and the frame holds the page only if the bytes pass §3.3; if the reference has slot 0, or the read or a check fails, the load takes its frame out of the page table, and nothing is served (§3.3). A load also ends so, serving nothing, before its thread restarts or waits for the flip, as a thread that holds no gate does when a flip is pending and every frame it could take for the page's MapLeaf is dirty (§5.2), and that thread loads the page again only once the flip has run: a gated operation may be waiting for the load, and the flip waits for that operation. A pin that finds the page being loaded waits for that load to end, and reads neither the reference nor the slot itself; when the load ends it looks again, so it pins the frame if the load made it hold the page, and otherwise starts afresh: it never takes a frame whose load failed. A page being loaded is not resident: a thread that may not read a page under its latches (§5.3) does not wait for a load either, and restarts as it would for a page that no frame holds. No thread waits for a load while it holds a map latch, since the load takes the page's MapLeaf's latch, or for a MapLeaf its MapInner's, to read the reference (§4.1). A creation of a page (a split's, a chain's or a new tree's, §6.4, §6.7, §6.8; a MapLeaf, §5.6 "Restart") that finds a frame for its page in the page table makes no second one. If the page is being loaded, the creation waits for that load to end and looks again, holding no map latch; it may hold its tree's latches meanwhile, since that load reads no page: the page's reference has slot 0 until the creation's page is written, and the load reads it through a MapLeaf that the creation holds pinned (§5.6), or, for a MapLeaf, through a MapInner, which is always resident (§4.1). If the frame holds the page freed (§4.2), the creation makes its page in that frame. A frame that holds an allocated LPID's page live is damage (XX001, §9.2), since the LPID bitmap called a live LPID free; for a MapLeaf it is one that another restart made (§5.6).

**Current references move only under a frame** (I-PIN-CURRENT; card S-THETA). Once the open has built the current map from an image (§14 step 2, and again from meta `k−1` after a fall-back has discarded every frame and the map built from image `k`, §14 step 4a item 2), a page's current reference changes only while a frame holding the page is in the page table, held by the thread that changes it: a dirty eviction (§5.2) and step 2's write of a frame (§13 step 2) hold its latch, a map page's two-step write holds a pin on it until its parent's ref is set (§4.1), and a free holds the page in `write_guard` (§4.2). So the reference a load reads stays the page's current one until the load ends, and the slot it names stays referenced by the current map (§4.4): no load installs bytes that a write has superseded. Mutants (test `t_pin_load_race`, §15 I-PIN-CURRENT): **LOAD-REF-BEFORE-PUBLISH** (a pin reads the reference, and the slot into a frame of its own, before that frame is in the page table, and puts it there afterwards if the page is still absent: a load that read the reference before another thread loaded the page, wrote it and evicted it installs the old bytes, and that write is lost); **LOADING-SERVED** (a page being loaded counts as resident, so a pin that finds it is served its frame before the load ends); **FAILED-LOAD-KEPT** (a failed load leaves its frame in the page table, so a later pin is served bytes that failed §3.3); **MISS-TWO-FRAMES** (a pin puts its frame in the page table without looking again, so two pins of one page each load it); **CREATE-OVER-LOADING** (a creation that finds its page being loaded puts a frame of its own in the page table as well); **CREATE-BESIDE-FREED** (a creation that finds a freed page's frame puts a new frame in the page table for the page); **EVICT-UNLINK-BEFORE-ENTRY** (a dirty eviction takes its frame out of the page table before it sets the map entry, so a pin between the two loads the old bytes); **LOAD-KEPT-ACROSS-RESTART** (a load whose thread restarts, or waits for the flip, keeps its frame in the page table as a page being loaded, so a gated operation that waits for the load keeps the flip waiting while the loader waits for the flip); **RELOAD-BEFORE-FLIP** (card S-KAPPA: a thread whose load ended for a pending flip loads the page again before that flip has run, so it can publish the page as being loaded ahead of the gated operation that needs it, again and again while a scheduler favours it); **CREATE-WAIT-UNDER-MAPLEAF** (card S-KAPPA: a creation that finds its page being loaded waits for that load holding the page's MapLeaf latch exclusively, as one that sets the entry in the same hold would, while the load takes that latch shared to read the reference).

### 5.5 What lives in the pool
The pool is the **only** place tree content, overflow content and MapLeaf content live in memory. Versions retained for long readers are tree entries (§7), so under memory pressure they occupy **disk, not RAM**: they are evicted like any other page. This makes X-4's ruling R2 Assumption 1 true by construction: (b) adds no unspillable N16 consumer for long readers (PLAN-v3 §4, "Ruling R2").

### 5.6 The epoch gate and `write_guard`
**The epoch gate.** The gate is a `RwLock`. It is held **shared** by every gated operation:
- every tree mutation, including its splits;
- every pack step, root split and root collapse;
- every GC page mutation and every LPID free;
- every directory change;
- every dirty eviction;
- every apply insert.

Each of these holds the gate from its first page mutation to its last. The checkpoint's flip takes the gate **exclusively** (§13 step 0).
- **Acquisition.** An operation acquires the gate before its first latch, never twice, and releases it after its last latch. Nested work, such as an eviction or a page-local prune inside an insert, runs under the outer hold. A thread that holds latches and no gate never blocks on the gate (§5.2).
- **Restart.** An operation restarts only before its first mutation; an LPID allocation counts as one. That restart (§5.3) releases its latches and the gate, pins what it needs, keeping no pin but the page it found cold, its reservation's pages and MapLeafs, which §5.4 counts per thread, and the MapLeafs of the pages it will dirty (below), and re-acquires the gate. A flip may land in the gap, which is harmless because the operation has changed nothing. After its first mutation an operation never restarts and never releases the gate before its end. So whatever it would otherwise read under an inner page's exclusive latch (§5.3), such as the MapLeaf of an LPID it frees, it pins before that mutation. It may read a page in place only with no such latch held, such as a chain's next page under its leaf's latch (§6.7). Among those pins is the MapLeaf of each clean page it will dirty, from which that page's first mutation takes §4.1's pin (card S-MU; R-1-memory §14's Q-MAPLEAF-DIRTY): the operation pins it before its first mutation, reading a cold one in place only where §5.3 lets it read, with no inner page's exclusive latch held, and otherwise restarting to pin it. A restart keeps these pins with its reservation's, so that its retry does not find them cold again, and a retry releases, before it pins another, each that no page it now plans to dirty needs; they are `R_MAP`'s, at most one per page of the thread's path term (§5.4, R-1-memory §4.4), not a per-thread term. A split's reservation precedes its prune: the prune is a mutation (§10.3), so the pages a split latches and the MapLeafs of the LPIDs it allocates are pinned before it (§6.4). The prune frees no LPID (§10.3), so the reservation covers only the split's own pages and allocations. An LPID allocation takes its LPID under the free-set lock only if that LPID's MapLeaf is resident, and pins it before the lock is released. Otherwise it restarts, which is legal only before the first mutation. An LPID with no MapLeaf yet, one that `next_lpid++` would take past the last MapLeaf, counts as not resident. The restart creates and pins its empty MapLeaf in a gated operation of its own, with no other latch held, before it re-acquires the gate for the retry. It obtains the MapLeaf's frame first, holding no latch, because obtaining a frame may evict (§5.2), so no eviction or I/O runs under the MapInner's exclusive latch (§5.3) or the free-set lock (§4.2). Only then, once any missing level or MapInner above the MapLeaf has been made in the same gated operation (§4.1 "Growth"), does it latch exclusively the MapInner that holds the MapLeaf's ref, and it creates the MapLeaf in that frame only if no frame for the MapLeaf is in the page table and the ref is still null. It looks in the page table first, as a pin does (§5.4 "What a pin finds"): a MapLeaf that another restart made has no home until its first write, so its ref stays null while a frame holds it (§4.1), and the ref alone cannot tell it from a MapLeaf never made (card S-THETA). Otherwise another restart created it first, and that restart's retry may already have mapped an LPID there, so a second MapLeaf would lose the mapping (I-LPID-EXACT): this restart releases the unused frame and pins the MapLeaf, in the frame that holds it or through the ref, releasing the MapInner's latch first if pinning must load the MapLeaf or wait for another thread's load of it (§5.3, §5.4). Mutants **MAPLEAF-EXISTS-BY-REF** (the restart decides by the ref alone, and puts its own MapLeaf's frame in the page table in place of the first one's) and **MAPLEAF-WAIT-UNDER-INNER** (a restart that finds the MapLeaf being loaded waits for that load holding the MapInner's latch, which the load needs) (`t_alloc_restart_progress`'s race variant, §15 I-LINK-ATOMIC). An empty MapLeaf maps no LPID, so creating it changes no image's mapped set and is not an allocation. A restarted allocation keeps the MapLeaf pins its restart took and, on its retry, takes the lowest free LPIDs that lie under MapLeafs it holds pinned, before any other; under a pinned new MapLeaf, the LPIDs from `next_lpid` on count as free. That is the only exception to §4.2's lowest-first order. The arm makes progress because a pinned MapLeaf stays resident and a free only adds LPIDs: the retry restarts again only if other allocations took free LPIDs under its pins meanwhile, leaving fewer than it needs, and either the rest lie under non-resident MapLeafs or one of its pins has no free LPID left. A retry that holds the pin of a MapLeaf left with no free LPID takes no LPID under a MapLeaf it does not hold pinned: it restarts instead. Each such restart drops the pin of every MapLeaf left with no free LPID before it pins another, and pins at least one more MapLeaf, so an operation that takes `n` LPIDs never holds more than `n` such pins at once, within §5.4's third per-thread term, not `R_MAP` (mutant RESTART-KEEPS-EMPTY-MAPLEAF, §5.4). The bound in restarts: with no concurrent allocation and no change to its path, an allocation restarts at most once in all; each further restart is charged to LPIDs another operation took or to a change another operation made to its path, so some operation always progresses and the arm cannot livelock. An operation that allocates several LPIDs, such as a split that also splits its parent, takes them in one hold of the free-set lock, and only if every one's MapLeaf is resident, so its allocations are one mutation and its restart stays legal until they are taken. An allocation made after the operation's first mutation must have reserved its LPID and MapLeaf before that mutation, or must run with no inner exclusive latch held, reading a non-resident MapLeaf in place as above once it has released the free-set lock, as a chain build does (§6.7). Mutants: DROP-RESTART-MID-OP, DROP-RESTART-AFTER-REMOVE, UNLINK-RESTART-AFTER-UNLINK (§6.8), CHAIN-BUILD-SPLIT, REPLACE-FREE-UNDER-P (§6.7), SPLIT-RESERVE-AFTER-PRUNE, ALLOC-READ-UNDER-P, SPLIT-RESERVE-ALWAYS (§6.4), DELETE-FREE-UNDER-P, CHAIN-BUILD-UNDER-P (§6.7), ALLOC-RETRY-LOWEST, ALLOC-NEW-MAPLEAF-IN-PLACE (§5.6), RESTART-KEEPS-EMPTY-MAPLEAF (§5.4).
- **Waiting.** Holding the gate shared across a page read is permitted; the flip waits for it.
- **What this guarantees.** Every multi-page change is wholly in epoch `k` or wholly in epoch `k+1` (I-CKPT-AT-T).

**I-LINK-ATOMIC.** Every gated operation leaves the reference graph closed. An LPID becomes mapped in the same gated operation that inserts its first reference, and unmapped in the same gated operation that removes its last, after removing it. The references are a parent's child pointer, an entry's chain reference, an Overflow page's next pointer, and a directory entry's root. So every image, at every cut, satisfies I-LPID-EXACT with no special case, and §4.2's immediate reuse never hands out an LPID a mapped page or a directory entry still names.

A freed page's own references end with it, so an operation that frees a path frees it top-down. A thread's name for a page is not a reference: §6.2's coupling and §6.6's re-descent keep it from outliving the latch that gave it, and a root name, which comes from the root table, is valid only for the latch hold it was validated in (§6.8 "Root names").

**`write_guard(frame)`** is the only way to mutate a page.
- **When it copies.** It copies only if the frame's content epoch `< e`, **and** that content is not yet at a slot the image map references (the page is dirty with respect to image `k`). In that case it first copies the bytes into an **epoch-copy frame**, which belongs to the checkpointer. It never copies a MapInner: step 2 builds a MapInner's image page from image refs, which only step 2 and the open set (§4.1, §13 step 2), so no mutation in epoch `k+1` changes what image `k` takes from a MapInner (card S-MU). A MapLeaf whose content epoch is `e − 1` is copied at its first mutation in epoch `e`, whether or not its frame is dirty and whatever slot it was loaded from, unless the map phase has taken that content for image `e − 1`: its content is at no slot the image map references (§13 step 2), and without the copy an eviction could move its current home, which §4.4 then frees, leaving step 2 only its image ref's page, which lacks its epoch-`(e − 1)` mutations (card S-MU).
- **After the call,** the frame's content epoch is `e`.
- **Reserve.** Epoch copies come from the `R_EPOCH` reserve. When the reserve is exhausted, `write_guard` dirty-evicts the oldest epoch copy it can claim (§5.2) and reuses its frame. It claims a copy with `try_latch` on the frame's latch, never a blocking acquire (§5.2), and moves to the next-oldest copy if that latch is not free, so two writers never evict the same copy. A claim that wins a copy's latch re-checks under it that the frame is still the epoch copy it chose, and moves to the next-oldest copy if not, since its candidate may be stale: another claim may have reused the frame, or step 2's map phase may have detached the copy and returned the frame to the reserve (§13 step 2). No thread returns a frame to the reserve while it holds the frame's latch, so a reserve frame's latch is held only by such a re-check or by a draw, briefly and waiting on nothing. `write_guard` draws a frame from the reserve with `try_latch` too, passing over one whose latch is not free, so the draw never waits, and a reserve whose free frames are all latched counts as exhausted; a draw, like a claim, latches one frame and waits on nothing, so it counts as that thread's claim in the bound below. The claim writes the copy to a fresh slot and appends its record to the image-record list (§13 step 2) before it releases the copy's latch. It takes no map latch and calls no `write_guard`, because only step 2 writes the image map. So a claim waits on no latch and claims never nest: a writer that holds a MapLeaf's latch in `write_guard` can claim a copy whichever MapLeaf maps the copy's page. The copy's install is under the latch of the page it preserves. **Bound:** `R_EPOCH` exceeds the copy frames that can be latched at once, namely one claim per thread plus the copies step 2 is writing (§13). So an unclaimed copy always exists, a claim always succeeds, and a writer never waits holding a page latch. A draw and a claim each try every candidate once, and a candidate latched when tried may be free a moment later, so a pass of both may find no frame: the writer then starts again from the draw, and neither ever blocks (card S-MU). R-1-memory §4.3 sets the number, `R_EPOCH ≥ threads + s + 1`, where `s` counts the reserve frames step 2 holds at once (its copy writes in flight, or the map phase's one detached frame), and derives the one claim per thread from this paragraph: nothing a draw or claim does while it holds its frame's latch draws or claims another. Checkpoints therefore never need pool frames proportional to the dirty set, and the image-record list is O(F) (§5.7). Mutant **EXHAUST-DOUBLE-EVICT**: two writers evict one copy, and the second clears the frame the first refilled. Mutant **EXHAUST-BLOCKING-CLAIM**: the claim blocks on the frame's latch. Mutant **CLAIM-LATCHES-MAP**: a claim records its copy by latching the MapLeaf that maps the copy's page. Mutant **IMAGE-RECORD-AFTER-RELEASE**: a claim appends its record after it releases the copy's latch. Test `t_epoch_reserve_two_exhausters` (A-3c on stand-ins, with A-4c's shuttle harness; A-7a through `write_guard`; §15.1): two first touches exhaust the reserve together; both copies reach the image. Its cross variant: two writers each hold a MapLeaf's latch in `write_guard`, A and B, and exhaust the reserve together, every claimable copy being of a page that the other's MapLeaf maps; both finish, both copies reach the image, and a hook asserts that no claim takes a map latch. Its floor variant: a reserve of exactly `threads + s + 1` frames, each holding a claimable copy; `threads − 1` writers are each held by a hook inside a claim, after it has latched the copy it claims and before it releases it, and step 2 by a hook inside a copy's write, holding its `s` frames; one more first touch completes within the step budget, and a hook on the reserve's latches sees no thread hold two reserve frames' latches at once. The hooks release no one until that first touch completes. Mutant **RESERVE-PER-THREAD-ONLY**: the open sizes the reserve as `threads` frames, so the last first touch finds every reserve frame latched. Mutant **CLAIM-DEPTH-TWO**: `write_guard` draws or claims a second reserve frame while it holds its first, for example to copy the MapLeaf that maps the page it copies; the latch hook sees it.

### 5.7 Store memory outside the pool
These consumers do **not** grow with the version count or with retention. Each is accounted separately to R-1 (per-consumer accounting, X-4 R2 item 2):

| Consumer | Bound |
|---|---|
| page table | O(F) |
| the root table (§6.8) | O(trees): one entry per directory entry |
| cursor batch buffers (§6.6) | A-4e's per-call bound per open cursor, within the session's cursor account: a fill copies what the account allows, at least one entry, and an OPEN or FETCH that cannot hold one entry fails with 53200 (R-1-memory §6.3) |
| MapInner pages | ≈ 2/510 of the MapLeaf count, since a level-1 MapInner keeps its MapLeafs' image refs beside their current refs (16 bytes a MapLeaf), plus one per level on the path to the root (§4.1) |
| the two free-set bitmaps | 1 bit per slot plus 1 bit per LPID |
| the pending-free lists (§13 step 6) | ≤ the pages written in two checkpoint intervals: the superseded slots of image `k−1` and of image `k−2` (I-TWO-GEN); each generation held in at most one bit per slot, as a bitmap where a list would be larger (R-1-memory §5.2) |
| the image-record list and the map phase's buffers (§13 step 2) | ≤ F records, one per page whose epoch-`k` content was dirty at the flip; one map page's buffer per radix level, for the deepest radix a `u64` LPID allows; reserved at open (R-1), so a checkpoint allocates none of it; emptied once the map phase has applied them |
| WAL buffers | configured |

Write sets and the lock table are S-2/R-1 consumers, not store consumers. **W6 measured 117 B/key of write set held in RAM** (`spikes/e1-bench/README.md` §W6), which is R-1's concern (X-4 R2 item 3).

---

## 6. B+tree

Reference: `spikes/e0-btree/src/tree.rs` for search, insert, split, latch coupling and the cursor stack. This section adds what E0 deliberately left out (README "Known limits"): merge, page freeing, a stable root and the tree directory.

### 6.1 Shape (I-TREE)
- **Keys** are memcmp-ordered byte strings: entry keys (§7).
- **Leaves** hold entries with strictly ascending keys.
- **Inner pages** hold separators with strictly ascending keys and `n+1` children. Every key below child `i` lies in `[sep(i−1), sep(i))` (E0's T-ORDER).
- **No leaf sibling links.** Range scans use a cursor stack, so copy-on-write never has to fix siblings (PLAN-v3 §3.3).
- One tree per relation and per index.

### 6.2 Latching (I-LATCH)
- **Writers** use latch coupling (crabbing) with exclusive latches: they release every ancestor once the latched child is *safe*, meaning it has room for one more maximum-size cell (E0's T-COUPLING). Only an operation that changes the directory latches a directory page (§6.8 "Root names"), and it couples down the directory in the same way.
- **Readers** use shared latches, top-down. They hold the parent while they latch the child.
- **Order.** Within one tree, latches are taken in (depth ascending, then left to right among siblings); directory pages come before any tree page and map pages after, in §5.3's order.

The consequence that §4.2 and §6.6 rely on: no thread can reach an inner or leaf page except through a latched parent or a re-descent from the root, named as §6.8 "Root names" says, so an LPID unlinked under its parent's exclusive latch is unreachable afterwards. An overflow chain's pages are reached from their entry instead, under the terms §4.2 states.

### 6.3 Stable root (I-ROOT-STABLE)
A tree's root LPID is fixed when the tree is created and never changes. This departs from E0, which kept the root in a `RwLock<PageId>` and used a T-ROOT restart.
- **Root split:** move the root's cells into two new pages and rewrite the root as an inner page with two children.
- **Root collapse:** when the root is an inner page with one child, copy the child's content into the root and free the child.

**Why.** The tree directory (§6.8) never changes on a split or a collapse, readers need no root re-check, and the root never moves between images.

### 6.4 Split
A leaf that cannot take an insert first runs the **page-local prune** (§10.3). A split happens only if the prune did not free enough room. The prune frees no chain (§10.3), so it reads no page under the inner latches the insert holds.
- **Separator.** The split separator is the shortest byte string `s` with `last(left) < s ≤ first(right)`. Under (b), a split may fall inside a group, so `s` may include ts bytes.
- **Split-point preference.** A leaf split **should** prefer a group boundary within the middle third of the page. That makes more groups page-local, which is what page-local GC can prove things about (§10.3), and a latest-snapshot read of a key then touches one leaf.
- **Prefix truncation.** Inner-node prefix truncation is card A-4d.
- **Atomicity.** A split is all-or-nothing: E0's T-ATOMIC carries over. Every page it needs is reserved before the first mutation, including the MapLeafs of the LPIDs it allocates, and before the prune (§5.6). The insert decides whether it splits from the prune's marks, before the prune mutates: under the leaf's exclusive latch it marks what the prune would delete, with the `w` the prune will use, and splits only if the insert's cell does not fit with the marked entries, and any cell it replaces, counted as gone. A split's pages and allocations are then reserved, or the insert restarts, before the prune's mutation, which applies exactly those marks. An insert that does not split reserves nothing. Mutants SPLIT-RESERVE-AFTER-PRUNE, ALLOC-READ-UNDER-P and SPLIT-RESERVE-ALWAYS (§15 I-LINK-ATOMIC).
  - *The decision is exact.* The prune reads nothing outside its leaf and frees no chain or LPID (§10.3), so its marks are a function of the latched leaf and `w` alone. The leaf stays exclusively latched from the marking to the prune's mutation, so the marks cannot change in between. A restart releases the latch, so the marks are recomputed after the re-descent.
  - *Unconditional reservation is rejected.* An LPID allocation is a mutation (§5.6), so an insert that reserved before it knew it splits would allocate before the prune and, when the prune made room, would have to free an LPID it never linked, in the same gated operation. Every insert into an unsafe leaf would also pin the next free LPID's MapLeaf, and restart on a cold one, for a split that does not happen.
  - *Deciding after the prune is SPLIT-RESERVE-AFTER-PRUNE.* An insert that learns it must split only after its prune has mutated can no longer restart, so a non-resident page or MapLeaf would be read under `P`'s exclusive latch.

### 6.5 Pack step: merge and redistribution (I-PACK)
`pack(P, L, R)`. **Preconditions:**
- `P` is an inner page;
- `L` and `R` are adjacent children of `P`, with `R` immediately right of `L`;
- all three are pinned and resident (pin-then-validate, §5.3), and exclusively latched in the order `P`, `L`, `R`;
- the step is one gated operation.

With `F_PACK = 0.85`, the step does one of three things:
1. **Merge.** If `used(L) + used(R) ≤ F_PACK·U` (for inner pages, plus the cell of the separator between them, which is pulled down into `L` first), move all of `R`'s cells into `L`. Remove `R`'s separator and child pointer from `P`, then free `R`'s LPID (§4.2). A merge only shrinks `P`, so it can always run.
2. **Redistribute.** Otherwise, if the **first cell that would enter `L`** fits with `used(L)` staying `≤ F_PACK·U`, move the **longest prefix** that keeps `used(L) ≤ F_PACK·U`.
   - **Leaves.** The cell that enters `L` is `R`'s first cell, and the move is a prefix of `R`'s cells. Replace the separator in `P` with a new shortest separator.
   - **Inner pages (a rotation through `P`).** The cell that enters `L` is **`P`'s separator** between `L` and `R`, not `R`'s first cell. It comes down with `R`'s first child pointer. Moving `k` children brings down `P`'s separator and `R`'s first `k − 1` separators, together with `R`'s first `k` child pointers. `R`'s `k`-th separator rises into `P` in its place. The fits test is on `P`'s separator.
3. **Full.** Otherwise, do nothing. The cell that would enter `L` first (`R`'s first cell for a leaf, `P`'s separator for an inner page) is at most `C_max` and does not fit, so `L` already holds more than `F_PACK·U − C_max` (§11).

**Parent-overflow skip.** A redistribute whose new separator is longer than the old one by more than `P`'s free space is **skipped** instead, and nothing changes. A pack step never splits. The caller counts the skip (the sweep reports it, §10.4), and I-SPACE excludes the skipped `L` (§11). The next sweep retries it. A skip needs `P` within one separator's growth of full, so it is rare once merges have removed separators from `P`. Mutant: SKIP-UNCOUNTED, a skip left out of the count. It is caught by forcing a skip (a nearly full parent and long separators) and asserting I-SPACE with the reported count.

**`F_PACK`** is an engine constant, not a format field. It is below 1 so that a packed leaf can absorb new versions without splitting at once. Under (b), every update inserts a version into its key's leaf.

**Correctness.** The step preserves the multiset of entries, I-TREE and every key's subtree bound. It is atomic with respect to readers (latches) and to images (the gate).

**Who calls pack.**
- **The GC sweep** packs systematically, left to right (§10.4).
- **A delete that descends from the root under latch coupling** (no caller in 2.0: DropTree's last step, the only directory removal, does not pack, §6.8) may pack the affected leaf with its right neighbour, or its left neighbour if it is the rightmost child, when `used < U/4`, while it still holds the parent. The two leaves are latched left to right: to pack with the left neighbour, it releases the leaf and, still holding the parent exclusively, latches the neighbour and then the leaf.
- **A delete made while holding only a leaf** (the page-local prune, §10.3, including at eviction) never packs. Taking the parent's latch from below would break I-LATCH. It leaves the page for the next sweep.
- **Inner pages** whose `used` falls below `U/4` after a pack removed a child are packed under their parent, by a **re-descent** that latches the parent first. Nothing ever latches a parent after its child (I-LATCH). After a delete, they pack the same way as the leaf (the right neighbour, or the left one for a rightmost child). **The sweep uses its own rule instead** (§10.4 step 5): an inner page packs only with its right neighbour, and a junction walk follows. A left pack would make the left neighbour's former last child, which was exempt and may be underfull, a middle child beside a page the sweep has already passed, and nothing would pack the two again, so I-SPACE would fail (§11).
- **The root** collapses when it is an inner page with one child (§6.3).

Under N3 = (b), **logical deletes are tombstone inserts** (§7). Only GC, DropTree, overflow freeing and provisional-entry settling (C-T0 v8 §2.4) ever remove entries or pages.

### 6.6 Cursors (I-CURSOR)
- **Within a call.** A cursor may hold its root-to-leaf stack shared, or less, while one storage call runs; card A-4e decides how much. Recommended: release the ancestors once the leaf is latched, and reach the next leaf by re-descent.
- **Parking.** Before a storage call returns, a cursor parks: it releases every latch and every pin, and keeps only its tree id, its registered snapshot `S` and its parked position. It resumes through a fresh, validated root name (§6.8) and a re-descent from the root at its parked position, never by a remembered LPID. A parked cursor holds nothing, so an open cursor adds nothing to §5.4's minimum and delays no writer. It keeps no pin, because a pin kept across user-paced calls would make §5.4's minimum depend on the number of open cursors, and with MVCC a pin buys no correctness.
- **Why parking is exact.** A cursor resumed at its registered `S` returns exactly what a latch-held scan would: entries at or below `S` are stable (C-T0 v8 §3.6, I-GC-READEQ); cursors and portals keep `S` registered until they close (C-T0 v8 §3.6); chains are read under the registration (§6.7); and a parked cursor's tree cannot be dropped, since the drop needs `W > D` while the cursor's `S < D` holds `W` (C-T6 §7.5). Under SER, C-T0 v8 §8.1 registers a scan's SIREAD over its full bound and checks the lock table before the tree read, so each key's tree read still follows `L`, and §8.3's case analysis is unchanged by parking.
- **The parked position.** Either *after group `k`*, which resumes at `upper(k)`, or *inside group `k`, after entry key `x`*, which carries the group's state: the head's candidate version, if any (§8, "Candidate rule"), and whether the newest version `≤ S` was already found. It mirrors the sweep token (§10.4). A resume inside a group continues after `x` with that state, so a finalize that converts the head meanwhile changes nothing it returns.
- **A bound per call.** Card A-4e bounds the entries a call examines, skipped versions included (§8.3's seeks). At the bound a cursor parks. A point read (§8.1) or a newest probe (§8.2), which also step over versions above `S` under SER (C-T0 v8 §8.1), releases its latches at the bound and re-descends at its parked position with the same carried state, inside the same call. So no latch hold exceeds the bound (§5.3, "The fairness bound").
- **Batches.** A batch is copied out under the leaf latch: at most A-4e's bound per open cursor, held outside the pool (§5.7). Chain values stay references and are read after the latch is released, under the registered `S` (§6.7, "Reading without the leaf latch"). A batch holds chain references only for a cursor that holds a registration, which every cursor does, and never holds own-transaction entries, whose layer comes from the write set (C-T0 v8 §4 step 1). A cursor's batch buffer is freed when it closes, a close by `ROLLBACK TO` included (C-T0 v8 §5.5).
- **Reverse and scroll.** No sibling links exist, so a reverse cursor reaches the previous leaf by releasing its latches and re-descending, never by latching leftward while it holds a leaf. A parked position holds in both directions: a resume in the other direction (a SCROLL cursor's FETCH PRIOR) starts at the same boundary and skips and repeats no key against the oracle in that direction; card A-4e maps SQL's cursor positions onto it.
- **Safety.** This is what keeps merges, frees and LPID reuse safe for scans that do not hold latches throughout.

Iterators are **streaming**: a range scan never materialises its range. The spike's whole-range materialisation is a spike limit (`verdicts/X-2.md` non-blocking finding 3), not a behaviour.

### 6.7 Overflow chains
An entry whose value exceeds `V_INLINE` stores a reference `(len u64, first LPID)` in its value, with tag 4 or 5 (§7.3). The chain is a list of Overflow pages.
- **Immutability.** Chains are immutable once written (versions are immutable).
- **Building.** An insert descends, latches its leaf and makes room for the reference, any split included, before it maps the chain's first page. So its restarts (§5.6) all lie before the build, and no restart strands a mapped chain. A chain is mapped in the gated operation that inserts its entry, as it is freed with its entry (I-LINK-ATOMIC, §5.6). The build holds only the leaf: once the insert has made room, any split's separators installed, it releases `P` and every other ancestor, and only then allocates the chain's LPIDs and maps its pages, reading a non-resident MapLeaf in place (§5.6). It does not reserve them before its first mutation instead, because a chain at the maximum value size may need more MapLeafs than §5.4's per-thread count allows. An insert that both builds a chain and frees a replaced one (Freeing, below) releases `P` once, before both. Mutant CHAIN-BUILD-UNDER-P (§15).
- **Freeing.** A chain is freed **in the same gated operation** that deletes its entry (§10), or that replaces its entry's value: a provisional rewrite (C-T0 v8 §2.4) or a superseding replay op (§14), or that settles an aborted or dead tag-8 entry inside a writer's operation on `k` (settlers (b) and (c), C-T0 v8 §2.4), unless the new value is the same chain. **Exception:** a conversion of a tag-8 provisional entry (C-T0 v8 §2.4) moves the chain to the new tag-4 or tag-5 entry and deletes the provisional entry without freeing it. Both happen in one gated operation, so no image holds two references to one chain. The free holds only the entry's leaf in every gated operation outside the sweep that frees a chain: an insert that replaces or settles a chain-bearing entry, settler (a)'s abort cleanup (C-T0 v8 §7.1), `T`'s delete on `ROLLBACK TO` (C-T0 v8 §5.5), and a replayed `Delete{revert}` of a tag-8 entry (§14). An insert releases `P` and every other ancestor once any split has installed its separators, and only then replaces the entry and frees the old chain. Such a delete never packs: it releases `P` and every other ancestor before it deletes, so it is a delete made while holding only a leaf (§6.5). It loses no pack by that: coupling kept `P` only because the leaf had no room for a maximum-size cell (§6.2), so the leaf is more than three quarters full (§3.2), and one delete leaves it above `U/4`. Each reads the chain's pages and their MapLeafs in place (§5.6), as the sweep's chain deletes do (§10.4). It cannot pin them before its first mutation instead, because learning a chain's LPIDs reads its pages, and a chain may be longer than §5.4's per-thread count. The free stays in the replacing operation: a separate operation would leave the old chain mapped with no reference at a cut (I-LINK-ATOMIC). Mutant REPLACE-FREE-UNDER-P (§15). Mutant DELETE-FREE-UNDER-P (§15). A chain build or free therefore holds the flip for the chain's length. A-5 measures the flip hold per gated operation, not only per chain: a chain's build and free at the maximum value size (`ROW_LIMIT` for the row layer, C-T6-rehost §4.3), at every allowed page size; a replace, which builds one chain and frees another in one gated operation; and, with cold (non-resident) chains, the gated operations of a GC sweep and of a DropTree walk over a toast-region leaf and over a leaf of maximum-size values (§6.8, §10.4). If any is too long, A-5 proposes a resumable build or free, which is an edit of this file (§19 item 6).
- **Reading without the leaf latch.** A reader that holds a registered snapshot `S ≥ W` may read the chain after releasing the leaf latch. GC never deletes a version that is the newest `≤ S` for any registered `S` (I-GC-READEQ), so a version that a registered snapshot reads cannot lose its chain while the reader holds that registration.

### 6.8 Tree directory
- **What it is.** A system B+tree, unversioned, with a stable root at `dir_root`. Its entries map `tree_id (u64 BE) → (root LPID u64, kind u8, state u8)`.
- **Tree ids** are C-T0 §10 storage ids, never reused. The WAL names trees only by tree id (§12).
- **States:** `live` or `dropping`.
- **DDL visibility** lives in the versioned catalog rows (C-T5), not here.
- **The root table.** An in-memory map `tree_id → (root LPID, kind, state)`, one entry per directory entry, never persisted. It is a name, not a reference: no image holds it, and I-LINK-ATOMIC's reference list is unchanged. It is built at open (§14 step 2) and changed only by a directory change, inside that change's gated operation, while it holds the directory leaf exclusively. It has no generation counter: tree ids are never reused and an entry only goes `live` → `dropping` → absent, so comparing (root LPID, kind, state) is exact, and no unrelated directory change makes a reader retry. A root-name read performs no store to memory common to all trees, so the table adds no shared cache line to an ordinary operation; A-7b chooses the mechanism. If it is lock-based, its lock is a leaf lock: nothing is acquired under it, and it ranks like the page table and the free-set lock (§4.2).

**CreateTree(id)** descends the directory, then allocates a root leaf and inserts the directory entry, in one gated operation (I-LINK-ATOMIC, §5.6). Its restarts (§5.6) all lie before the allocation. It allocates by the restart arm of §5.6's allocation rule: it takes the root's LPID, with those of any directory split (§6.4), in one hold of the free-set lock only if each one's MapLeaf is resident, pins them before the lock is released, and otherwise restarts; no mutation precedes the allocation, so that restart is legal. It is idempotent: if the id already exists, nothing happens. It installs the tree's root-table entry in the same gated operation, after the root page is initialised and while it holds the new root's latch exclusively. The entry's bytes are charged to `R_file` before the gated operation: after the open, moved from `S`, or the DDL statement fails there with 53200; for a CreateTree that recovery replays, counted by §14 step 3b before the open sizes its pool (R-1-memory §5.2; card S-LAMBDA).

**DropTree(id)**
1. Set the entry's state to `dropping`, in one directory mutation. The same mutation sets the root-table entry's state and, when it changes the entry from `live`, raises `gc_w_used` to its `w`: for a DropTree logged in a record, the `w` its op carries (§12.3), the watermark C-T0 v8 published as the drop-cleanup job read it once it found the drop allowed, the same value in normal operation and when recovery replays the DropTree; for a DropTree that runs with no logged op (C-T6-rehost §5.3), the watermark C-T0 v8 published at the time of the mutation (§10.5 "Drops", §14 step 4). This step carries the raise, not the walk's first step, because an image can hold the mark before any page is freed, and the walk resumes at open (§14 step 6).
2. Free the tree's pages bottom-up in bounded gated steps, each reached by a fresh descent from the tree's root: a walk step names the root through the root table, accepting `dropping`, validates it under the root's latch (root names, below), and latches no directory page, since it changes no directory entry:
   - **Chains first.** A chain-bearing entry is deleted with its chain (§6.7) in its own gated operation, holding only its leaf, before the step that unlinks that leaf. So one gated operation frees at most one chain and holds the flip for that chain's length (§6.7), no inner page is latched across the chain's reads (§5.3), and no later step frees a chain. A leaf this empties stays legal until it is unlinked (§6.1).
   - **Unlink.** A step unlinks a child that holds no chain reference from its parent and frees it, in one gated operation. A parent the step empties is freed in that same operation and unlinked from its own parent. Every page it latches, and the MapLeaf of every LPID it frees, is pinned before its first mutation (§5.6), so it reads no page after that mutation and frees at most one path of pages.

   A root's parent is its directory entry, so an unlink step that would empty the root changes nothing: it releases its latches and the gate and runs as the last step (step 3), which latches from the directory leaf down. That restart is legal, since the step has mutated nothing (§5.6).
3. **The last step frees the root and removes the directory entry in one gated operation.** A leaf root's chains went first (step 2), so it frees no chain.
   - **Before its first mutation,** it descends the directory and pins the directory leaf, the root and every page it empties (at most height + 1 pages, within §5.4's path term), and the MapLeaf of every LPID it frees (§5.4's third per-thread term, not `R_MAP`). A non-resident page restarts it, and that restart releases the gate harmlessly (§5.6). So the flip waits for at most one directory descent over pinned pages, and no page read.
   - **In one hold,** it latches top-down from the directory leaf to the pages it frees, removes the entry first, and frees the root and any emptied pages after it, top-down (I-LINK-ATOMIC). It removes the tree's root-table entry after the directory entry, while it holds the root's exclusive latch, and before it frees the root; "Root names" below depends on this order. Once the entry is gone, the pages are unreachable, so a concurrent allocation may take a freed LPID at once (§4.2). The last step does not pack: its pins cover the directory leaf and the tree path only (§5.4), and latching the directory neighbour after the root would break I-LATCH. No directory leaf is packed in 2.0, and no directory page is freed, so a leaf that drops leave under-full or empty keeps its page for the store's life. Tree ids only ascend and are never reused (C-T6-rehost §6), so a tree id allocated after a drop sorts above every id retired by then: a CreateTree of such an id lands at or near the directory's right edge and does not refill a leaf further left. The wasted space is therefore bounded by the number of trees ever created, not the number that exist, and 2.0 has no step that reclaims it; §19 T6 records the cost and its revisit trigger.

**One walk per tree.** At most one walk runs per tree: a DropTree of a tree whose walk is running does nothing. The claim on the walk is in memory and is not persisted, so a crash loses it and §14 step 6 starts each `dropping` entry's walk once.

**Root names.** A thread names a tree's root only through the root table, and validates the name under the root's latch. (1) It reads the tree's entry; if the entry is absent, or `dropping` where its operation needs `live`, it stops without naming a page. (2) It pins the entry's root LPID and latches the root frame, blocking on its fair latch (§5.3), holding no other latch except, for a directory change, its directory leaf. DropTree's last step may therefore block on the root while it holds the directory leaf exclusively: only directory changes can queue behind it, and the root's only possible holders then hold it briefly and wait on no directory latch, namely a stale-name thread's first latch, §13 step 2's data-phase latch on the root's frame, under which it takes at most a current MapLeaf's latch (§13 step 2), and an evictor's victim latch, taken without blocking, under which it takes only map latches (§5.2), since sweeps skip `dropping` trees, cleanup deletes stop on `dropping`, and one walk runs per tree. A pin on this path that finds the LPID unmapped (§5.4 "What a pin finds": no frame holds it, and its current map entry has slot 0) holds no frame, and the thread re-reads the entry at once: if the entry has changed, the result is a retry from (1), never XX001 or poison; if it is unchanged, the result is XX001 (§9.2 says when that poisons the store). A correct store cannot produce the second case: CreateTree maps the root before it installs the entry, and DropTree's last step removes the entry before it frees the root, so a pin that finds an entry's root unmapped is ordered after that free and the re-read sees the removal. The latch may land on a page that is no longer the tree's root, even another tree's page: until (3) the thread reads nothing of the page but its header `id` (§3.1), which it checks against the LPID. A mismatch is not acted on before (3), since a stale name can latch a frame whose new owner has not yet initialised it: if the re-read finds the entry changed, the mismatch is a retry from (1), and only a mismatch under an unchanged entry is XX001. (3) Under that latch it reads the entry again: if the entry is unchanged, the name is valid for this latch hold; otherwise it releases the latch and the pin and starts again at (1). (4) It keeps no root LPID across gated operations, restarts or a cursor's release (§6.6). Validation is exact. DropTree's last step holds the root's exclusive latch from before it removes the root-table entry until after it frees the root, and the root's LPID can be reused only after that free (§4.2). If the thread latches the frame DropTree latched and the grant comes after DropTree releases it, the release is ordered after the removal and before the grant, so the re-read sees the removal; if the grant comes first, DropTree cannot latch the root until the thread releases it, so the LPID is still the tree's root throughout the hold. If the LPID has been reused, in that frame or another, the thread's pin finds the reuse's mapping and is ordered after the free: the free and the reusing allocation change the LPID's bit under the free-set lock (§4.2), and the pin acquires the mapping the reuse installed, so the re-read, after the pin, sees the removal. Splits and collapses never change an entry (I-ROOT-STABLE), so an entry changes only on CreateTree, DropTree and a state change. Only an operation that changes the directory (CreateTree, DropTree, a state change) latches a directory page; it couples down the directory as §6.2's writers do. So C-T6 §7.5's checks are not check-then-act: a sweep step or a cleanup delete reads the entry's state and the root's name in one table read and reads both again under the root's latch, a `dropping` or absent entry stops it before it names a page, and no root name survives into a later step, where §4.2's reuse could have changed what it names. Keeping a root name across operations would be an edit of this rule (§19 T5).

At open, every `dropping` entry's walk resumes (the B-6 boot job; C-B1 "drop cleanup = freeing pages"). DropTree is idempotent: dropping an absent id does nothing. Every image therefore holds either the entry with its root mapped, or neither. A crash mid-walk leaks no page and names no freed LPID (I-LPID-EXACT, I-LINK-ATOMIC). At open, an unmapped root of a `dropping` entry is corruption like any other unmapped reference (XX001); §14 step 6 needs no other arm.

**When the drop runs** (after `W` passes the DDL) is C-T0 v8's rule (v7.8 §9.2).

**I-LPID-EXACT** holds of every image, crash images included; "quiescent" (§15) describes amcheck's reading, not an exemption for crash images. The mapped LPIDs of an image are exactly:
- the pages reachable from the directory roots, a `dropping` entry's root among them, including overflow chains (each referenced by exactly one entry);
- the directory's own pages.

Mutants (§15 I-LINK-ATOMIC): **DROP-ENTRY-SPLIT** (the root free and the entry removal in two gated operations), **DROP-UNLINK-SPLIT** (a child freed one gated step before its unlink), **CREATE-TREE-SPLIT** (the root mapped one gated step before the entry), **DROP-RESTART-MID-OP** (the last step mutates, meets a non-resident directory page, and releases the gate on the restart), **DROP-RESTART-AFTER-REMOVE** (the last step removes the entry, meets the evicted root or MapLeaf, and restarts, releasing the gate), **UNLINK-RESTART-AFTER-UNLINK** (an unlink step unlinks a child, meets the evicted MapLeaf of its LPID, and restarts), **DROP-LAST-STEP-PACKS** (the last step packs the directory leaf with its neighbour), **DROP-FREE-BEFORE-REMOVE** (inside the hold, the root is freed before the entry is removed), **DROP-LEAF-CHAINS** (a step frees a leaf's chains with the leaf), **DROP-SECOND-WALK** (a DropTree starts a second walk beside a running one), **ROOT-NAME-KEPT** (a sweep keeps a root LPID across gated operations and latches it after its reuse), **ROOT-NAME-UNVALIDATED** (no re-read under the root's latch), **ROOT-UNPUBLISH-LATE** (DropTree removes the root-table entry after releasing the root's latch), **ROOT-PIN-UNMAPPED-FATAL** (a pin of an unmapped LPID on the root-name path raises XX001 without re-reading the entry), **ROOT-DECODE-BEFORE-VALIDATE** (the thread reads the latched page as the tree's root before the re-read), **ROOT-TABLE-GLOBAL-GEN** (validation compares a table-wide counter that every directory change bumps), **ROOT-HEADER-MISMATCH-FATAL** (a header `id` mismatch before the re-read raises XX001), **ROOT-PIN-UNMAPPED-SPIN** (an unmapped pin retries from (1) even when the re-read finds the entry unchanged). Mutants (§15 I-LATCH): **DIR-LATCH-ON-READ** (an operation that does not change the directory latches a directory page).

---

## 7. Versioned entries (N3 = (b))

Decided by X-4 (PLAN-v3 §4, "E1′ decision") from the e1-bench evidence (§16). The layout is X-2's (`spikes/e1-arm-b/src/encode.rs`), pinned by its proptest. S-1 owns it (decision block, "Consequences").

### 7.1 Timestamps
- **Reserved values.** `ts = 0` and `ts = u64::MAX` are never commit timestamps. C-T0 already reserves `Ts::ZERO`. The suffix `u64::MAX` marks a **provisional entry** (§7.3 tags 6–8; C-T0 v8 §2.4): `entry_key(k, u64::MAX) = seek_newest(k)`, the head of k's group.
- **Uniqueness.** One commit writes at most one version per user key per tree, at its own unique ts, so **entry keys are unique** (I-ENTRY-UNIQUE). A tree holds at most one provisional entry per user key.

### 7.2 Entry key
```
entry_key(k, ts) = escape(k) ‖ 0x00 0x00 ‖ be64(!ts)
escape(k)        = k with every 0x00 byte replaced by 0x00 0xFF
prefix(k)        = escape(k) ‖ 0x00 0x00                 # every entry of k starts with it; no other key's entry does
upper(k)         = prefix(k) ‖ 0xFF × 9                  # exclusive upper bound of k's group
seek(k, S)       = entry_key(k, S)                        # first entry ≥ it in k's group = newest version ≤ S
seek_newest(k)   = entry_key(k, u64::MAX) = prefix(k) ‖ 0x00 × 8
```
Reference: `encode.rs:67` `prefix_of`, `:84` `encode_key`, `:94` `encode_seek_newest`, `:102` `encode_key_upper`, `:120` `decode_key`.

**I-ENC-ORDER.** For any user keys and timestamps, the memcmp order of entry keys is (user key ascending, ts **descending**). Why:
- Escaping is a byte homomorphism, and it emits `0x00` only as the first byte of a `0x00 0xFF` pair, so `0x00 0x00` occurs in an entry key only as the terminator.
- For `k1 < k2`, the whole group of `k1` sorts below the whole group of `k2`. If `k1` is a proper prefix of `k2`, the terminator's second `0x00` loses against the `0xFF` follower of an escaped zero, or against any non-zero byte (`encode.rs` module docs).
- `!ts` big-endian inverts ts order within a group.
- `upper(k)` is above every entry of `k`, since `0xFF × 9` is longer than any 8-byte suffix, and below every entry of any `k' > k`, by the same argument.

**Decoding.** An entry key is split **from the right**: the last 8 bytes are `!ts`, the 2 before them must be `0x00 0x00`, and the rest must unescape cleanly (every `0x00` is followed by `0xFF`). Decoding never needs the schema. A key that does not decode is XX001 (I-ENC-ROUNDTRIP). `decode(entry_key(k, ts)) = (k, ts)` for every `k` and `ts`.

**Prefix ranges.** A user-key prefix `p` needs no extra rule. It is the user range `[p, succ(p))`, and `prefix()` maps it exactly. Every key that extends `p` encodes at or above `prefix(p)`: escaping preserves concatenation, and an escaped continuation begins with a non-zero byte or with `0x00 0xFF`, both of which exceed the terminator.

**Size.** `|entry_key| = |k| + zeros(k) + 10`. It must be `≤ K_MAX` (§3.2).

### 7.3 Entry value
One tag byte, then a tag-specific body:

| tag | meaning | body | C-T0 v7.8 §2.2 origin |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | live value | value bytes | header 0x00 |
| 1 | tombstone | none | header 0x02 |
| 2 | live value, `key_changed` | value bytes | header 0x01 |
| 3 | moved-tombstone (an UPDATE moved the row to a new primary key) | none | header 0x03 |
| 4 | live value in an overflow chain | `len u64, first LPID u64` | (overflow, §6.7) |
| 5 | live value in an overflow chain, `key_changed` | as tag 4 | (overflow) |
| 6 | provisional live value | `txn (u32 boot id, u64 n), seq u32, data_seq u32, flags u8` (bit 0 = `key_changed`), then the value bytes | (C-T0 v8 §2.4) |
| 7 | provisional tombstone | `txn, seq, data_seq, flags` (bit 0 = `moved`) | (C-T0 v8 §2.4) |
| 8 | provisional live value in an overflow chain | `txn, seq, data_seq, flags, len u64, first LPID u64` | (C-T0 v8 §2.4) |
| 9–255 | **reserved** | — | — |

**Why these tags.**
- Tags 0 and 1 are the spike's (`encode.rs:50`, `:53`).
- Tags 2 and 3 carry the C-T0 behaviour that needs them (the KEY SHARE rule, and moved rows under RC EPQ), whose physical clauses C-T0 v8 replaces with references to this file (PLAN-v3 §3.4). C-T0 v8 owns what they mean.
- Tags 6–8 are C-T0 v8 §2.4's provisional entries, assigned by card S-2. They occur only with the ts suffix `u64::MAX`.
- *Tombstone* below means tag 1 or 3; *live* means tags 0, 2, 4 and 5. *Ordinary* means tags 0–5.

**I-TAG-RESERVED.** The following are XX001 on every read path and are never served:
- a reserved tag (9–255);
- an entry whose suffix encodes ts `0`;
- an ordinary tag (0–5) with the suffix `u64::MAX`, or a provisional tag (6–8) with any other suffix.

### 7.4 Size accounting
An inline version costs `|k| + zeros(k) + 10` key bytes, plus `1 +` value bytes, plus the cell and slot overhead. The escape's cost is measured: W1's 8-byte keys `i.to_be_bytes()` for `i < 2^20` (`spikes/e1-bench/src/util.rs:67`) always carry five `0x00` bytes, and W1 measured **124.07 B applied per txn** for a 100-byte value. That is 101 value bytes + 10 + 13.07 escaped key bytes (`spikes/e1-bench/README.md` §W1, "(b) B/txn"). The escape cost is accepted as part of X-2's pinned layout; leaf prefix compression is an open mitigation (§19 Q2).

---

## 8. Reads

Every read below runs on one tree. The write set merge (a transaction's own writes) is S-2's (C-T0 v8, I-HALLOWEEN); this section is the committed-data half.

**Provisional heads (C-T0 v8 §2.4, §4).** A group's first entry may be a provisional entry `e` (§7.3 tags 6–8). Every read in §8.1–§8.3 takes from C-T0 a **resolver** for `e`. The resolver returns *ignore* (dead, `Open`, `Aborted`, or the reader's own entry) or *a version at `c`*. It is called **while the leaf holding `e` is still latched** (C-T0 v8 I-PROV-LOOKUP). Then:
- **Head first.** The head is examined before the group's ordinary entries (I-PROV-READ). A conversion inserts `k@c` before it deletes `e` (C-T0 v8 I-PROV-CONVERT-ORDER), so a reader that finds no `e` finds `k@c`. Mutant ORDINARY-FIRST.
- **Candidate rule.** If the resolver yields a version at `c`, the read's answer is the newer of `(c, e's data)` and the ordinary answer below. Both may be at `c`, during a conversion; they are then the same version.
- **Fast path.** When the caller states that no current-boot provisional entry exists in the tree (C-T0 v8 §4's per-tree count is 0), §8.1 skips the head check and is exactly one descent. "Current-boot" means `txn.boot` equals C-T0 v8's boot id (§12.2), never the checkpoint epoch `e` (§1), which changes at every checkpoint. Dead entries never affect §8.1 at `S < u64::MAX`, because `seek(k, S)` lands after them.
- **Provisional data** is decoded like the matching ordinary tag (6 like 0 or 2, 7 like 1 or 3, 8 like 4 or 5, by the flags).

### 8.1 Point read at S (I-SEEK)
`get(k, S)`:
1. descend once to `seek(k, S)`;
2. take the first entry `e ≥ seek(k, S)`. If the seek lands at the end of a leaf, the cursor stack moves to the next leaf (§6.6);
3. if `e` does not start with `prefix(k)`: no version of `k` is `≤ S`, so the result is **absent**;
4. otherwise decode `e`'s value: live → the value (reading the chain for tags 4 and 5); tombstone → **absent**; reserved → XX001. A provisional head is handled by the rule above, before this step.

There is one descent and no per-version stepping (`engine.rs:238` `version_at`). Mutants: TS-ASC, SEEK-NO-PREFIX-CHECK, TOMBSTONE-AS-VALUE.

### 8.2 Newest probe (I-NEWEST)
`newest(k)` = the first entry at or after `seek_newest(k)` that starts with `prefix(k)`, as `(ts, live value | tombstone)`, or none (`engine.rs:266`). If that entry is provisional, the provisional-head rule applies: `newest(k)` is the newer of its resolved version (if any) and the next entry of the group.
- **Precondition, owned by S-2.** The caller holds `k`'s lock, and locks are released only after the holder's commit is visible (C-COMMIT-ORDER). Then no apply to `k` is in flight, and the newest entry is the newest committed version. The one exception is a conversion of an already-visible provisional entry, which the head-first rule makes unobservable (C-T0 v8 §5.1).
- **Who uses it.** S-2's RR conflict rule (40001 iff `ts > S`, checked **after** the lock wait, `engine.rs:430–435`) and its liveness rule (a tombstone is not live, `engine.rs:465`).
- **GC cannot change the answer.** GC never deletes the newest entry of a group, except a tombstone with nothing older (§10.2). For both rules, that tombstone and no entry give the same answer (`verdicts/X-2.md`, "GC safety holds").

### 8.3 Range scan at S (I-SCAN)
`scan(lo, hi, S)` yields `(k, value)` for each user key `k` in the range, in key order: the newest version `≤ S`, omitted if it is a tombstone. Encoded bounds:

| user bound | encoded bound |
|---|---|
| `lo` included | `prefix(lo)` (inclusive) |
| `lo` excluded | `upper(lo)` (inclusive) |
| `lo` unbounded | the empty string |
| `hi` excluded | `prefix(hi)` (exclusive) |
| `hi` included | `upper(hi)` (exclusive) |
| `hi` unbounded | the tree end |

Using `upper(hi)` for an excluded `hi` is the SCAN-HI-INCL mutant (`verdicts/X-2.md`). Using `prefix(hi)` for an included one drops `hi`'s group.

**Per group:**
0. apply the provisional-head rule to the group's head;
1. skip the entries with `ts > S`;
2. take the first entry with `ts ≤ S`;
3. continue at `upper(k)`.

Steps 1 and 3 **should be seeks** (a binary search within the leaf, or a re-descent) rather than entry-by-entry stepping, once the remaining group exceeds a small threshold that A-4e sets. This is the version-skipping mitigation named in the decision block for the latest-snapshot scan cost (§16, §19 trigger T1). Reference: `engine.rs:301` (stepping, and materialising; both are spike limits).

**Reverse scans** return exactly the forward result in reverse order. Within each group, they apply the forward rule (A-4e designs how).

**Concurrency.** A scan interleaved with GC steps sees, for each key, a state that I-GC-READEQ makes read-equivalent, so its results equal those of a scan with no GC.

---

## 9. Apply (the storage side of commit)

### 9.1 What apply does
- Apply turns each write of a commit at `ts = c` into **one entry insert**: `Put` → `entry_key(k, c)` with tag 0, 2, 4 or 5; `Delete` → tag 1 or 3. Each insert is a gated operation.
- **Order.** A commit inserts its entries in ascending user-key order, tree by tree (`engine.rs:495`).
- **When.** C-T0 v8 decides when apply runs, and how `visible_ts` and lock release follow it (C-T0 v8 §3.1). A large transaction does not apply at commit: its provisional entries become readable at its flip, and its finalize converts them (C-T0 v8 §3.2).

**I-ENTRY-UNIQUE / I-REPLAY-IDEM.** Inserting an ordinary entry whose key already exists:
- with an **identical** value: a no-op. This happens only in replay (§14) and in a provisional conversion that another settler already made (C-T0 v8 §2.4). *Identical* means the same tag and the same value bytes. For tags 4 and 5 it means the same `len` and the same chain bytes: the chain is compared, not its LPID. A chain built for the re-insert is then freed in the same gated operation;
- with a **different** value: XX001. Two commits cannot share a ts. **Exception:** the replay of a chunked transaction's superseded ops (§14).

**Provisional entries** are inserted, replaced and deleted only through C-T0 v8 §2.4: by their owner, or by a settler. Replacing a provisional entry's value is not an I-ENTRY-UNIQUE conflict.

### 9.2 Failure (I-FAIL-STOP)
- **No logical failures.** Apply cannot fail logically: key size is checked when the write enters the write set (§3.2), and entries are unique. So an error of another class raised inside one of the contexts below, such as 54000, 55000 or 22023, is an engine invariant violation (XX000, below; card S-MU).
- **Physical failures poison the store.** An I/O error (58030), 53100, 53200 or XX001 poisons the store when it is raised inside any of these:
  - a gated operation (§5.6): an apply insert, a tree mutation or SMO, a pack step, a GC mutation, an LPID free, a directory change, a dirty eviction, a materialise or conversion step (C-T0 v8 §3.2);
  - a checkpoint step (§13);
  - a step of a background store job (the drop walk, §6.8; the GC sweep, §10.4);
  - a WAL write or `sync_data`, a segment's creation, preallocation or recycling rename and its `wal/` directory sync (§12.1, §12.4, §12.5), and recovery's step-5 truncation (§14).

  It does so whether or not the operation has mutated a page yet. An XX001 is a failed integrity check, a check of what the device returned: a read whose bytes fail §3.3, a referenced LPID that a pin finds unmapped (§5.4 "What a pin finds", §6.8 "Root names"), or another check that damaged data from the device can fail, such as §9.1's re-insert with a different value. Like 58030, it is a physical failure.
  - *Why even before a mutation.* An apply cannot be skipped: an XX001 on a page it must descend through leaves its commit unappliable, and nothing may skip a commit (C-T0 v8 §3.1). An SMO cannot be undone (no partial unwinding, below). Any other gated step that has mutated nothing yet (GC, pack, drop walk, materialise) would otherwise be retried forever by its job against the same unreadable page, holding back `W`, the drop or the finalize. That is the livelock this rule removes (below).
  - *Why the WAL path.* A failed WAL `sync_data` is never retried as if its bytes were safe (PostgreSQL's 2018 fsync-retry lesson). I-ACK-PREFIX's waiters (§12.4) cannot pass the failed batch, so without the poison they would wait forever. And §14.1 already treats a WAL append's 53100 as a stopped store.
- **Engine invariant violations** (cards S-THETA and S-IOTA). A check that a correct engine never fails, whatever the device returned, fails with **XX000**, internal error (detail: the check that failed), never XX001: it reports a bug in the engine, or memory gone bad, and says nothing against the data on the device (the owner's decision of 2026-11-20, PLAN-v3 §10 item 22). Such checks are of the engine's own bookkeeping (a frame's state or pin count and the page table, §5.1 and §5.4; a latch's or a lock's state, §5.3 and §4.2; C-T0 v8 §2.4's owner lookup and settle table, whose failures C-T0 already makes poison the store) and of what the engine made itself and is about to write: a page that a dirty eviction has sealed (§5.2), and the records that creation and §13 step 4 check before they write them, with step 4's `wal_ckpt_end ≤ durable_end` and `gc_w ≤ T` (§2.2, §13, §10.5). A check of what the device returned, a page's bytes (§3.1, §3.3), an entry they hold (§5.4, §6.8), a header record (§2.2) or a WAL record (§12.5), is not one, even where step 4 checks the same domains: it fails with XX001, or with 55000 for a header record of a later format version (§2.2). A check that what the device returned can make a correct engine fail is XX001 even where an engine bug can fail it too, so that no damage on the device is ever reported as an engine bug: a boot checkpoint's `gc_w ≤ T` is XX001 for that reason (§10.5). An engine invariant violation poisons the store wherever it is raised, a client read included: what failed is the store's memory, which the next eviction or checkpoint could write, not a page's bytes, which nothing serves. A panic in store code is one too, and its error is XX000. In a build whose panics unwind, the panicking thread makes the mark before it releases any latch, pin or the gate it holds, as item 1 below asks of every failing operation. Unwinding may release first a mutex of the store's that the thread holds (the page table's, the free-set lock, a wait queue's: item 4's internal mutexes), so such a mutex is poisoned as unwinding releases it, in every backend of the store's sync layer, as std's `Mutex` is, and a thread that then takes it acts on nothing it guards: it returns the poison's error, making the mark with XX000 first if none is made, since a mutex that its holder's panic released is an engine invariant violation; only the broadcast (item 4) still takes it, to wake the waiters queued under it (card S-KAPPA). In a build whose panics abort, the process stops (item 6); on wasm32, where a panic traps and the instance outlives the call, the browser handle stops instead (item 6). Mutants **INVARIANT-NOT-POISON** (an engine invariant violation returns its error without the mark), **PANIC-UNPOISONED** (a panic unwinds through store code, releasing its latches, pins and the gate, without the mark), **INVARIANT-AS-CORRUPT** (card S-IOTA: an engine invariant violation, or a panic, fails with XX001, as at draft 7.19) and **MUTEX-POISON-IGNORED** (card S-KAPPA: a thread that takes a mutex released by its holder's panic goes on with what it guards, as std's `PoisonError::into_inner` would let it, or a backend of the sync layer does not poison it) (`t_poison_before_unlatch`, §15 I-FAIL-STOP).
- **Client reads never poison** on damage they meet in what the device returned: an XX001 on a read path that mutates nothing is returned to its caller, and the store stays usable. Such paths are §8's reads and the newest probe, cursors, amcheck, and every read of a read-only open (§14.1). A later gated operation that meets the same page poisons then.
- **How a poisoned store ends.**
  1. **The mark.** The poison mark is one atomic store that takes no lock and holds the error. The failing operation makes it before it releases its first latch, the gate or a pin.
  2. **Every acquisition checks it.** From then on, every call into the store returns the error, reads included, except R-1-memory §8's report and a test build's seams, which serve no page content and change nothing (card S-MU). Every latch acquisition, every `try_latch` (the evictor, §5), every gate and pin acquisition checks the mark, so no reader or evictor walks a structure that the failed operation left half-changed.
  3. **Every wait includes it.** Every blocking wait, in the store and in C-T0 v8, has the mark in its wait predicate, evaluated under that wait's own mutex. The waits are:
     - commit-ack and visibility waiters (C-T0 v8 §3.4);
     - the group-commit queue and every wait on `written_end` or `durable_end` (§12.4), checkpoint step 1's included;
     - gate and pin waiters, among them a thread that holds no gate and waits for a pending flip to run before it loads a page again (§5.4 "The load"): after a poison the flip never runs, so the mark in its predicate and the broadcast are what end its wait (mutant **FLIP-WAITER-UNWOKEN**, card S-KAPPA: the broadcast does not reach that wait's queue; `t_poison_wakeup_race`'s flip-waiter variant, §15 I-FAIL-STOP);
     - background jobs;
     - the lock-table waits (C-T0 v8 §6.3): `wait_for` on a holder, relation and advisory lock waits, deferrable-prefix waits and `wait_for_lockers`.

     A holder is released only once `visible_ts` reaches its commit (C-T0 v8 §3.3), and after a poisoned apply `visible_ts` never advances. So without the mark in its predicate, a lock waiter would park forever. Every wait releases its mutex and sleeps in one step (a condition variable or equivalent) and re-tests its predicate on every wake.
  4. **The broadcast.** After making the mark, the poisoner releases every latch, pin, the gate, the settle stripe and every internal mutex it holds, and **then** broadcasts to every wait queue, taking each queue's mutex in turn. A panicking thread, which cannot know which of its releases is the last, broadcasts after each latch, pin or the gate it releases, or once where store code catches the panic after its last release, so that a broadcast follows its last release (card S-MU). "Every wait queue" includes each live transaction's waiter list. No waker holds a page latch while it takes a waiter's mutex (C-T0 v8 §2.6). A waiter that tested its predicate before the mark is either still holding its mutex, so the broadcast waits for it to sleep, or already asleep on it. So no waiter sleeps through the broadcast. Each woken waiter returns the error. A pending COMMIT returns it too, and its outcome is unknown to the client, as after a crash: it may be durable and recovered.
  5. **Nothing more is written:** no WAL write, no sync, no checkpoint step, no meta.
