The mark takes no lock, so a write whose thread checked the mark before it was made may complete; but every writer checks the mark immediately before it writes, and no write starts once its thread has seen the mark, so nothing completes that a crash at the mark could not have left (card S-MU). On disk the store is a crashed store (§14.1).
  6. **The process stops.** A server exits non-zero after returning the error to its sessions. An embedded or browser handle returns it from every call until it is closed. On wasm32 a panic traps: the call ends without unwinding, so no mark is made and the latches, pins and gate it held stay held, while the instance and its memory outlive it and the caller can call into it again. So the browser binding (card D-5a) treats a trap from any call into the store as this stop: it refuses every later call on that handle, with XX000 and without entering the instance, until the handle is closed, which drops the instance (card S-KAPPA; PLAN-v3 §9 D-5a, whose card states the test, since the binding is not store code). The next open runs recovery (§14), which replays the commit (PLAN-v3 §3.4; `engine.rs` B-FAIL-STOP).
- **Why XX001 is named.** Without it, a gated operation that hits an unreadable referenced page fails alone while the store runs on. In E4, every other task then parked forever behind the service loops (X-5c, MP-LIVELOCK: 41 rounds; 0 with this rule as fill G8).
- **No partial unwinding.** The store never tries to undo a partial apply: under (b), the replay of a partially applied commit is idempotent (§14).

---

## 10. GC (physical)

C-T0 v8 owns the watermark `W`: how it is computed, published and floored after a restart (§17). This section owns what may be removed given `W`, in what order, and how the space comes back. The rule is X-2's, as corrected by X-2-F1 and adopted in the decision block's "Consequences".

### 10.1 Precondition (A-GC-STABLE)
A GC mutation on a tree with watermark `w` requires two things:
1. `w ≤` the watermark C-T0 v8 published at the time of the mutation;
2. **every insert of an entry with `ts ≤ w` into that tree has finished.**

Condition 2 holds for apply and for provisional conversions, because `w ≤ W ≤ M`, the apply frontier (C-T0 v8 §3.5, §9.1): applies of later commits carry ts `> visible_ts ≥ M`, and a conversion inserts at the commit ts `c` of a large transaction that is not finalized, so `c > M`. Provisional entries (suffix `u64::MAX`) are not versions and are not inserts in this sense. It must also hold for bulk loads, which write entries at a ts `S` (§12.8). Registering `S` itself is not enough, because it gives only `W ≤ S`, and a GC at `w = S` could then run beside the load's unfinished inserts at `S`. So for the whole load, S-3 keeps a snapshot registered below `S`, which gives `W < S`.

**The registration comes before `S` is chosen.** `W` is monotonic (§1), and it equals `visible_ts` when no older snapshot is registered. So `W` may already be `S` when `S` is picked, and a registration at `S − 1` made after that cannot hold `W` down. S-3 therefore:
1. registers a snapshot at some `R` (normally the current `visible_ts`), which holds `W ≤ R` from then on;
2. picks the load's `S > R`, for example `S = R + 1` once `visible_ts > R`;
3. keeps `R` registered until the load's last insert has finished.

That gives `W ≤ R ≤ S − 1 < S` for the whole load.

**No mutant for this condition (S-1 gate, ruling 1).** The load writes only live entries, all at `S`, into an unpublished tree. Suppose a GC with `w = S` races those inserts. Each loaded entry lands either behind the sweep's position, where it is newer than everything that GC kept, or ahead of it, where it is the newest `≤ w` and is kept. The worst outcome is under-pruning, and no read at any `S' ≥ W` changes. A mutant that registers `S` instead of `R` is therefore unobservable without a new hook. Condition 2 stays as a formal precondition, because it is what the proof of §10.4's carried state uses.

Under these conditions, **the set of entries with `ts ≤ w` changes only through GC**, which is what makes carrying state across leaves sound (§10.4).

### 10.2 The rule
For each user key, with `w` fixed:
- **keep** every version with `ts > w`;
- **keep** the newest version with `ts ≤ w`. Exception: when it is a tombstone, **elide** it, provided every older version of the key is deleted, and the tombstone is deleted **last** (I-GC-ORDER). This holds whether or not versions above `w` exist (X-2-F1, correcting the spike's `above_w == 0` condition at `engine.rs:585`). An elided tombstone still counts as the key's newest `≤ w` version, so every older version is deleted and none becomes the kept one;
- **delete** every older version, freeing its overflow chain in the same gated operation (§6.7). The sweep deletes a chain-bearing version in its own gated operation, holding only its leaf, and only while it is still the same entry (§10.4, "Chains first").

**Provisional entries are not versions here.** No GC mutation counts, keeps or prunes a provisional entry as a version, or lets one change the carried state of §10.4. A GC mutation may delete a **dead** provisional entry (C-T0 v8 §2.4 (c)). Readers ignore dead entries, so that is read-equivalent. Because `W ≤` the apply frontier (C-T0 v8 §3.5), no conversion ever inserts an entry with `ts ≤ w`, and §10.1's condition 2 holds.

**Two departures from the spike's `gc`:**
- **The elision condition.** The spike elides only when `above_w == 0` (`engine.rs:585`); this file elides in every case (X-2-F1).
- **The delete order.** The spike pushes the tombstone candidate first (`engine.rs:585–588`) and then deletes the candidates in push order (`engine.rs:599`). It therefore deletes the tombstone **before** its older versions, and a read at `S ≥ W` between those deletes sees an older version. This transiently violates I-GC-ORDER. Here the tombstone goes last.

**I-GC-READEQ.** Every GC mutation leaves unchanged the result of every read in §8.1–8.3 at every snapshot `S ≥` the watermark published at the time of the mutation. It also leaves unchanged the §8.2 answers for both S-2 rules.
- **Pruning.** A pruned version is shadowed for every such `S` by a newer version `≤ w ≤ S` that stays.
- **Tombstone elision.** Deleting the tombstone last means that at every intermediate state, either the tombstone shadows the older versions, or no older version exists. Both read as absent at every such `S`.

Mutants (all three are `verdicts/X-2.md`'s, with X-2's meaning):
- GC-DROPS-VISIBLE;
- G-WATERMARK+1;
- G-RESURRECT: an elided tombstone at `≤ w` lets the next older version become the kept one.

I-GC-ORDER's mutant is new: **G-TOMB-FIRST**, the tombstone deleted before its older versions (the spike's order). It is caught by a group that spans leaves (so the sweep needs more than one mutation), with reads at `S ≥ W` between the sweep's steps: through an interleaving hook, or through a checkpoint flip between the steps followed by a crash. The image then holds the intermediate state, and recovery replays no GC.

**I-GC-COMPLETE.** After a complete sweep (§10.4) of a tree with `w` fixed and no concurrent inserts, every key has:
- its versions `> w`;
- at most one version `≤ w`, which is live.

Mutant: GC-KEEPS-TOMB, the spike's narrow elision. It is caught by a directed test:
1. put;
2. delete;
3. register a reader;
4. a newer put;
5. gc.

The `≤ w` tombstone must be gone, and the reader's reads must be unchanged. The reader is what makes the tombstone the newest `≤ w` while a version above `w` exists. X-2-F1's test as written ("with no readers") puts `w` at or above the newer put, which turns the tombstone into an ordinary older version and leaves the mutant uncaught.

### 10.3 Page-local prune
- **When it runs.** It **must** run before a leaf split (§6.4). It **may** run on any current-epoch leaf that a thread holds exclusively, including at eviction. It **must not** run on an epoch-copy frame, whose content belongs to image `k`, with `gc_w` already fixed.
- **What it may do,** using a `w` that satisfies §10.1, within one leaf, in one page mutation:
  - **delete** a version when a newer version of the same key with `ts ≤ w` precedes it **on the same leaf**;
  - **elide** a newest-`≤ w` tombstone only when an entry of a **different key follows its group on the same leaf**, which proves the group ends here, **and** none of the group's older versions holds a chain. The same mutation then deletes the group's older versions. Otherwise the tombstone is kept, and the prune may still delete the group's non-chain older versions, each shadowed by the tombstone (I-GC-READEQ). *On the leaf* covers the whole group: the tombstone is the newest `≤ w`, versions are in ts-descending order (§7.2), and the group ends here, so every older version of the group follows the tombstone on this leaf.
- **Chains.** The prune frees no chain. It deletes and elides only entries that hold no chain reference: tags 0–3 (by the two rules above) and dead tags 6–7 (§10.2). It never deletes a tag-4, tag-5 or tag-8 entry, dead or not, and so never maps, unmaps or reads an Overflow page. Chain-bearing garbage is left to the sweep's "Chains first" (§10.4), which deletes each such entry in its own leaf-only gated operation. The rule holds at every call site: before a split, on any exclusively held leaf, and at eviction, including nested under another operation's hold (§5.2).
- **What it must not do (I-GC-LOCAL).** It never deletes or elides on the strength of an entry it cannot see. In particular, the leaf's first entry is never pruned as "not newest" unless a newer `≤ w` entry precedes it on this leaf: its group may continue from the left. Mutant: PRUNE-FIRST-ENTRY.

The page-local prune is incomplete by design: a group that spans leaves keeps its garbage until the sweep reaches it, and so does every chain-bearing entry.

**Why the prune frees no chain.** Freeing a chain reads each of its pages for the next pointer (§10.4, "Why not under `P`"). Before a split the leaf is full, so it is not safe (§6.2), and the splitting insert still holds `P`, and possibly more ancestors, exclusively; at eviction the prune may run nested inside another operation's hold (§5.2). A chain free there would read pages under an inner exclusive latch (§5.3) after the operation's first mutation, when it can no longer restart (§5.6), and one insert into a toast-region leaf of cold chains would do thousands of such reads while the flip waits. A prune that frees no chain reads no chain page and frees no LPID, so it does no I/O under an inner latch, holds the flip for no chain, and needs no MapLeaf pins, at every call site. The insert stays live: §6.4 is unchanged, so if the prune frees too little room, the insert splits, and nothing waits on the sweep. The cost is space: chain-bearing garbage on a leaf stays until the sweep, and a toast-region leaf full of it splits where a chain-freeing prune would have made room. That is B-3's bound to hold (I-SPACE is read after a complete sweep, §11; trigger T2), not a correctness question. *Rejected:* a splitting insert that deletes each chain-bearing prunable entry in its own leaf-only gated operation, then restarts. It would put up to a leaf's worth of cold chain frees in front of a foreground insert, add a second chain-deleting path with its own identity check (C-T0 v8 §2.4 (c)) and liveness argument beside the sweep's, and could not serve the prune at eviction.

### 10.4 The sweep
`gc_sweep(tree, w, token) → token | done` runs bounded steps of the §10.2 rule over a whole tree, in key order.
- **Concurrency.** At most one sweep per tree at a time.
- **Fixed `w`.** `w` is fixed for the whole sweep and is stored in the resumable `token`, together with:
  - the carried state: the current user key, whether its newest `≤ w` version has been seen, and a pending tombstone elision;
  - the **resume position**: "after entry key `x`" or "from key `f`";
  - the running count of parent-overflow skips (§6.5).

  Resuming with a later `w` would also be safe, but a fixed `w` keeps the token simple.

**Per step** (one gated operation; latches only top-down and left to right, I-LATCH):
1. **Descend.** Re-descend from the root by the resume position (I-CURSOR) with exclusive latch coupling. Keep latched both the leaf `L` that holds the position and its parent `P`: `P` first, then `L`.
   - On the way down, record the **fence** `f`: the lowest separator to the right of the path, or "tree end".
   - If `L` has a right sibling `R` under `P`, latch `R` after `L`.
   - Every page is pinned and resident before it is latched (pin-then-validate, §5.3). A non-resident page makes the step release everything, pin the page, and restart.
   - If the tree's root is a leaf, `L` is the root, and there is no `P` and no `R`.
2. **Mark.** Apply §10.2 in order to `L`'s entries past the resume position, then to all of `R`'s, using the carried state. This is sound by §10.1.
3. **Delete.** Delete the marked entries, in this step's mutation. None of them holds a chain, so the mutation frees no chain:
   - **Chains first.** If a marked entry of `L` or `R` holds a chain reference (tags 4, 5 and 8, §7.3), the step ends before its first mutation, so it has changed nothing and the flip may land after it (§5.6). The sweep then deletes each such entry with its chain (§6.7), in key order, each in its own gated operation, holding only its leaf: its descent releases the leaf's parent once it has latched the leaf, because a delete never grows the leaf, and it never packs (§6.5). Then the step runs again from the same token.
     - *Each delete is conditional on the entry's identity* (C-T0 v8 §2.4 (c)), checked under the leaf's exclusive latch, because the latches are released between the step's end and the delete. An older version is identified by its entry key `k@ts` (I-ENTRY-UNIQUE; no insert re-creates a `ts ≤ w` key, §10.1). A tag-8 entry sits at `entry_key(k, u64::MAX)`, which every provisional entry on `k` shares, so it is deleted only if its bytes still show it dead. In the gap a writer on `k` may settle the dead entry (settler (c)) and write its own current-boot provisional entry at that key: the delete finds a current-boot entry and leaves it, and its chain, alone. No entry becomes dead during a boot (below), so a dead entry found there is the one the step marked. If the entry is gone, or is no longer the same entry (a writer deleted it), the operation does nothing. Mutant CHAIN-DELETE-BY-KEY (§15): the delete matches the entry key alone, so it deletes the writer's live entry and frees its chain.
     - *Why not under `P`.* Freeing a chain reads each of its pages for the next pointer. Under `P`'s exclusive latch that is I/O §5.3 forbids, and after the step's first mutation it cannot restart (§5.6). One step could also free up to two leaves' worth of chains, and on a toast-region leaf nearly every entry holds one. So one gated operation frees at most one chain and holds the flip for that chain's length (§6.7), and no inner page is latched across a chain's reads (§5.3).
     - *Why it is read-equivalent.* Each such entry is an older version or a dead provisional entry (§10.2), never a kept version and never a tombstone, which has no chain. So its own delete is a GC mutation that I-GC-READEQ allows by itself: the newer version that shadows it, or the tombstone, stays. An elided tombstone is still deleted after every older version of its group (I-GC-ORDER), and each chain is freed after its entry, in the same gated operation (I-LINK-ATOMIC).
     - *Why the re-runs end.* The carried state is unchanged, because a deleted older version is neither a key's newest `≤ w` version nor a pending tombstone. Nothing creates an entry the step could mark as chain-bearing garbage, for two reasons. No insert creates an older version, since none creates an entry with `ts ≤ w` (§10.1). And no entry becomes dead: §10.1's reason excludes provisional entries, but dead means written in a previous boot (C-T0 v8 §2.4), so every provisional entry written while the sweep runs is current-boot, stays current-boot for the rest of the boot, and is never marked (§10.2). So the chain-bearing entries past the position that the step can mark form a finite set that only shrinks, each round removes from it every entry it marked (deleted, found gone or found replaced), and concurrent writers cannot keep the step from its mutation. A leaf the deletes empty is legal (§6.1), as one the step's own mutation empties is.
   - **The tombstone's group ends within `L` or `R`:** a newest-`≤ w` tombstone t is deleted in the same mutation as the remaining older versions of its group. The chain-bearing ones went first, each in its own gated operation, so the step's mutation, and t's delete in it, comes only after every one of them is gone (I-GC-ORDER; mutant STEP-MUTATE-BEFORE-CHAINS, §15). Within one gated operation under exclusive latches, the order is invisible.
   - **The group continues past this step:** t is recorded as **pending**. It is deleted in its own gated operation, by key, once the sweep has passed the group's end. If t is already gone (a page-local prune elided it), that operation does nothing.
4. **Pack.** If `R` exists, run `pack(P, L, R)` (§6.5), and add any parent-overflow skip to the token's count. This is how GC returns pages.
5. **Advance.**
   - **If `R` existed,** the resume position becomes "after `R`'s last entry key". The pack preserves entries, so the next descent lands on the page that now holds that key (the merged `L`, or `R`), and the sweep packs that page with its right sibling.
   - **If `L` was `P`'s rightmost child,** `P`'s children are done, and the sweep runs the **cascade** `finish(P)` before it moves on.
     - **Addressing.** Let `x` be `L`'s last entry key, and count levels from the leaves (a leaf is level 1). Each page of the cascade is addressed by `x` and its level: the page at that level on the path to `x`, or that page's right sibling.
     - **Latching.** Each pack is its own gated operation, reached by a fresh descent toward `x` that latches the parent first (§6.5).

     `finish(X)` runs on an inner page `X` whose children are all done:
     1. **Root.** If `X` is the root, collapse it while it is an inner page with one child (§6.3). Stop.
     2. **Rightmost child: go up.** If `X` is its parent `G`'s rightmost child, `X` is exempt (§11). `G`'s children are now done too, so run `finish(G)`.
     3. **Otherwise, pack right.** If `used(X) < U/4`, run `pack(G, X, X_r)`, where `X_r` is `X`'s **right** neighbour (never its left one, §6.5), and add any skip to the token's count. If the pack moved cells from `X_r` into `X` (a merge or a redistribute), run the **junction walk** from `X`. Then stop: `X_r`'s children, or what is left of them, are not done, so `G`'s children are not done either.
   - **The junction walk** from an inner page `X` that has just gained cells from its right neighbour.
     - **The problem.** `X`'s former rightmost child `c` is now a middle child, and its new right sibling `c_r` came from the neighbour and has not been swept. `c` was exempt as a rightmost child, so it may be underfull.
     - If `c` is the leaf `L`, stop. The leaf step that follows packs `L` with `c_r` (see below).
     - If `c` is an inner page with `used(c) < U/4`, run `pack(X, c, c_r)` and count any skip. If that pack moved cells into `c`, continue the walk from `c`, one level down.
     - Otherwise, stop. `c`'s own rightmost child has not moved, so it is still exempt.
   - **After the cascade,** the next step depends on `L`:
     - if `L` now has a right sibling under its parent (the junction walk reached `L`), the position stays "after `x`", so the next step packs `L` with that sibling;
     - otherwise the position becomes "from `f`".

     The token records the cascade's progress (`x`, the current level, and whether a junction walk is under way), so a resumed sweep continues the cascade instead of starting it again. Each check reads the current pages, so a repeated check would only cost work.

**Why the cascade reaches every page, at every height.**
- **Only unswept pages shrink.** In every pack, the right page has not yet been swept. Left pages and parents only grow, except a parent whose own `finish` is still to come. So a page the sweep has passed never drops below its clause again.
- **Exemptions are re-checked.** A passed page loses its rightmost-child exemption only when its parent gains cells from the right. That is exactly the junction walk, which re-checks the page.
- **Every inner page is finished after its last change.** Step 2 recurses through rightmost children, so `finish` reaches every ancestor whose subtree has just ended, not only `L`'s parent. A page that gains unswept children from a merge or redistribute runs `finish` again when those children are done.

At the end of a complete sweep, therefore, every non-root inner page has been checked against `U/4` after its last change, and every leaf has been packed with its final right sibling. Both I-SPACE clauses then hold at every height (§11).

Mutants:
- **SWEEP-PACK-LEFT:** the sweep packs a rightmost-child inner page with its left neighbour. The leaf clause then fails at the junction.
- **CASCADE-ONE-LEVEL:** the cascade checks only `L`'s parent, so step 2 does not recurse. The inner clause then fails at height ≥ 4.
- **JUNCTION-SKIP:** no junction walk after an inner pack. The inner clause then fails at height ≥ 4.

A-4b catches all three with small-page trees of height ≥ 4, asserting I-SPACE after a complete sweep.

**Sweep report.** A complete sweep returns `done` together with its count of parent-overflow skips, which I-SPACE and B-3 read (§11).

**Interruption.** A crash or stop between steps leaves a read-equivalent state (I-GC-READEQ) and leaks nothing (I-LPID-EXACT): every LPID free is in the same gated operation as its unlinking.

When sweeps run, how often, and with what I/O budget is S-2/B-3's decision (background jobs, C-B1 semantics).

### 10.5 The image's watermark (`gc_w`)
- **What it records.** The store keeps `gc_w_used`, the largest `w` it has applied in any GC mutation or DropTree mark (§6.8 step 1; "Drops", below). At open it starts at the recovered meta's `gc_w`, so a checkpoint taken before the first GC mutation (including the boot checkpoint, §14 step 8) still records the image's bound (mutant GCW-NOT-RECORDED). At the flip of checkpoint `k`, it records that value as meta `k`'s `gc_w`. While the flip holds the gate, no GC mutation is in flight, so `gc_w` bounds every GC mutation contained in image `k`.
- **Bound.** `gc_w ≤ T` always. Each `w` was `≤ W` at its use (§10.1 condition 1), and `W ≤ M`, the apply frontier (C-T0 v8 §3.5, §9.1). `M` is monotonic, and the flip takes `T := M` (§13 step 0), so every `w` used before the flip is `≤ T`. A DropTree mark's `w` is a published `W` read no later than its mutation (§6.8 step 1, §12.3), so the same argument covers it. At open, `gc_w_used` starts at the recovered `gc_w ≤` the recovered `T ≤` the recovered apply frontier; a mark that pass 2 replays raises it to its op's `w`, which is below `c`, the commit ts the mark is replayed at, since `c` was issued above `visible_ts ≥ W` (C-T0 v8 §3.1 step 1, §3.5) after the drop-cleanup job read `w`; and `c ≤` the recovered apply frontier, which the boot checkpoint takes as `T` (§14 step 8). A violation at meta write is fail-stop (§13 step 4), and an engine invariant violation (XX000, §9.2), except at a boot checkpoint: there `gc_w_used` is still what recovery read, the opened meta's `gc_w` raised by the `w` of each mark pass 2 replayed, which in a correct engine only a meta or a record that no correct writer writes can put above the recovered frontier, so a violation there is detected damage, XX001 (detail: `gc_w`, with `gc_w_used` and `T`).
- **I-GCW.** Meta `k`'s `gc_w` is `≥` every `w` of a GC mutation or a DropTree mark in image `k`, and `≤ T`. Mutants:
  - **GCW-NOT-RECORDED:** the meta carries the previous `gc_w` or 0. After a restart, `W` can then start below the image's pruning, and a snapshot in that gap reads a pruned state;
  - **GCW-BOUND-SKIP:** the `gc_w ≤ T` check at meta write is removed. Real GC never trips the check, so A-7c judges it with a forged `gc_w_used > T` at the flip: the meta write must fail-stop with XX000 (§9.2, §13 step 4), and no meta with `gc_w > T` reaches the file (§15.1);
  - **BOOT-GCW-AS-INVARIANT** (card S-IOTA): a boot checkpoint's `gc_w > T` fails with XX000, as at every other checkpoint. A-7d judges it with a meta whose `gc_w` exceeds its `T` and every commit's ts, forged through `Env` with a valid CRC into both of its places (fault injection): a read-write open of it fails at its boot checkpoint's meta write with XX001, and no meta place changes (§15.1).
- **Drops (card S-1-F4).** A DropTree's mark (§6.8 step 1) raises `gc_w_used` to its `w` in its own gated operation, as a GC mutation at `w` would, though it deletes no entry. The raise happens when the mark changes an entry from `live` to `dropping`: a `dropping` or absent entry was marked before some image's flip, whose `gc_w` already counts it.
  - *Which `w`.* For a logged DropTree, the one its op carries (§12.3), in normal operation and in replay alike: the watermark C-T0 v8 published, as the drop-cleanup job read it once it found the drop allowed. A retired tree's drop runs only once `W` exceeds the snapshot `S_job` at which the drop job read its retirement, and `S_job ≥ S_ddl`, the retiring DDL's commit ts (C-T0 v8 §9.3, C-T6-rehost §7.4); the job reads `w` no earlier than it finds `W > S_job`, and `W` is monotonic, so `w > S_ddl`. `w` is at most the published `W` at the mark, so at most `T` at every later flip, and it is below the commit ts `c` that the mark is replayed at (Bound, above). One value in both paths makes the floor a replay leaves equal to the one an image that holds the mark records. Not `c − 1` in normal operation: applies finish out of ts order, so a flip after the mark can take `T = M < c − 1`. Not `c − 1` in replay either, as draft 7.12 had it: that is safe, but after a crash that replays the drop it refuses AS OF snapshots in `[w, c − 1)` whose data is intact (mutant REPLAY-DROP-AT-TS).
  - *Why.* The catalog names a dropped tree at every snapshot below `S_ddl`, and once its drop has started the tree cannot be read: a thread that names it finds it `dropping` or absent and stops (§6.8 "Root names"), and its walk frees the pages, resuming at open (§14 step 6). Before a restart, `W > S_job` keeps every such snapshot out (C-T6-rehost §7.5). After a restart, `W` starts at the floor (After open, below). If the floor counted GC mutations only, an image that holds the mark, a freed page or the removed entry, or a replay of the drop's record, could open with `W` below `S_ddl`, and an AS OF snapshot in `[W, S_ddl)` would be admitted (C-T0 v8 §3.6) and fail with a spurious XX001 or an absent tree, where 72000 was due. That holds for every open, not only salvage. The mark carries the raise, not the walk's first step, because an image can hold the mark before any page is freed.
  - *Cost.* Eight bytes per `DropTree` op (§12.3). Every `w` a store applies is at most the watermark it has published, so the floor after a replay is never above the watermark the store had published before the crash.
  - Mutants:
    - **DROP-NO-GCW**: the mark does not raise `gc_w_used`; an image that holds the mark, a freed page or the removed entry reopens with `W` below `S_ddl`;
    - **DROP-GCW-AT-WALK**: the walk's first step raises `gc_w_used`, not the mark; an image flipped between the mark and that step holds `dropping` with `gc_w` below `S_ddl`;
    - **DROP-GCW-AT-TS**: in normal operation the mark raises `gc_w_used` to its commit ts minus one; a flip while an earlier commit's apply holds the apply frontier below that value takes `T < gc_w_used`, and the meta write fails stop (§13 step 4);
    - **REPLAY-DROP-NO-GCW**: a replayed mark does not raise `gc_w_used`; the boot that replays the drop keeps a floor below `S_ddl`, and so does its boot meta;
    - **REPLAY-DROP-AT-TS**: a replayed mark raises `gc_w_used` to `c − 1`, where `c` is the commit ts it is replayed at, not to its op's `w`; that is safe, but after a crash that replays the drop, AS OF snapshots in `[w, c − 1)` get 72000 although their data is intact;
    - **DROP-OP-W-ZERO**: the op codec drops `w`, so a replayed op carries 0 and its mark raises nothing, which leaves REPLAY-DROP-NO-GCW's floor;
    - **DROP-MARK-AT-PUBLISHED-W**: a logged DropTree's mark raises `gc_w_used` to the `W` published at its mutation, not to its op's `w`: safe, but an image that holds the mark then records a floor above `w`, and a restart from it refuses AS OF snapshots in `[w, W)` that a replay of the same drop would admit.
  - Test `t_drop_restart_floor` (A-6a, A-7b, A-7c and A-7d in the forms §15.1 states; B-6 in full): a tree that the catalog names below `S_ddl`, retired at `S_ddl` and dropped by a drop-cleanup commit at ts `c` whose op carries `w`, a published `W > S_ddl` (§12.3), with no GC mutation at a `w ≥ S_ddl` in any form (S3's GC `gc_w_used` stays below `S_ddl`; once `W` passes `S_ddl` no leaf splits and no current-epoch leaf is evicted, so that no page-local prune (§10.3), which is a GC mutation, runs at such a `w`, the drop-cleanup commit's own catalog delete included; and B-6 holds B-3's sweep off), so that without the drop's raise `gc_w_used`, and the floor after a reopen, would stay at or below `S_ddl − 1`, in six variants: (a) the walk held before its first step, a checkpoint whose image holds the mark and no freed page, then a crash; (b) a checkpoint whose image holds the mark and some freed pages, then a crash part-way through the walk; (c) a checkpoint after the walk's last step, whose image no longer holds the entry, then a crash; (d) the drop's record above `T` and its apply after the flip, with `w < c − 1` (the job read `w` while `visible_ts` stood above it: at B-6 a snapshot registered above `S_job` holds `W` below `visible_ts` when the job reads it), then a crash before the next checkpoint, so recovery replays it; (e) variant (d) opened read-only; (f) the drop applied while an earlier commit's apply holds the apply frontier below the drop's commit ts minus one, then a flip. After each reopen `gc_w_used` is at least `w` (exactly `w` in (a), with `W` published above `w` before the mark, and in (d) and (e), the larger of `w` and the opened meta's `gc_w`), the boot meta of (d) records it, (e) writes nothing, an AS OF snapshot at `S_ddl − 1` gives 72000, and one at the reopened `W`, which is `gc_w_used` as recovery leaves it and lies above `S_ddl`, and which in (d) and (e) is taken at the op's `w` itself, the value a correct reopen publishes, so that a floor left above `w` refuses it, succeeds and reads a catalog that does not name the tree (one at `S_ddl` gets 72000 as well, since `W > S_ddl`; C-T0 v8 §3.6). After a read-write reopen both AS OF snapshots are taken before `W` is first published above the floor, or with a non-zero AS OF retention window, which holds `W` at the floor for one window length (C-T0 v8 §9.1): with a zero window a publication can raise `W` to the recovered `visible_ts` at once. In (f) the meta write succeeds. DROP-NO-GCW fails (a)–(c), DROP-GCW-AT-WALK fails (a), REPLAY-DROP-NO-GCW fails (d) and (e), REPLAY-DROP-AT-TS fails (d) and (e) with `gc_w_used = c − 1`, DROP-OP-W-ZERO fails (d) and (e) with the opened meta's `gc_w`, DROP-MARK-AT-PUBLISHED-W fails (a) with the `W` published at the mark, and DROP-GCW-AT-TS fails (f). At A-6a, the op's `w` encodes and decodes inside `Commit` and `Chunk` records, and the fuzz targets reach it; DROP-OP-W-ZERO fails that round trip.
- **After open,** C-T0 v8's watermark must be at least `gc_w_used` as recovery leaves it: the recovered meta's `gc_w`, raised by every DropTree mark that pass 2 replays (§14 step 4), because the recovered pages were pruned with it and the recovered directory holds every drop it counts (Drops, above). A read-only open is held to the same floor (§14.1). In v7.8 this was `/sys/gc_w`, synced before GC acted (C-T0 v7.8 §9.1). v3 has no unversioned system KV, so the image carries it instead. Pruning done after meta `k` is lost with a crash, together with its pages, so the meta's value is exact.

---

## 11. Space reclamation

The decision block requires that GC actually return space. "S-1/A-4b's merge and free set must give the space back." E0 never merged or freed pages, which is why (b) stayed at **20.11×** after GC cut its versions from 1.1M to 100k (`spikes/e1-bench/README.md` §W4; `spikes/e0-btree/src/tree.rs` T-NOMERGE). The chain from a dead version back to a reusable slot:
1. **Entry → page.** The sweep deletes the version (§10.4), and the pack step merges or redistributes leaves, freeing merged-away LPIDs (§6.5). Inner pages and the root follow.
2. **LPID → slot.** A freed LPID's home slot is no longer referenced by the current map. If the durable image, the image being written or the previous image still references it, it stays pending (§4.4).
3. **Slot → free set.** After the first checkpoint `k` whose flip follows the sweep, image `k` no longer references the slot, and image `k`'s persisted bitmap marks it free (I-FREE-EXACT). It is free in memory once meta `k+1` is synced (§13 step 6 of checkpoint `k+1`, I-TWO-GEN), one checkpoint later than at draft 7.10.
4. **Free set → reuse.** The slot is allocated lowest-first, before the file grows (§4.5).

**I-SPACE (quiescent).** Take a complete sweep (§10.4) of a tree with `w` fixed and no concurrent inserts, which reports `skips` parent-overflow skips (§6.5). After it:
- **leaves:** every leaf has `used > F_PACK·U − C_max`, where `C_max` is the largest cell in the tree, with two exceptions: the rightmost child of each inner page, and the at most `skips` leaves whose pack step was skipped. A pack step leaves `L` short of that only by merging, and `L` then takes part in the next step;
- **inner pages:** no non-root inner page has `used < U/4`, except the rightmost child of each inner page and the at most `skips` pages whose pack was skipped. This holds at every tree height: the sweep's cascade recurses to every ancestor, and its junction walk re-checks a page that has stopped being a rightmost child (§10.4 step 5);
- **root:** the root is a leaf, or has at least 2 children;
- **LPIDs:** every LPID removed from the tree is freed (I-LPID-EXACT).

A test of I-SPACE asserts the clauses with the reported `skips`. B-3 reports `skips` with its measurement, and the derived bound below counts them. In that shape merges dominate and separators are short, so `skips` is expected to be 0.

**Tree bytes (the metric of B-3 and T2).**
- **Definition.** The tree bytes of a tree are `page_size ×` the number of its **reachable pages**: the mapped LPIDs reachable from its root, meaning inner pages, leaves and overflow chains. amcheck (A-8) counts them on a quiescent tree.
- **What it is not.** It is not the file length, `slot_count` or the count of pages ever allocated. The file never shrinks (§4.5), so those stay at the post-update peak, about 11 versions per key in W3/W4, and B-3 could never pass on them.
- **The e1-bench figure.** e1-bench's `tree_bytes` is `page_count × page_size`, which counts pages ever allocated (`spikes/e1-arm-b/src/engine.rs:617`). It equals the reachable count only because E0 never frees a page.
- **File length.** B-3 also reports the file length beside it, for information only (§19 Q1).

**What this gives B-3's acceptance (derived, to be measured by B-3).** The acceptance re-runs the W3/W4 shape on the real tree and requires after-GC tree bytes `≤ 1.5×` the pre-update tree bytes (decision block), both measured as defined above. In that shape:
- before the updates and after GC, each key holds exactly one live version of equal size, so the leaf cell bytes are equal;
- no page holds more than `U`, so the pre-update tree has at least `cells/U` leaves;
- I-SPACE caps the after-GC leaves at `cells/(F_PACK·U − C_max)`, plus one per inner page and one per skip.

At 8 KiB pages (`U ≈ 8.1 KiB`) with W3's ~135-byte cells, the bound is ≈ `1/(0.85 − 0.017) ≈ 1.2×`, plus under 1% for the partial last children and the inner pages, plus `skips` pages. That is within 1.5× **whatever the pre-update fill**. A B-3 failure re-opens N3 (decision block).

---

## 12. WAL (N4, N14)

### 12.1 Segments
- **Files.** Segments are `wal/<first-lsn as 16 lowercase hex digits>.log`, each `wal_seg_size` bytes (§2.1), preallocated. `wal_seg_size` is at least 212, four record headers (53 bytes each, §12.2), so that the largest record, `wal_seg_size / 4`, holds at least a header. Creation refuses a smaller value, and outside the store's tests one below 8 MiB (§2.3). An open whose file's identity (§2.2) carries a smaller value fails with XX001 before it reads the WAL, and writes nothing. Mutant **SEG-SIZE-UNCHECKED**: creation writes a `wal_seg_size` below its floor, or an open accepts one below 212. Test `t_wal_seg_size` (A-7c, A-7d; §15.1): at A-7c, creation asked for 8 MiB − 1 or for 211 fails with 22023 and leaves no file, and asked for 8 MiB succeeds; with a test's floor of 212, 211 fails the same way and 212 succeeds. At A-7d, a file whose page 0 and meta copies carry 211, written by the test with valid CRCs (a forged input), fails the open with XX001, read-write and read-only, and leaves every file byte-identical. SEG-SIZE-UNCHECKED fails it.
- **LSN.** An LSN is a byte position in the logical WAL stream; a segment covers `[first_lsn, first_lsn + wal_seg_size)`.
- **Recycling.** A segment is recycled by renaming it to a new first LSN, one above the segment that holds the append position, which no segment file holds. Its old content is then stale (§12.5). No recycling rename takes a first LSN below the append position: that name may be the start segment of a meta that an open selects once newer metas are lost, which must then be missing, not present with another segment's old records (I-WAL-START; mutant RECYCLE-TARGET-LOW, §12.5). Step 6 recycles segments lowest first, and makes each rename durable (the `wal/` directory entry synced through `Env`) before it renames the next, so while a segment is present, no higher segment has been recycled (I-WAL-START).
- **Segment 0** exists from file creation (§2.3).

### 12.2 Record framing
```
magic      u32  "NWAL"
len        u32  bytes of the whole record
crc        u32  CRC32C of the bytes after this field, to the end of the record
lsn        u64  this record's own start position
prev_lsn   u64  the start LSN of the record appended immediately before this one (SegmentEnd included); all-ones if none
prev_crc   u32  the crc field of that record; 0 if none
kind       u8   1 Commit | 2 Chunk | 3 CommitMarker | 4 SegmentEnd
txn        (u32 boot id, u64 n)      C-T0 TxnId, n ≥ 1; in SegmentEnd, (the writing boot's id, 0)
commit_ts  u64  0 in Chunk and SegmentEnd
payload
```
Every record carries the boot id of the boot that wrote it (§12.5). TxnId `n` starts at 1 in every boot (C-T0 v8 §2.5), so `SegmentEnd`'s `n = 0` names no transaction.

`prev_lsn` and `prev_crc` are the WAL back-pointer that §12.5 checks (I-WAL-CHAIN). They lie after `crc`, so the record CRC covers them. They name the record appended immediately before this one in the stream, by its start and by its `crc` field: across a segment hop, that is the `SegmentEnd`, or the record that ended exactly at the previous segment's end. **A boot's first record** names the last record its recovery walk accepted (§14), or, if that walk accepted none, the opened meta's `wal_start_prev` and `wal_start_prev_crc` (§2.2). The creating session's first record carries meta 0's, all-ones and 0. They are compared, never read through. The record that the next append names is the **chain head**: the start and the `crc` field of the record reserved last, its `crc` final once that record is sealed. It moves with the append position, in the append critical section (§12.4). After recovery, until the boot's first append, it is the recovery's: the last record the walk accepted, or, if the walk accepted none, the opened meta's pair (§14 step 5). So a boot checkpoint, whose flip precedes its boot's first append, records the recovery's chain head as the pair for its append position (§13 step 0; mutant BOOT-PAIR-UNSET, §12.5).
- *Why the CRC as well as the position* (PLAN-v3 §10 item 9, 2026-11-03 addendum). A position does not tell apart two histories that share a record boundary: two boots that append records of equal length from the same point put different records at the same starts. The predecessor's `crc` tells them apart unless the two predecessors are byte-identical (or their CRC32C values collide). SQLite chains its WAL checksums for the same reason.
- *Order of sealing.* A record's `crc` covers its `prev_crc`, so it is final only once its predecessor's is. The writer therefore finishes record CRCs in LSN order. It may checksum the rest of a record earlier and fold `prev_crc` in last (CRC32C is linear). Positions are still reserved at append, and I-ACK-PREFIX is unchanged (§12.4). A record's `prev_lsn` and `prev_crc` name the record reserved just before it, which was the chain head when the record was reserved (§12.4), and later reservations move the chain head on. So a batch's first record is sealed with the previous batch's last record's `crc`, taken once that record is sealed, never with a value read before then (mutant SEAL-STALE-HEAD, §12.5).
- *Design note (non-normative; S-1-F5's gate, (2)).* CRC32C is affine, so a writer can fold `lsn`, `prev_lsn` and `prev_crc` into a record's CRC at reservation, inside the append critical section (§12.4), at O(log len) cost, since the payload is serialised by then (`len` is fixed at reservation). The chain head's `crc` then moves with the append position, and no batch's write or sync waits on another batch's I/O. A writer that seals later must keep the order above.

**Records never span segments.** A record is placed at the append position only if it ends exactly at its segment's end or leaves room for a `SegmentEnd` after it. Otherwise the writer writes `SegmentEnd` there, and the record starts at the next segment's first LSN. So a segment ends with `SegmentEnd` or with a record that ends exactly at its end, never with a shorter gap. A single record is at most `wal_seg_size / 4`; larger transactions are chunked.

### 12.3 Record kinds and ops
**Kinds:**
- `Commit{ops}`: one small transaction at `commit_ts`;
- `Chunk{seq u32, ops}`: repeated, written before the commit; `commit_ts = 0`;
- `CommitMarker{nchunks u32}` at `commit_ts`: commits the chunked transaction.

**Ops:**
```
CreateTree{tree_id, kind}   DropTree{tree_id, w u64}   Ddl{bytes} # first within a record but for a RequireCkpt (opaque Ddl bytes are catalog-owned)
Put{tree_id, key, flags, value, before?}                          # flags: key_changed
Delete{tree_id, key, flags, before?}                              # flags: moved | revert (Chunk only)
RequireCkpt{ckpt_id}                                              # store-level; first in its record, before CreateTree, DropTree and Ddl too (§12.8, I-CKPT-DEP)
```
- **Keys.** `key` is the **user key**, not the entry key. The version entry is formed at apply time with the commit's ts (§9). The WAL therefore carries no LPID, no slot and no N3 detail.
- **Index entries** are logged explicitly and never re-derived.
- **`before`** is N14's optional before-image, written when the catalog enables it for the tree.
- **`revert`** (only in `Chunk` records) means the transaction no longer writes `key`. It cancels the transaction's earlier ops on that key (§12.7, §14).
- **`RequireCkpt`** names the checkpoint a commit depends on (§12.8). It changes no tree; recovery checks it before pass 2 (§14 step 3b).
- **`w`** in `DropTree` (`u64`) is the watermark C-T0 v8 published, as the drop-cleanup job read it once it found the drop allowed (`W > S_job`, §10.5 "Drops"; C-T0 v8 §9.3, C-T6-rehost §7.4). A DropTree's mark raises `gc_w_used` to it, in normal operation and in replay alike (§6.8 step 1). It costs 8 bytes per `DropTree` op (card S-GAMMA, S-BETA-O1).
- **Bytes.** Card A-6a pins the op encoding and fuzzes it.

### 12.4 Group commit, the durable point, and order (I-ACK-PREFIX, I-WAL-TS-ORDER)
- **Append.** A record's LSN is fixed when it is appended: the commit path reserves its position (and, for `Commit` and `CommitMarker`, its ts, below) and moves the **append position** past it. Every reservation, of every kind, `Chunk` and `SegmentEnd` included, is one entry to the **append critical section**, which moves the append position and the chain head (§12.2) together; the I-WAL-TS-ORDER critical section of a `Commit` or `CommitMarker` (below) contains its entry. Any mechanism that makes each reservation one indivisible step will do. A reservation is not a gated operation (§5.6), and the store's append takes no gate: a caller may reserve a `Chunk` while it holds the gate shared, inside a gated materialisation (C-T0 v8 §3.2), or while it holds none, since a `Chunk` may carry ops that change no page (a `RequireCkpt`, §12.8; a large transaction's `CreateTree` and `Ddl` ops, C-T6-rehost §5.3, §5.5). So a `Chunk` may be reserved while step 0 holds the gate exclusively, and the open-chunk set, not the gate, keeps §12.6's terms right. Nor does the store's append take the I-WAL-TS-ORDER critical section for a `Chunk`, nor for the `SegmentEnd` of a `Chunk`'s hop where a build reserves it in an entry of its own: it may take that section only for a `Commit` or `CommitMarker` and the `SegmentEnd` of its hop (below), and a caller may hold it around a `Chunk`'s reservation or not (§13 step 0), but a `Chunk` reserved through the store's append by a thread that holds neither the gate nor that section, its hop's `SegmentEnd` included, waits on neither, so it can fall between any two of step 0's entries to the append critical section, even where step 0 holds both around them. The section also keeps §12.6's **open-chunk set**: the entry that reserves a chunked transaction's first `Chunk` adds the transaction with that `Chunk`'s LSN, and the entry that reserves its `CommitMarker` records the marker's commit_ts, each in the same indivisible step as its reservation (mutant START-LSN-SPLIT-READ, §14). The record's bytes are written later, by a writer.
  - **The section is a leaf lock**, like the root table's (§6.8) and the image-record list's (§13 step 2). A thread may enter it holding any lock ranked before it: the lock-table entries that a writer holds logically and a settle stripe (C-T0 v8 §2.6), the epoch gate, frame latches (a build may reserve a `Chunk` inside a gated materialisation, C-T0 v8 §3.2), and the I-WAL-TS-ORDER critical section with the SSI mutex ranked before it (C-T0 v8 §2.6, §8.4); under it the thread takes no lock that this file or C-T0 v8 ranks, starts no I/O and waits for nothing. A reservation that hops (§12.2) moves the append position past its `SegmentEnd` to the next segment's first LSN and reserves its record there, and does nothing about that segment: it neither creates, renames, preallocates nor syncs it, and does not wait for it to exist. The writer makes the segment durable outside the section, before its first write there (§12.5), using a segment that step 6 has already renamed to that first LSN or creating one; until then the append position may stand in a segment that does not exist (§13, "The start segment").
    - *Why.* Step 0 enters the section while it holds the epoch gate exclusively (§13 step 0). A wait inside the section on I/O would hold up step 0, and with it every gated operation, for the length of that I/O, and a wait there for a segment that only a later step 6 recycles would deadlock with that checkpoint's step 0, which must enter the section before its step 6 runs.
    - Mutant **APPEND-SECTION-IO**: a reservation that hops creates, renames, preallocates or syncs the next segment, or waits for it to exist, inside the section. Test `t_append_section_leaf` (A-6c, A-7c; §15 I-LATCH, §15.1).
- **Writers.** Batches of appended records are written and synced by one writer thread, or cooperatively by committing threads in embedded and wasm modes (N10). More than one batch may be in flight. The rule below holds however batches are scheduled.
- **The two frontiers.** `written_end` is the largest LSN `L` such that every record byte below `L` has been written (its `write` returned). `durable_end` is the largest `L ≤ written_end` such that every record byte below `L` was written before the start of a `sync_data` on its segment that has completed. Both advance **only over the contiguous prefix**: writing or syncing a batch while an earlier one is unwritten advances neither past the earlier one.
- **After recovery** (I-BOOT-DURABLE). A read-write open's writer starts with the append position and `written_end` at the valid end (§12.5) and the chain head from the recovery (§12.2), but with `durable_end` at the walk's start, the opened meta's `wal_start_lsn` (meta `k−1`'s after a fall-back, §14 step 4a), never at the valid end. The walk may have accepted records that a crashed process wrote and never synced: they survive a process crash in the page cache, and a later power cut can still lose them. So the boot counts none of them durable until its own `sync_data` of their segment completes, and its boot checkpoint's step 1, which waits for `durable_end ≥ wal_ckpt_end`, here the valid end (§13), syncs every segment that holds a byte the walk accepted, before the boot meta is written and before any ack.
  - *Why.* A walk from a meta older than the boot meta (a meta rollback: both copies of the boot meta lost, a fall-back or an explicit rollback; §2.2, §14 step 4a) reads those bytes again on its way to this boot's records (§12.5, "A walk may cross into newer boots"). If a power cut has lost them, that walk stops among them, above the older meta's `wal_ckpt_end`. After both copies of the boot meta are lost, the coverage check then passes (§14 step 3a), and every commit this boot acked is lost without detection, an I-REDO-COMPLETE break (§14); a fall-back from the boot meta fails its proof instead (§14 step 4a item 3), and a recoverable store is refused. Without a rollback no walk reads below the boot meta's `wal_start_lsn`, which is the valid end (§12.6, §14 step 8), so the break needs a meta rollback together with a process crash and a power cut; class C alone does not reach it (card S-1-F6). E4 does not certify this rule: its SimEnv resolves every unsynced write at each crash, so its prototype's reset of `durable_end` to the valid end is true there.
  - Mutant **BOOT-DURABLE-AT-VALID-END**: `durable_end` starts at the valid end, so the boot checkpoint syncs nothing the walk accepted. Test `t_boot_durable_start` (A-7d; §15 I-BOOT-DURABLE, §15.1).
- **I-ACK-PREFIX.** A `synchronous_commit=on` commit is acked only once `durable_end` is at or above the end of its `Commit` record, or of its `CommitMarker` for a chunked transaction, whose chunks lie below it. With `synchronous_commit=off`, it is acked once `written_end` is at or above that end, before the sync (PLAN-v3 §3.3). C-T0 v8's I-ACK (`visible_ts ≥ c`) is a further condition, not a substitute. Checkpoint step 1 waits on the same `durable_end` (§13).
  - *Why.* The reader stops at the first invalid record (§12.5), so a record behind an unwritten one is never replayed. An ack over that hole loses an acked commit in an ordinary crash, with no media fault, and I-REDO-COMPLETE depends on this rule. E4 found exactly this (X-5c-F3, seed 21 r0). Two tasks each wrote and synced their own batch and advanced the frontiers with `max`, so ts 15 was synced and acked while the batch holding ts 14 was unwritten, and the crash lost both.
  - Mutant **ACK-OVER-HOLE**: the frontiers advance by `max` over completed batches. Test `t_ack_prefix` (A-6c): batch 2 is written and synced before batch 1 is written, then a crash; batch 2's `on` commit must not have been acked.
- **Apply waits for the append.** A commit's apply starts only after its records are appended.

**I-WAL-TS-ORDER.** Across `Commit` and `CommitMarker` records, `commit_ts` strictly increases with LSN. The commit path must assign the ts and the append position **in one critical section**. S-2's sequencer must comply; this file imposes the requirement because recovery depends on it. Without it, a lost record could leave a durable later-ts commit behind a missing earlier one, and the recovered state would not be a ts-prefix, which breaks E4's oracle (PLAN-v3 §4 E4). Mutant: TS-AFTER-APPEND.

### 12.5 Stale and torn records (I-WAL-STALE)
A reader walks records from a start LSN. **It stops at the first record that:**
- has a bad magic or CRC;
- has `lsn` ≠ the expected position, which catches a recycled segment's old content;
- has `len` running past the segment;
- has a boot id (§12.2) **older** than the boot id of the last record the walk accepted, or, for the walk's first record, older than the opened meta's `boot_id` (§2.2). Boot ids are compared in serial-number order: `a` is older than `b` iff `(a − b) mod 2^32 ≥ 2^31`.

A record that passes every one of these checks is accepted only if its `prev_lsn` and `prev_crc` also name its predecessor (I-WAL-CHAIN, at the end of this section), and then only if its body decodes ("Malformed records", below). A mismatch there is not a stop, and neither is a body that does not decode. Besides these checks, only a segment that the walk enters by a hop, missing or too short for a record header at its first LSN, ends the walk cleanly ("Short segments", below); a read error ends it with 58030 ("Read errors", below).

*Why the boot id, and why ordered.*
- **Stale records come from older boots.** Boot ids increase across the boots whose records one walk can reach: each boot's id is newer than the opened meta's `boot_id` and than the boot id of every record its recovery accepted (C-T0 v8 §2.5 with §14 step 8). A boot appends only from the valid end its own recovery found (step 5 truncates there, §14), which lies past every record that recovery accepted. So in the stream as it stands, boot ids never decrease in LSN order. A **stale** record is one that a later boot's truncation superseded: an older boot's record past an earlier valid end, uncovered when post-sync damage reverts the truncation that erased it (§14 step 5). Its magic, CRC and `lsn` may all be intact. It lies at or after the truncation point of a boot newer than its writer. If that newer boot appended up to the stale record's position, the records the walk accepts just before it come from that boot or a later one, so the stale record is older than the record before it, and the walk stops. If the newer boot appended nothing there, the bytes at its truncation point are the ones its own recovery found invalid (a reverted truncation restores exactly them), and the walk stops there anyway.
- **A walk may cross into newer boots.** The walk does not stop at a **newer** boot id. The opened meta need not be the newest meta written: faults on both copies of a meta can leave an older meta as the newest valid one. Then the walk legitimately runs from that meta's `wal_start_lsn` through its own boot's records, to the valid end at which the next boot's recovery truncated, and on into that boot's records, whose `lsn` values continue the stream. Stopping there would lose that boot's acked commits silently.
- **Bound.** Ordering u32 ids is sound while fewer than 2^31 checkpoints separate any two boots whose records the walk compares (68 years at one checkpoint per second).
- **Residual.** A boot none of whose records any walk reached (its first record torn) can still share its id with a later boot, if both copies of that boot's meta are lost after their sync, or a later open falls back from it or rolls it back explicitly (§2.2, §14 step 4a; C-T0 v8 §2.5). If that boot's records past the later boot's truncation point read back again, its stale record is not older than the later boot's records, and this check accepts it. That needs such a meta rollback together with post-sync WAL damage that reverts the truncation (classes MW and MP in one history). §14 step 5 makes that truncation durable before the boot appends past it (I-BOOT-DURABLE, card S-1-F6), so only post-sync damage can revert it, and a meta rollback with ordinary crashes alone does not reach this case (test `t_wal_trunc_durable`). Back-pointers detect such a record as a splice unless its own predecessor is byte-identical, at the same position, to the record the walk accepted before it, or their CRCs collide (I-WAL-CHAIN, below; §19 T4).
- **Mutants.** **BOOT-ID-STOP-SKIP**: the boot-id bullet is removed. **BOOT-ID-EQ-STOP**: the walk stops at any boot id other than the opened meta's.
- **Tests.**
  - `t_stale_boot_record` (A-6b, A-7d; §15.1): the step-5 truncation write is reverted after its sync, leaving an older boot's complete record at the next expected position after the newer boot's records. The walk must stop there. It fails under BOOT-ID-STOP-SKIP.
  - `t_meta_rollback_crosses_boot` (A-7d, class MP):
    - boot `b−1` writes meta `k` and crashes;
    - boot `b` recovers, truncates at its valid end `p_b`, writes its boot meta `k+1`, then appends and acks `synchronous_commit=on` commits from `p_b`;
    - a post-sync LOST write on both copies of meta `k+1` reverts it, and a crash follows before boot `b`'s next checkpoint;
    - the fixture keeps meta `k`'s WAL span out of recycling (`p_b` lies in the segment holding meta `k`'s `wal_start_lsn`), and keeps boot `b`'s page writes off image `k`'s slots;
    - the open selects meta `k` and must replay boot `b`'s acked commits; the recovered state is at or above the max acked commit.

    It fails under BOOT-ID-EQ-STOP, which stops at `p_b`, passes I-WAL-COVER and returns `Ok` without them.

After `SegmentEnd`, **or after an accepted record that ends exactly at its segment's end** (§12.2), the reader moves on to the next segment's first LSN, **and the valid end is then at least that LSN, even when that segment is short or missing**. The **valid end** is the LSN at which the walk stopped: the start of the first record it did not accept, or, after an accepted `SegmentEnd` or an accepted record that ends exactly at its segment's end, the next segment's first LSN, if the walk stops there. Recovery truncates there, and new appends overwrite from it. I-WAL-COVER compares meta `k`'s `wal_ckpt_end` against this valid end, never against an end-of-file (§14 step 3a).
- *Why the hop at an exact end.* A record that ends exactly at its segment's end leaves no room for a `SegmentEnd`. Without the hop the walk would stop there, step 5 would truncate the whole next segment, and the acked commits in it would be lost with no media fault, an I-REDO-COMPLETE break in class C (E4: 1064 r1, 4770 r2, 5054 r1 and 6263 r2). A flip after them would also give a false I-WAL-COVER XX001. The next segment's first record is still checked by `lsn` and boot-id order, as after `SegmentEnd`.
- Mutants: **SEG-EXACT-NO-HOP**: the reader does not hop after a record that ends exactly at its segment's end. **SEG-TAIL-SHORT**: the writer leaves a tail that is non-empty but shorter than a `SegmentEnd`.
- Test `t_wal_exact_segment_end` (A-6b, A-7d, class C; §15.1): a record sized to end exactly at its segment's end, then acked `on` commits in the next segment, then a power cut. Recovery replays them; under SEG-EXACT-NO-HOP, step 5 zeroes the next segment and acked commits are lost. Variant: a flip after them; under the mutant it gives a false I-WAL-COVER XX001. Variant: a tail one byte shorter than `SegmentEnd`, under SEG-TAIL-SHORT.

**The start segment (I-WAL-START).** The segment that holds the opened meta's `wal_start_lsn` existed, durably, before that meta was written (§2.3, §13 step 1), and it leaves `wal/` only by recycling, which runs lowest first (§12.1) and only below a later meta's start (§12.6), or by §14 step 5's removal of segment files whose first LSN lies above the valid end (I-BOOT-DURABLE). That removal never takes the start segment of the meta the open recovers from, whose `wal_start_lsn` is at or below the valid end, nor that of its boot meta, which starts at the valid end (§12.6); a newer meta whose start lies above the valid end is one that open retired (§14 step 4a) or could not read (§2.2). So a walk whose start segment is missing has detected damage, never a valid end: the segment was recycled under newer metas that are lost, or it was lost itself, or step 5 removed it under a meta the truncating open could not read (§14 step 3). Mutant **START-SEG-AS-END**: a missing start segment ends the walk cleanly at `wal_start_lsn`. Under it, after both copies of every newer meta are lost, an open of meta 0 over a recycled segment 0 opens an empty database, and at a quiescent flip, where `wal_start_lsn = wal_ckpt_end` and `T = ts_ckpt_hi`, the coverage check passes with every commit after the flip gone (E4's second silent-loss path, `header-design-verify.md` §1.4). Mutant **SEG0-LAZY**: segment 0 is made at the first append, not at file creation. The start segment's preallocated length was durable before the meta was written too (§2.3, §13 step 1), and no correct build shortens a segment file ("Short segments", below), so a walk that needs a byte past the start segment's end-of-file has detected damage as well, never a valid end: a read-write open fails with XX001 before pass 2 and writes nothing, and a read-only open serves salvage cut where the walk stopped (§14 step 3a). Mutant **START-SEG-SHORT-AS-END**: a read past the start segment's end-of-file ends the walk cleanly, as a short segment entered by a hop does. Under it, a start segment whose length a device lost after its sync ends the walk where its file now ends: at a quiescent flip the coverage check passes, the open recovers none of the commits past that point, and step 5 zeroes or removes every later segment, intact or not. Mutant **RECYCLE-TARGET-LOW**: step 6 renames a segment to the lowest first LSN that no segment file holds, which can lie below the append position (§12.1). After two runs, the start segment of a meta that an open selects once both copies of the two newer metas are lost can be present again, holding another segment's old records; the first of them fails the `lsn` check, the walk stops cleanly at `wal_start_lsn`, and at a quiescent flip the open returns `Ok` with every commit after the flip gone.
- Test `t_wal_start` (A-6b, A-7c, A-7d; §15.1). At A-6b: the WAL's creation leaves segment 0 present at every power cut after it returns (SEG0-LAZY fails it); recycling `n ≥ 3` segments under a SimEnv that loses unsynced renames independently, with a power cut at every step, never leaves a segment renamed while a lower one is present (RECYCLE-UNORDERED, §13 step 6, fails it); a walk from a start whose segment is missing reports it, never a valid end (START-SEG-AS-END fails it); over two recycling runs, every rename's target lies above every segment present and above the append position's segment (RECYCLE-TARGET-LOW fails it); and a walk from a start whose segment ends before a byte the walk needs, at the start itself and after records the walk accepted there, reports damage at that position, never a valid end (START-SEG-SHORT-AS-END fails it). At A-7c: a flip with the append position at a segment's first LSN and nothing past it, the segment there not yet made, and in a variant made by a recycling rename that is not yet durable, then a power cut after step 5, leaves that segment present (START-SEG-NOT-ENSURED, §13 step 1, fails it). At A-7d, in full:
  - (a) on a fresh file, checkpoints 1 and 2 with commits between them, so that step 6 of checkpoint 2 recycles segment 0, then a post-sync LOST on both copies of metas 1 and 2 and a crash. The open selects meta 0; the read-write open fails with XX001 before the walk and leaves every file byte-identical, and the read-only open serves image 0 at `T`, reporting the missing segment. START-SEG-AS-END fails it, opening an empty database;
  - (b) the same with a nonzero start: both copies of metas `k` and `k+1` lost after step 6 of checkpoint `k+1` recycled meta `k−1`'s start segment;
  - (c) as (b), with step 6's renames cut by a power cut that keeps a higher rename and loses a lower one: the open refuses, or recovers every acked commit. RECYCLE-UNORDERED fails it, opening with acked commits missing;
  - (d) faultless: a crash at every step after file creation returns, of the first appends and of A-7c's flip. Every open succeeds (a crash inside creation, before meta 0 is durable, leaves no valid meta copy: XX001, §2.2); SEG0-LAZY and START-SEG-NOT-ENSURED fail it with XX001 on a healthy store;
  - (e) a quiescent flip of checkpoint `k` (`wal_start_lsn = wal_ckpt_end`, `T = ts_ckpt_hi`), then acked commits that fill the rest of its start segment and run on into the next segment, then the start segment's file cut short by the test inside those commits (a length lost after its sync, forged through `Env`), and a crash. The read-write open fails with XX001 before pass 2 and leaves every file byte-identical; the read-only open serves salvage cut where the walk stopped, with every acked commit below the cut, and, in a run whose cut lies at `wal_start_lsn`, image `k` at `T`. START-SEG-SHORT-AS-END fails it: the read-write open returns `Ok` without the commits past the cut, and its step 5 zeroes or removes the next segment;
  - (f) on a fresh file whose first segments lie at first LSNs 0, `s` and `2s` (`s` = `wal_seg_size`), checkpoints 1, 2 and 3: meta 1's flip quiescent, with its start in segment `s`, and meta 2's start at or above `3s`, so that step 6 of checkpoint 2 recycles segment 0 and step 6 of checkpoint 3 recycles segments `s` and `2s`; then a post-sync LOST on both copies of metas 2 and 3, and a crash that follows checkpoint 3's step 6 with no write between, so that no slot of image 1, which that step 6 freed, has been reused (card S-LAMBDA). The open selects meta 1, whose start segment was recycled: the read-write open fails with XX001 (start segment missing) and leaves every file byte-identical. RECYCLE-TARGET-LOW fails it: its second run renames segment `s` to segment 0's freed name and segment `2s` to `s`'s, so the walk stops at meta 1's `wal_start_lsn` on the stale bytes at that offset (a magic, CRC or `lsn` stop), and the open returns `Ok` with every commit after meta 1's flip gone.

**Short segments.** A segment that the walk enters by a hop may be missing, or hold fewer bytes than a record header at its first LSN, because its creation or preallocation was cut short (for example by 53100) before anything was written in it. The walk ends there, and that first LSN is the valid end: for a read-write and a read-only open alike it is a stop, never 58030 and never damage, since **a writer appends to a segment only after its creation or recycling rename (§12.1) and its preallocated length are durable**, not merely returned: the segment `sync_all`'d and its `wal/` directory entry synced through `Env` (or the platform's equivalent), so no record in it is acked before then and nothing acked lies past that point. Durable means made durable by the writer's own boot, or shown durable by a record that the boot's recovery walk accepted in the segment, since that record's writer wrote it only once the segment was durable: a segment present at open can be a crashed process's create or rename, kept in the page cache with no directory sync behind it, so a boot's writer makes any other segment durable itself before its first write there, even one that existed at open. **Every other read past a segment file's end-of-file is detected damage, never an end:** a read in the start segment ("The start segment", above); a read after a record that the walk accepted in the same segment, since that segment's length was durable before the record was written; and a read of a record's body after a whole header, wherever it lies, since a segment whose preallocation was cut short holds zeros, never a header with a valid magic. No correct build shortens a segment file: recycling renames it whole, §14 step 5 zeroes in place, and preallocation only extends a file. So, unless a writer broke the writer rule, such a file has lost bytes that a completed sync covered, and they, and every segment after them, may hold acked commits. A read-write open fails with **XX001** (detail: short segment, with the segment, its file's length and the position the walk had reached) before pass 2, and writes nothing; a read-only open serves salvage cut at that position (§14 step 3a, §14.1).
- *Mutants.* **SEG-ACK-BEFORE-DURABLE**: a created or renamed segment, or one present at open that this boot has not made durable, is appended to, `sync_data`'d and acked before its directory entry is durable; a power cut loses the create or rename, and recovery stops cleanly short of an acked commit. **SEG-LEN-NOT-DURABLE**: the writer makes a segment's directory entry durable but not its preallocated length (no `sync_all`). That loses no synced record, since a `sync_data` makes durable the length its data needs (E-DURABLE), but a power cut leaves the segment short behind its last synced record, where the walk reports damage, so a healthy store's read-write open fails with XX001. **SHORT-READ-AS-END**: a read past a segment file's end-of-file after a record that the walk accepted in that segment, or of a record's body, ends the walk cleanly at that record's start; then a segment whose length a device lost after its sync ends the walk early, and at or above `wal_ckpt_end` every later commit is lost without detection while step 5 zeroes or removes the segments after it. **SHORT-HOP-AS-DAMAGE**: a segment entered by a hop that is missing, or too short for a header at its first LSN, is damage, not a stop, so a crash while the next segment is created or preallocated refuses a healthy store.
- *Why a position, never the end-of-file.* The valid end, and a salvage cut, are where the walk stopped, never an end-of-file. A segment file cut short past where the walk stopped below `wal_ckpt_end`, with its end-of-file at or above `wal_ckpt_end`, must still fail the coverage check (§14 step 3a). Mutant **COVER-AT-EOF**: the coverage check compares against the end-of-file of a short segment (test: `t_wal_loss_below_ckpt_end`, short-segment variant, §15 I-WAL-COVER).
- Test `t_wal_short_read` (A-6b, A-7d; §15.1). At A-6b, on crafted segments: a segment entered by a hop that is missing, empty, or shorter than a header at its first LSN ends the walk there with a stop, and one that holds only zeros stops on its magic; a segment entered by a hop and cut short inside its second record's header, inside its second record's body, or exactly at its first record's end, and one whose first record's body runs past its end-of-file, each give damage at that record's start, never a stop. SHORT-READ-AS-END fails the damage cases, and SHORT-HOP-AS-DAMAGE the stops. At A-7d, through the open: (a) acked commits in two segments past the start segment, then the first of them cut short by the test inside its second record, past `wal_ckpt_end` (forged through `Env`), and a crash: the read-write open fails with XX001 before pass 2 and leaves every file byte-identical, and the read-only open serves salvage cut at that record, with every acked commit below it; (b) faultless: a power cut at every step of creating and preallocating a segment and of writing its first records, and a crash, a process crash in one run and a power cut in another, at every step of §14 step 5's truncation of a valid end that lies inside a segment (card S-LAMBDA): every open succeeds and recovers every acked commit. SHORT-READ-AS-END fails (a): the read-write open returns `Ok` without the commits past the cut, and its step 5 zeroes or removes the next segment; SHORT-HOP-AS-DAMAGE fails (b), with XX001 on a healthy store, and so does **TRUNC-SHORTENS-SEGMENT** (card S-LAMBDA: step 5 shortens the valid end's segment file and then extends it again, so that a process crash between the two leaves a file that ends where the next walk, having accepted records in it, needs another header, which it meets as detected damage).
- Mutant: LSN-CHECK-SKIP. With the `lsn` check removed, a recycled segment's old records, whose magic and CRC are intact, are replayed as if they were new.

**Read errors.** A read of a segment that fails with an I/O error, or an open of one that fails other than by the file's absence, is neither a stop nor damage: the bytes it could not read may hold acked records, and a later open may read them. The walk ends with that error and no valid end. A read-write open fails with **58030**, and so does an explicit rollback, and a fall-back whose pass 1 from meta `k−1` meets one (§14 steps 3 and 4a). It writes nothing more than the open had written when the read failed: nothing, when pass 1 from meta `k` meets it; otherwise at most the fence (§14 step 1), pass 2's evictions and, once a fall-back or an explicit rollback has retired meta `k`, the retire write (§14 step 4a). A read-only open whose pass 1 meets one, from meta `k` or from meta `k−1` in a fall-back, serves salvage instead (card S-IOTA; the owner's decision of 2026-11-20, PLAN-v3 §10 item 22): cut at the **read cut**, the start of the first record whose bytes the walk could not read (where the open of a segment failed, the position at which the walk was to read from it), it serves the ts-prefix at the frontier of the records the walk accepted below the cut, writes nothing, and reports the read error and the cut, and it is never reported as a complete open, whatever the coverage check finds against the cut, since the bytes the walk could not read may hold acked commits (§14 step 3, §14.1). Pass 2 reads again the span that pass 1 read, and a read error that only pass 2 meets fails the open with 58030 in both modes, a read-only open's included: pass 1 read those bytes, so the error may not recur, and if it persists the next open's pass 1 meets it, where a read-only open serves the salvage above. An open that later reads those bytes recovers as if the error had not happened. Mutant **WAL-READ-ERROR-AS-END**: a read error ends the walk as a stop where it was, so a read-write open truncates there (§14 step 5), and a read error that a later read would not meet costs every acked commit behind it, without detection. Mutant **WAL-READ-ERROR-RO-REFUSES** (card S-IOTA): a read-only open fails with 58030 on a read error in pass 1's walk, as at draft 7.19. Mutant **WAL-READ-ERROR-RO-AS-END** (card S-IOTA): a read-only open takes a read error in pass 1's walk for its valid end, so it serves the state below the read cut but reports neither the read error nor a cut, as an open that recovered every acked commit would. Mutant **PASS2-READ-ERROR-AS-END** (card S-IOTA; S-ETA-F6): pass 2 takes a read error that pass 1 did not meet for the end of its walk, so a read-write open succeeds without the commits behind the read, which its boot checkpoint then leaves below its `wal_start_lsn` for good, and a read-only open serves what pass 2 had applied. Mutant **READ-SALVAGE-ABOVE-FRONTIER** (card S-LAMBDA): the read-error salvage alone admits snapshots above its frontier, as SALVAGE-ABOVE-FRONTIER does for WAL loss (§14.1), so that below `wal_ckpt_end` it serves part of a commit whose entries image `k` holds past the read cut.
- Test `t_wal_read_error` (A-6b, A-7d; §15.1). At A-6b, through an `Env` whose reads of one segment fail (a hook): a failing read of a record's header, a failing read of its body, and a failing open of the segment that a hop enters each end the walk with that error, after the records before it, never with a stop or damage, and the walk writes nothing. At A-7d, acked commits after meta `k`, and a hook that fails every read of one synced record past meta `k`'s `wal_ckpt_end`: (a) transient, at the first open only: the read-write open fails with 58030 and leaves every file byte-identical, and the next open recovers every acked commit; (b) persistent: the read-write open and an explicit rollback naming meta `k` each fail with 58030 and leave every file byte-identical, and the read-only open serves salvage at the read cut, the unreadable record's start, with every acked commit below it and nothing at or above it, reports the read error and the cut, and leaves every file byte-identical; (c) in a fall-back: image `k` fails at open in step 2 (`t_fallback`'s proven fixture with its fault on a page that step 2 reads: a MapLeaf, a FreeSet page or a directory page; the hooked record lies past meta `k`'s `wal_ckpt_end`, so pass 1 from meta `k` would meet it before pass 2 could fail image `k`) and the read fails in pass 1 from meta `k−1`: the read-write open fails with 58030, writes nothing more than the fence and the pass-2 evictions made before the fall-back, and retires nothing, and the next open, without the hook, falls back with proof and recovers every acked commit; the read-only open serves salvage from meta `k−1` at the read cut, reports the read error, the cut and meta `k`'s `wal_ckpt_end` and `ts_ckpt_hi` (§14 step 4a item 5), and leaves every file byte-identical; (d) pass 2 alone (card S-IOTA; S-ETA-F6): a hook at the end of pass 1 arms the fault, so that pass 1 reads the record and every later read of it fails, with a pool small enough that pass 2 evicts before it reaches the record: the read-write open fails with 58030, every meta place and WAL file is byte-identical, and every slot it wrote is one that neither image `k` nor image `k−1` references; the read-only open, with a pool that holds pass 2's replay (§14.1), fails with 58030 and leaves every file byte-identical; and the next open, without the hook, recovers every acked commit. In (d)'s fall-back form, image `k` fails at open as in (c), and the hook arms at the end of pass 1 from meta `k−1`, so that pass 2's read fails after the retire write: the read-write open fails with 58030, and the next open selects meta `k−1` and recovers every acked commit. (e) below `wal_ckpt_end` (card S-LAMBDA): as (b), but the hooked record lies below meta `k`'s `wal_ckpt_end`, at or before the record of a commit `c` that was applied before the flip and whose entries image `k` holds: the read-write open fails with 58030 and leaves every file byte-identical, and the read-only open serves salvage at the read cut, reports the read error and the cut, and at every snapshot it admits reads the model's state at or below its frontier, never an entry of `c`. WAL-READ-ERROR-AS-END fails (a) to (c): the read-write open returns `Ok`, and the next open lacks the acked commits behind the read. WAL-READ-ERROR-RO-REFUSES fails (b) and (c): the read-only open fails with 58030. WAL-READ-ERROR-RO-AS-END fails (b): the read-only open reports neither the read error nor a cut. PASS2-READ-ERROR-AS-END fails (d) and its fall-back form: the read-write open returns `Ok`, and the next open lacks the acked commit behind the read, or the read-only open serves a state. READ-SALVAGE-ABOVE-FRONTIER fails (e): a read at a snapshot at or above `c` sees `c`'s entries from image `k`.
